# calu: implementation plan

Status: phases 1-4 and 7 done (2026-11-09; see §12 and `HANDOFF.md`). This file holds the decisions,
the architecture and the order of work, derived from the owner's requirements in
[REQUIREMENTS.md](REQUIREMENTS.md). Decisions carry a date; open questions are listed in §11.

## 1. Goal

calu runs Claude Code sessions **unsupervised for long stretches** (e.g. overnight) on Linux
machines, under the control of LLM **checker agents** and humans:

- Claude Code works in its **auto mode**. Every tool call is additionally checked by calu's
  checkers and rules; a call runs only if both approve (§2, D5).
- Checkers can allow, deny or **escalate** a call. An escalation pauses the session until a human
  resolves it, from any connected client or directly in the database.
- calu runs as a background process (e.g. in tmux), independent of any IDE. Several user
  interfaces can attach to the same session at the same time.
- All sessions report to one **PostgreSQL database**. The database holds the configuration, the
  checker definitions, the full step history and the open escalations, so that one person can
  watch and unblock many sessions ("herd management").
- Nothing that steers or bypasses the checkers is within reach of Claude Code or of the commands
  it runs.

calu is the successor of stay-in-the-loop (sitl), which runs inside VSCode for one user. calu reuses sitl's core (§9). Whether sitl continues
alongside calu is decided later (§11).

## 2. Decisions

| # | Decision (date) |
|---|---|
| D1 | **Python 3.14, managed with uv** (2026-11-09). First deliverable: a package installable with `uv tool install`, providing one `calu` command. A single-file binary (PyApp or PyInstaller) was considered and dropped (D41); a port to Go or Rust stays possible once calu works. |
| D2 | **Clients connect over WebSocket with JSON-RPC** (2026-11-09), the front-end-neutral protocol of sitl extended for many clients (§6). |
| D3 | **PostgreSQL is the central store** (2026-11-09): configuration, security settings, checker definitions, session and step history, escalations. No token-level streaming goes into the database. |
| D4 | **The sandbox boundary encloses the tool calls, not Claude Code** (2026-11-09). calu and the Claude Code process form the trusted zone; every command Claude runs is executed in a sandbox that cannot reach or modify the trusted zone. The mechanism is open (§4.4). |
| D5 | **Checkers AND Claude Code's auto mode** (2026-11-09, verified in sitl's `spikes/spike_auto_mode.py`; revised 2026-11-09 on the owner's instruction): running in the auto mode is the intended way to use calu, because a step then needs the checkers **and** the auto mode classifier, but calu does **not** set the permission mode. Claude Code's own default is the auto mode, so choosing another mode is a deliberate act of the user, in Claude Code's settings or in `claude.permission_mode` (None by default = leave it alone). The hook adapts to whatever mode is in effect: it reads the mode from its own input, answers a PASS with no decision while that mode is "auto" (so the classifier decides as well), and otherwise answers "allow", so the checkers decide alone. DENY answers "deny", ESCALATE answers "ask" and goes to calu's `can_use_tool`. |
| D6 | **All configuration and security settings live in the database** (2026-11-09). Securing the database and the calu process secures everything that could let an agent bypass the checkers. Nothing that steers the checkers is read from files the agent can write. |
| D7 | **Linux only** (2026-11-09): the machine where Claude Code and calu run. Clients may run anywhere. |
| D8 | **Password-protected start, credentials dropped before Claude starts** (2026-11-09, details in §5). |
| D9 | Claude Code authenticates as it is set up on the machine (claude.ai login, AWS Bedrock, Vertex, ...); calu does no billing check (taken over from sitl). |
| D10 | **sitl code is ported into calu** (2026-11-09) and published under calu's GPL-2.0, with its unit tests. sitl's condensed third-party documentation (`.claude/skills/`) is not copied; calu's documents link to the original sources instead. |
| D11 | **sitl's coding rules apply to calu** (2026-11-09): Python first, contract docstrings in Google style enforced by ruff (`D`, `DOC`), plain technical language. They are the owner's rules from sitl's `CLAUDE.md` and are listed in `AGENTS.md`. |
| D12 | **Credential store** (2026-11-09, answers the first half of Q3): the database credentials (connection URLs) are kept encrypted in `~/.config/calu/credentials.json` (mode 0600). Each entry is encrypted with AES-256-GCM under a key derived from a password with Argon2id. Entries: `admin` (database owner, for `db init/migrate`, `setup`, configuration changes) and `session` (role `calu_session`, for `calu run`). The sandbox (D4) hides `~/.config/calu`. A per-session database role stays open (Q3). |
| D13 | **Database access with psycopg 3 and plain SQL migrations** (2026-11-09): no ORM. Migrations are numbered SQL files in the package (`calu/db/migrations/`), applied in order and recorded in `schema_migrations`. psycopg's async connections support `LISTEN/NOTIFY` directly. |
| D14 | **`calu db up` creates the local database owner** (2026-11-09): on the first start it generates the PostgreSQL owner password, stores it as the `admin` credential under an admin password the user chooses, and starts the container with it. The password is never written in plain text. |
| D15 | **Isolation of tool calls: Claude Code's own sandbox, configured by calu** (2026-11-09, answers Q1 on the evidence of `spikes/RESULTS.md`, CLI 2.1.319): calu passes the sandbox settings through `--settings` (`enabled`, `failIfUnavailable: true`, `allowUnsandboxedCommands: false`, `filesystem.denyRead` for secrets including `~/.config/calu`, Claude's credentials, `~/.ssh`, cloud credentials and the Docker socket, `allowedDomains` per project) and loads only the setting sources it chooses. Sandboxed commands get their own PID namespace (no access to the Claude Code process), cannot write `~/.claude`, `.claude/settings*.json` or `.git` hooks/config, and leftover processes die. Requires `bwrap` and `socat`. Fallback: `CLAUDE_CODE_SHELL_PREFIX` with a bubblewrap wrapper (verified to route every Bash command, including background ones). Edit/Write/Read still run in the Claude Code process and stay covered by the hook and the deny lists. |
| D16 | **Checkers on the Anthropic subscription through `claude -p --output-format json --json-schema`** (2026-11-09, answers Q7): schema-valid `structured_output` in 6 of 6 runs, 2.5-4.4 s per check with haiku; run with `--tools ""`, `--no-session-persistence` and stdin closed. |
| D17 | **Checker API keys stay out of the database** (2026-11-09): calu reads them from its environment and `~/.config/calu/.env` and hides them from Claude Code (as in sitl). |
| D18 | Replaced by D28 (2026-11-09). Originally: **a decision that reaches the session from a client is recorded as a cancellation with a note**. Only `calu_operator` may call `calu_resolve_escalation`; the session role may not, so that a session which somehow lost its credential cannot approve its own steps (§4.1 point 5). A client attached to the session process therefore decides in the session, which then cancels the row with a note that holds the decision and the client. The audit trail of such a decision is weaker than that of an operator's resolution; whether clients should instead resolve escalations with their own operator credentials is Q8. |
| D24 | **Third-party user interfaces are served through ACP, not through a plugin per editor** (2026-11-09, §6.1): a `calu acp` adapter speaks the Agent Client Protocol on stdio, is spawned by the editor and attaches to a running calu session over calu's own protocol. One adapter reaches JetBrains Air, Zed, Neovim, Goose and every other ACP client, and ACP's baseline `session/request_permission` is exactly calu's escalation. ACP is the floor: calu's own WebSocket protocol stays for what ACP cannot carry (the checker verdicts as structure, the herd overview, editing the rules). The interactive-plugin mode of §4.5 is **not** built (Q10 answered: no). |
| D23 | **The tool gate stays an in-process hook of the Agent SDK** (2026-11-09, verified in `spikes/RESULTS.md`, "Hook timeouts"): a hook that does not answer within its timeout stops the step there — the call is not run even with `permission_mode: "bypassPermissions"`, and Claude retries it once and then reports it. A shell hook of a plugin does the opposite (its timeout discards the output and the call continues through the normal permission flow), which is why the gate does not move into a plugin (§4.5). The PreToolUse timeout is 900 s, just above the checker deadline of 840 s, because no human wait happens in that hook; the two hooks that review a subagent's result may wait for a human and keep 24 hours. |
| D21 | **Four decisions: PASS, ALLOW, DENY, ESCALATE** (2026-11-09, the owner's wording). PASS is the normal answer: the checker has no objection and Claude Code's auto mode decides as before (the hook returns no decision). ESCALATE asks a human. ALLOW and DENY are deliberate exceptions: ALLOW lets the step run whatever auto mode would say (the hook answers "allow"), DENY stops it. Without auto mode (a model that has none, or a user who chose another mode), PASS has the same effect as ALLOW, because then the checkers decide alone. The allow lists of the rules produce PASS, not ALLOW: they say "no check needed", not "run this whatever the harness thinks". This replaces the three-valued decision of sitl, where ALLOW already meant "no decision" under auto mode (D5). |
| D22 | **Checkers may read files** (2026-11-09): a checker agent gets read-only tools for the files of the workspace, so it can find out what the code actually does instead of guessing or escalating. It gets nothing else: no writes, no commands, no network. |
| D29 | **The database identifies the process and the agent tree** (2026-11-09, the owner's requirement): many calu processes wrap many Claude sessions, each with its own subagents, so a row has to say where the process is and who is whose parent. `sessions` carries the host, the OS user, the project directory, the **pid** and now the **address** the process listens on ("ws://host:port" or a Unix socket path; migration 0008), next to Claude's own session id. The `agents` table holds one row per subagent of a session with `parent_agent_id` (NULL = the session's main agent, as a NULL `steps.agent_id` already means), its `sdk_agent_id`, description, type, background flag and status. The session fills it from the Agent tool call that starts a subagent (the call's id is the subagent's id, its caller is the parent) and from the runner's `agents.update` events. |
| D30 | **Every escalation can be decided from the database alone; the session's WebSocket is the convenient path, not the only one** (2026-11-09, corrected 2026-11-10 on the owner's instruction). The invariant: an operator who can reach the database can see why a step was escalated and decide it **without connecting to the session process** — which may run on another host, be unreachable, or have ended. `escalations.reason` and `escalations.context` therefore hold what a decision needs: the rendered step, the tool input (the command line, paths relative to the workspace), the file an edit would change, the changeset of a commit, the questions of an `AskUserQuestion` call with their options (D35), the conversation as the checkers see it (narrated, D3) and the folded summary. `calu escalations` lists them, `calu escalation <id>` prints all of it, `calu resolve <id> allow|deny|answer` decides, and the waiting session notices through the `escalation_resolved` notification **and** a poll of the row every second, so a lost notification only costs a second (D27's wait uses both). What the database does **not** hold is the live stream — token-by-token text, thinking, tool results as they arrive (D3); that is what attaching to the session is for. A client that *is* attached decides through the session instead, and the session records it for the client (D28), because a client holds no database login. A direct database login also covers what no single session can answer — the sessions and escalations of other hosts, and every **write** to the configuration, the rules and the checker instructions (§6.2). A client needs the session's address (D29) and a client token, and since migration 0009 a session accepts **every non-revoked token of its own session**, not only the one it printed: `calu token mint SESSION` creates one, `calu token list` shows them without the tokens themselves, `calu token revoke ID` ends one. So an editor, a local command or a remote interface keeps a token in its own configuration and survives a restart of the session. The session role may not read `client_tokens.token_sha256` (D28), so it presents the hash of the token a client sent and `calu_client_token_ok` answers whether it belongs to that session and is not revoked, stamping `last_used_at`; the role can therefore confirm a hash it was given but not enumerate the tokens, and it no longer needs UPDATE on the table, so it cannot un-revoke one. The printed token is checked first and without the database, so attaching still works while the database is unreachable. |
| D28 | **A session records the decisions of its own clients** (2026-11-09, the owner's answer to Q8, replaces D18): a client of a session process — `calu attach`, the ACP adapter, an IDE panel — has no database login; it authenticates to the session with a client token, and the session writes its decision to the database. `calu_client_decision` (migration 0007) resolves only an open escalation **of that session**, requires the SHA-256 of one of its client tokens, and records `resolved_by = 'client:<name>'`. The token hash is a capability the session process holds in memory: the SELECT privilege of `calu_session` on `client_tokens` is narrowed to the id, so the database credential alone cannot forge a decision. `calu_resolve_escalation` (any escalation, `resolved_by` = the operator) stays operator-only and is what a herd interface with its own login uses. What the agent still cannot do (§4.1 point 5): approve a step by reaching the database, because it has neither the credential nor the token — and an agent that owns the calu process could answer in-process anyway, which no database rule can prevent. Narrowing this further (one database role per session, row-level security) is Q3. |
| D27 | **An escalation nobody answers stops the session, which stays resumable** (2026-11-09, the owner's decision on Q6): `escalation_timeout_s` (default 8 h, 0 waits without a limit) bounds the wait. On the timeout the step is denied, the escalation row is cancelled with the reason, and the session stops, so the machine and the Claude session are free again. Claude Code's own session id is therefore written to the `sessions` row **as soon as Claude Code reports it** (at the start, not only after the first turn), so that `calu run --resume <id>` takes the conversation up again and `session.history` finds it; `calu sessions` prints it. |
| D26 | **A changeset checker reviews the whole change before a commit** (2026-11-09, the owner's wording): a step checker judges one edit with limited context, which is the right altitude for style, documentation and local design and the wrong one for "is this change correct and complete?". The commit is the moment the change exists, so a Bash call that creates a `git commit` is routed to `Route.CHANGESET` instead of the command checker (the deny lists still run first). calu collects the diff itself — `git diff --cached`, or against HEAD for `-a` and against `HEAD~1` for `--amend` — and gives it to the changeset checker next to the commit command and its message. A DENY undoes nothing: it stops the commit and tells Claude what to fix first. The checker type is `changeset` (route `changeset`, migration 0006); a database without it, or an administrator who disables it, leaves commits to the command checker. Verified with the real CLI: a PASS let a commit through and recorded a `checker_runs` row, a DENY stopped it and Claude reported the reason without retrying. |
| D25 | **The checkers' tools stand on Pydantic AI's workspace API** (2026-11-09): the checker agents get the `LocalWorkspace` capability with `read_only=True` and `authority="none"`, so the library pins the run to the project directory, refuses writes and commands, and marks the directory's files as carrying no authority (the agent under review writes them). calu writes only the tool functions that the library has no opinion about — `read_file(path, start_line, end_line)`, `find_files(pattern)`, `grep_files(pattern, path_glob)` in `calu.checkers.tools` — and adds what the library does not know: the read deny lists, bounds on every answer, and the framing that a file's content is data. Pydantic AI 2.66 ships no file tools of its own (`common_tools` are network searches, `native_tools.FileSearchTool` is provider-hosted), so there is nothing to reuse there; a language server belongs in the same toolset, through `pydantic_ai.capabilities.MCP` or as further functions. |
| D20 | **One token per session for its clients** (2026-11-09): `calu run` issues a client token, prints it once and stores only its SHA-256 (`client_tokens`). A client sends it in `initialize`; every other request is refused before that. The endpoint binds 127.0.0.1 by default, so remote access goes through an SSH tunnel or a TLS proxy until Q4 is answered. `--socket [PATH]` serves a **Unix socket** instead (mode 0600, under `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` by default): the token still applies, and the file system becomes a second layer, since a loopback port is reachable by every process of every user on the machine while a 0600 socket is not. A path longer than about 100 bytes is refused with its length, because the kernel's limit is 108 and `AF_UNIX path too long` explains nothing. |
| D19 | **One database connection per session, serialized and shielded** (2026-11-09): `calu run` opens two connections before it wipes the credentials (one for queries, one for notifications) and cannot open more later, because reconnecting would need the password again (§5). psycopg allows one statement at a time per connection, and a session writes from several tasks at once, so every statement goes through `calu.db.Db`: a lock serializes them, and each statement is shielded from cancellation (bounded by a timeout), because a task cancelled in the middle of a statement leaves the connection unusable for every other task. |
| D127 | **The escalation queue says what each step is** (2026-11-10, found reading the reports of a seeded database): `calu escalations` listed id, session, host, age and reason, and not the step itself -- "the deny list matched" for every entry, with no way to tell a `sudo` from a `git push` without opening each one. The row already carried the rendered step and never showed it; the queries now also return the command, the edited path or the tool's name from the escalation's context, and the line shows it. This is the queue the README sells as "answer from the database alone", so it has to be workable from the listing. |
| D138 | **A settings page you can find your way around** (2026-11-11, the owner's request to look at every tab with a headless browser): the page was one 9,400-pixel scroll of 64 settings, each with its full description, and no way to see what had been changed. It now has a sticky index of the sections with the number of changed values in each, a filter over name, key and description, a "changed only" switch, a `changed · default ...` badge beside every value that differs from its default, descriptions folded after their first sentence, units in the labels ("Escalation timeout (s)"), and a save bar that appears with the number of unsaved edits, marks the edited rows and offers Discard -- plus a warning before leaving with unsaved edits. Two faults found on the way: a `<details>` inside a `<p>` is invalid HTML, so the browser closed the paragraph and put "more" on a line of its own; and splitting a description at a colon cut a code example in half. A browser test drives the filter, the changed-only view, the save bar and Discard. |
| D139 | **The other pages, looked at on a phone and in dark mode** (2026-11-11, the same tour): the session page listed its connect commands in a table whose command column a phone squeezed to one character per line; each command is now a row with its label above it, the command in a block that scrolls sideways, and a Copy button. The fleet table turns into one card per session below 720 pixels, each cell labelled. The checkers page opened with three paragraphs on how to read its numbers; they are folded under "How to read the numbers". |
| D140 | **The live view of a running session, looked at** (2026-11-11, the rest of the tour, which had only seen the live page of a finished session): every checker verdict in the stream was orange, so an approval looked like a warning, and every tool call was its input as JSON (`Bash {"command":"..."}`). A verdict is now a badge with the colours the other pages use for decisions, and a tool call is a badge with the tool's name followed by the part of its input a person reads first (the command, the path, the pattern, the URL); other inputs stay shortened JSON. Which field that is, is display only, so it lives in the page script and not in the protocol. The stream already scrolls in its own box, so the message box below it stays in reach and was left as it is. |
| D141 | **A mark says when it reaches beyond its step** (2026-11-11, the half of Q20 (c) that restricts nothing): a checker may mark any existing project file (D112), and Q20 asks the owner whether it should be limited to the step's own files. Building the visibility does not pre-empt that answer, so it is built: migration 0023 adds `honesty_marks.beyond_step`, set when the mark is written -- true when the step named its files and the mark's path is not among them, false when it is, NULL when the step named no file (a Bash command) or the row is older. A repeated mark refreshes it with the reason, because both describe the latest verdict. `calu honesty list` prints `<not a file of its step>`, the session and rules pages tag the mark, and `HonestyMarkInfo` carries the field for clients. Enforcement is unchanged: such a mark blocks like any other until the owner decides Q20. |
| D142 | **A mark can be limited to its branch, as an option** (2026-11-11, Q19 (a) built without deciding it): `honesty.scope` is `project` (default, unchanged: a mark blocks on every branch) or `branch` (a mark made on another branch neither blocks a step nor reaches the checkers' note; `calu honesty list` still shows it, flagged stale by D91). A mark with no recorded branch applies everywhere, and so does every mark when git cannot say which branch is checked out, because guessing "another branch" there would let dirty code run. The branch is asked for only when the scope is `branch` and marks exist, through the session's cached `_git_branch`. Switching the default is the owner's decision; the option lets the owner try it on a real project first. |
| D143 | **Pushes, deploys and publishes as a rule set you add** (2026-11-11, Q16 (b) built without deciding it): `calu/defaults/rules/sets/publishing.txt` holds command-deny patterns for any `git push`, `kubectl`/`oc` and `helm` changes, `terraform`/`tofu` apply, destroy and import, `gcloud`/`aws`/`az ... deploy`, package uploads (npm, pnpm, yarn, uv, poetry, cargo, flit, hatch, twine, gem), `docker`/`podman push`, and `gh release`/`gh pr merge`. `calu rules add-set publishing [--project P]` appends them, skipping patterns the list already has -- a disabled one included, because re-adding it would undo a person's decision -- so running it twice adds nothing. After that they are ordinary rules: visible on the Rules page, removable one by one. The Rules page offers the set under the command lists with an Add button that does the same (`POST /rule-sets/{name}`). A set rather than disabled defaults, because `calu setup` seeds rules only into an empty database, so an existing install would never have received them. The default lists are unchanged; whether the set becomes a default is the owner's decision. |
| D144 | **A session says which of your own Claude Code setup it leaves out** (2026-11-11, the downside of Q21's recommendation, removed without deciding Q21): with `claude.setting_sources = ["project"]` a user's hooks, plugins, `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` and skills simply did not run, and nothing said so. `preflight.setting_source_warnings` now names what exists and is left out -- `~/.claude/settings.json` with `hooks`, `enabledPlugins` or `env`, `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`, a non-empty `~/.claude/skills/`, the project's `.claude/settings.local.json` -- as one start-up warning with the command that includes it and the reason it is off (its permission rules would let Claude Code approve steps by itself). It reads files only and skips one it cannot parse. MCP servers in `~/.claude.json` are not named, because whether `setting_sources` governs them is not established in calu's reference notes. |
| D145 | **A deny rule could be sidestepped by a prefix** (2026-11-11, found while reading the parts of the shell splitter the tests did not reach): the deny lists are matched against each simple command and are anchored (`^(sudo|su|doas)( |$)`), and the splitter records a command with its leading assignments. So `X=1 sudo ...`, `env sudo`, `command sudo`, `nohup`, `time`, `exec`, `nice`, `timeout 5`, `xargs`, `builtin eval` and `/usr/bin/sudo` all went past the list to the checker -- 17 of 22 probes, including `X=1 git push --force origin main`. The checker still judged them, but the guide promises that these always ask a person. `shell.command_variants` now strips leading assignments, the wrappers in `shell.WRAPPERS` (with their options and, for `timeout`, `chrt` and `taskset`, their positional argument) and a path before the program name, one layer at a time, and the deny list is matched against every stage. The allow list still sees only the command as written, so the extra forms can make a step wait for a person but never let one through. Tests pin 15 bypass spellings and 5 harmless wrapped commands that must stay unaffected. Still out of reach for a regex list, and left to the checker: commands built at run time (`$CMD`), `watch`/`flock`/`script -c` strings, and aliases or functions defined earlier in the same command. |
| D146 | **A Grep of a directory reads the files below it, and the read deny list now sees them** (2026-11-11, measured with the real CLI, `spikes/RESULTS.md`): the rules checked the directory a Grep names, and the default read allow list covers the project, so `Grep(pattern="API_KEY")` with no path ran with no check at all -- and Claude Code's Grep reads hidden files, so it returned the line of `.env`, of `server.key` and of a file in a hidden directory. It does respect `.gitignore`, which is why the usual project with an ignored `.env` was not exposed, but a non-ignored `.env`, `*.key` or `*.pem`, or any folder outside git, was. `calu.checkers.search.searched_files` lists what such a search reads -- `git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard` inside a work tree, every file outside one, narrowed by the `glob` parameter with one level of braces -- and a protected file among them makes the step ask, naming the files, exactly as a `Read` of one does. All output modes count: a files-only search still answers whether `.env` matches `API_KEY=a.*`, which is enough to recover a secret one character at a time. Over 200,000 files the listing stops and the step goes to the checker with a note. Recursive searches in Bash got the same treatment in D149. |
| D147 | **A glob and a file from git history reach the read deny list** (2026-11-11, the same review of the gate): (1) the splitter turned every glob into "unknown", so `cat .e*` went to the checker instead of asking, although the shell would expand it to `.env`. `resolve` now also returns what the glob matches at check time (`_Walker.expand` in `calu.checkers.shell`, Python's `glob` with the shell's default of not matching a leading dot unless the pattern does), followed by the unknown marker, because the files can change before the command runs -- a glob that reaches a protected file asks, any other glob still goes to the checker. (2) `git show REV:PATH` and `git cat-file ... REV:PATH` print a file from history and were free, because `git show` is on the allow list; the path is now recorded as a read. Still with the checker, not the rules: archive and copy tools over a directory (`tar`, `zip -r`), interpreters (`python -c`), `git log -p`, and `git grep`. |
| D148 | **Writes named by an option or done by git reach the write deny list** (2026-11-11, the write half of the same review; 21 of 26 probes already asked): `curl -o FILE`, `curl --output FILE` and `wget -O FILE` recorded FILE as a read, because only the attached forms (`--output=FILE`) were understood -- `shell.OUTPUT_OPTIONS` now names the separate-value output options of curl, wget, sort and the C compilers. `patch FILE` modifies FILE and joins `WRITE_COMMANDS`. `git config KEY VALUE` writes `.git/config` (or `~/.gitconfig`, `/etc/gitconfig`), which decides which hooks git runs (`core.hooksPath`), and `git checkout -- PATH` / `git restore PATH` overwrite PATH; both are now recorded as writes, so `.git` and `CLAUDE.md` are protected from them as from `echo >`. A `git config` that only reads (`--get`, `--list`, one argument) is not a write. Still with the checker: archive extraction (`tar x`, `unzip`), which writes files the command does not name. |
| D149 | **A recursive search in Bash is checked against the read deny list too** (2026-11-11, the open end of D146): `grep -r`, `rg` and `ag` went to the checker with "unknown files", so a checker that approved `grep -r KEY .` let it print `.env`. The splitter now describes what such a search reads (`shell.SearchSpec`): grep reads git-ignored and hidden files; rg and ag leave out what git ignores and hidden files, unless `-u`/`--no-ignore` and `-uu`/`--hidden` say otherwise; `--include=` and `-g` narrow the files. `searched_files` lists them and a protected one makes the step ask. The step still goes to the checker otherwise -- a search is never free. Found on the way: the splitter counted the working directory as searched for every recursive search, although its comment said "without a path argument"; it now does what the comment says (`_names_a_path`: the first plain argument is the pattern unless `-e` or `-f` gives it), so `grep -r KEY src` no longer asks about the root's `.env`. This is stricter than before for one common case: `grep -r` over a project with a git-ignored `.env` now asks, because grep really reads it. |
| D150 | **The honesty register follows `cd` and globs** (2026-11-11, the honesty part of the same review): in `enforce` mode a command that runs a marked file is refused, and `honesty.command_paths` decided which files a command names by resolving every word against the directory the command starts in. `cd src && python parser.py`, `(cd src; python parser.py)` and `python src/pars*.py` therefore ran a marked `src/parser.py` without naming it. The paths the shell splitter resolves -- which follows `cd` and, since D147, expands globs -- are now added to the word-by-word ones, so these are refused as well; a command that names no file (`make test`) is unchanged and stays with the checker. Only matters while `honesty.mode` is `enforce`, which is off by default. |
| D151 | **The rules run in a worker thread** (2026-11-12, the cost of D146-D149): since the rules list files, a check can take time -- measured on the stay-in-the-loop tree, `git ls-files` for a Grep takes 2-3 ms, the full walk for `grep -r` over 33,000 files about 0.8 s. The session called `Rules.evaluate` on its event loop, so for that long no client got a streamed event or a heartbeat. It now runs through `anyio.to_thread.run_sync`; `Rules` is only read during a check and a reload swaps the whole object, so nothing is shared mutably. A test makes the check sleep 0.4 s and requires the loop to keep ticking; it fails without the change. |
| D152 | **The ACP adapter checked against the published schema** (2026-11-12, preparing HANDOFF step 1 without an editor): `schema/v1/schema.json` of agentclientprotocol/agent-client-protocol, read field by field against `calu.acp`. The stop reasons, tool kinds, tool call statuses, permission option kinds and outcome shapes, the `initialize`/`session/new`/`session/load`/`session/prompt` required fields, the `session/update` discriminator and the four update kinds calu sends, the boolean config options (client capability `session.configOptions.boolean`, option `type`/`currentValue`, set request `configId`/`value`) and the category `mode` all match. One gap: the adapter handled `session/close` but did not offer it, and the schema lets a client call it only when `agentCapabilities.sessionCapabilities.close` is present; `initialize` now offers it. Newer update kinds (`usage_update`, `notice`, `session_info_update`, compaction) are optional and not sent. The schema also has a v2 directory; calu stays on v1, which is what the adapter announces. |
| D153 | **The deny-list fixes in a real session, and the Grep tool is gone** (2026-11-12, `spikes/smoke_session.py`, CLI 2.1.319, haiku): `X=1 sudo -n ls /root` (D145) and `grep -rn API_KEY .` over an un-ignored `.env` (D149) both escalated with the expected reasons from the rule check that now runs in a worker thread (D151), and the session went on after the denial. The same run showed that CLI 2.1.319 no longer has the Grep and Glob tools -- searches are Bash commands now -- so D149, not D146, is the check that protects a current session; D146 stays for older CLIs. HANDOFF "Verified by hand" item 31. |
| D154 | **A session names the tools calu has no route for** (2026-11-12, following D153): Claude Code adds tools with its own updates, and calu's routing table (`calu.checkers.policy`) only knows the ones someone looked at; everything else falls to `default_route`, the command checker. That is not unchecked, but it was silent -- and some new tools reach outside the session. The CLI's init message lists its tools, so the runner now compares them once per session with `KNOWN_TOOLS` and the configured `tool_routes` and logs a warning naming the rest (MCP tools excluded, their default route is documented) with the command that makes one always ask. Measured in a real session with CLI 2.1.319: CronCreate, CronDelete, CronList, DesignSync, EnterWorktree, ExitWorktree, ListAgents, Monitor, PushNotification, ReportFindings, ScheduleWakeup, TaskStop and Workflow. Which of them deserve a route of their own (`EnterWorktree` changes the directory Claude works in; `CronCreate` schedules work for later) is a default and left to the owner; the warning is what makes the question visible. |
| D155 | **A Claude Code worktree is judged like the project it checks out** (2026-11-12, measured with CLI 2.1.319): `EnterWorktree` -- one of the tools D154 named -- and subagents with `isolation: worktree` work in `<project>/.claude/worktrees/<name>/`, a full checkout with a `.git` file at its root. The path rules read every path relative to the project, so every file of a worktree was below `.claude/` and the write deny rule `(^|/)\.claude(/|$)` refused **every edit** in it, while the rules anchored at the root (`^CLAUDE\.md$`, `^\.git(/|$)`, `^AGENTS\.md$`) never matched the worktree's own copies. `Rules.match_form` now matches a path inside a worktree relative to the worktree's root; messages still name the real path. Only a directory with a `.git` entry counts as a worktree, so a file next to the worktrees or a directory that is not one stays below `.claude/` and stays protected. Checked against the worktree the CLI itself created: an ordinary edit passes the rules (and goes to the edit checker), the worktree's `CLAUDE.md` asks. |
| D156 | **`Monitor` runs a shell command and meets the same rules as Bash** (2026-11-12, one of the tools D154 named): CLI 2.1.319's `Monitor` takes a `command` that it runs "in the same shell environment as Bash" in the background, passing each output line to Claude. The deny lists, the honesty register and the allow-list fast path looked only at tools named `Bash`, so `Monitor(command="sudo ...")` or `Monitor(command="tail -f .env")` reached the command checker without the rules that promise to ask -- and with `default_route = allow` it would have run free. `policy.SHELL_TOOLS = {Bash, Monitor}` is now used by the rules, the honesty register, the routing (always the command checker) and the session; the checker sees the command fenced like a Bash command, with the monitor's timeout. A Monitor on a WebSocket (`ws`, no command) names nothing for the rules and goes to the checker. Verified in a real session: Claude loaded Monitor through ToolSearch and `Monitor(command="X=1 sudo -n ls /root")` escalated on the deny rule. Commit detection (the changeset checker) stays Bash-only. |
| D157 | **A Workflow's subagents are gated, and now their results are reviewed too** (2026-11-12, one of the tools D154 named): a Workflow script has no filesystem or Node access (`workflow-authoring` reference); it acts only through `agent()`, which starts subagents. Measured in a real session: such a subagent's `X=1 sudo -n ls /root` passed calu's gate and escalated on the deny rule. But calu registers subagents from Agent tool calls, so the SubagentStop of a workflow agent was "unknown" and its result went into the workflow -- and from there to the main agent -- with no review. The hook now reviews such a result like a background subagent's, describing it by its `agent_type`, with the task read from the first user line of `agent_transcript_path` (`sdk_runner.first_prompt`; checked on a real workflow transcript). Cost: one result-checker call per workflow agent, which a workflow already multiplies far beyond. |
| D158 | **An unattended run waits for Claude Code's background tasks** (2026-11-12, found while verifying D157): `calu run -p` ended at the first idle, but a turn can end while a background command, a Monitor, a Workflow or a background subagent still runs -- the run then stopped the session under them, with their steps still to be gated (the workflow's escalation arrived after the stop). Measured with a bare SDK client: when a background task finishes, Claude Code starts another turn by itself, and a `background_tasks_changed` system message lists the running tasks. The runner keeps that count, `TurnResult.background_tasks` carries it (new optional protocol field), the console says "N background task(s) still running", and `IdleSignal` treats an idle with tasks running as not finished. Verified: the workflow run waited, its escalation was resolved, its result reviewed, and the session stopped after Claude's follow-up turn. The web live view shows the same "N background task(s) still running" at the turn's end, and the fake runner can script it (`{"background_tasks": N}` ends the turn with N tasks and then starts the next scripted turn by itself), which a browser test and a whole-run test use. |
| D159 | **An editor's prompt stays open until Claude's background work is done** (2026-11-12, the ACP side of D158): the adapter answered `session/prompt` at the first `turn.result`, so the follow-up turn Claude Code starts when a background task finishes streamed `session/update`s after the prompt was answered -- and the v1 schema describes those updates as streamed "during prompt processing". The adapter now answers only at a `turn.result` with `background_tasks == 0` (or a failed turn), and tells the editor meanwhile that N background task(s) still run; `session/cancel` still ends the prompt at once. What a real editor makes of a long-open prompt is part of HANDOFF step 1. |
| D160 | **Claude may not run calu itself without asking** (2026-11-12): calu runs as the same OS user as Claude, and since D104/D105 the operator credential can be stored without a password and `calu attach` mints its own token. Without the sandbox (which makes `~/.config/calu` unreadable to Claude's commands) nothing but the checker model stood between Claude and `calu resolve 1 allow`, `calu config set honesty.mode off`, `calu rules remove ...` or a self-minted attach -- all of them went to the checker in a probe. `rules.BUILT_IN_COMMAND_DENY` now makes every invocation of calu ask: directly, by path, through `uv run`/`uv tool run`/`uvx`/`pipx run`/`poetry run`/`python -m`, and behind the wrappers of D145. Built in like the `~/.config/calu` path rules, so no rule list can drop it and existing installations get it at once; a word that merely names calu (`grep -rn calu`) is not affected. Left open and next: `calu web` has no login, so a local process -- Claude's `curl` included -- can post a decision to it (D161). |
| D161 | **`calu web` has a login: the token it prints** (2026-11-12, the open end of D160): D136 keeps other *websites* out, but any local *process* could still post to the interface -- `curl -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8800/escalations/1/resolve -d decision=allow` from Claude's Bash would decide its own step, sandbox or not, as long as `calu web` runs. `calu web` now makes a random token at every start and prints the address with it (`/?token=...`); `web.app.TokenGate` swaps a correct `?token=` for an HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict cookie with a redirect to the same URL without it, passes requests that carry the cookie, and refuses everything else with 401 or WebSocket close code 1008 -- the static files included. The token is never stored. Claude learns it only by starting `calu web` itself, which D160 makes ask. `create_app(token=None)` leaves the gate out for in-process tests; the test that starts a real `calu web` reads the printed address and checks that a request without the token gets 401. |
| D162 | **A start-up warning for the one self-approval path rules cannot close** (2026-11-12, after D160/D161): without a working sandbox, a credential stored without a password (D104) is protected only by the file mode, which does not separate Claude from the user it runs as. The rules stop `calu ...` (D160) and reads of `~/.config/calu` by Claude's tools, but not an interpreter Claude runs (`python -c "open(...)"`), which only the checker judges. `preflight.self_approval_warnings` names that combination at every start -- sandbox off, or its programs missing without `fail_if_unavailable`, together with any password-free credential -- and the two ways out: the sandbox, or a password (`calu credential set operator`). A working sandbox or no unprotected credential gives no warning. |
| D163 | **calu's database container no longer trusts connections from inside it** (2026-11-12, the next self-approval path after D160-D162): the official postgres image trusts local-socket and in-container loopback connections (measured on calu's dev container: `docker exec ... psql -U calu_owner` answered with no password). A program of the user that may use Docker -- Claude's included -- could therefore run `calu_resolve_escalation(...)` with the owner's rights, skipping every credential. The shipped compose file now creates volumes with `POSTGRES_INITDB_ARGS=--auth-local=scram-sha-256 --auth-host=scram-sha-256`, and `calu db up` converts an older volume in place (`local.harden`: `trust` -> `scram-sha-256` in pg_hba.conf, `pg_ctl reload` as the postgres user, a no-op once done). Tried on throwaway containers first: afterwards `psql` inside the container fails without the password and works with it, the healthcheck still passes, and calu's own connections, which come through the published port, already used scram. Docker access stays root-equivalent -- a script can still copy the data directory -- so this closes the one-line path, not every path; the sandbox is the real boundary: Claude Code's built-in sandbox defaults deny the Docker, containerd, podman and buildkit sockets (measured, `spikes/RESULTS.md`, Q1a). The development compose file (`docker-compose.yml`, tests only) is unchanged. |
| D164 | **`calu doctor` names the same self-approval risk as the start-up warning** (2026-11-12, D162 seen from before a session): doctor runs without a password, so it cannot read whether the sandbox is switched on, but it sees whether the sandbox can run here at all. A credential without a password on a machine without `bwrap` or `socat` is reported as a failed finding "self-approval", with the two ways out (install them, or `calu credential set operator`). Not essential: a session still starts. This is as far as calu can go while Claude and calu share an OS user without the sandbox: the remedy -- a password or the sandbox -- is the user's to apply, and both are named wherever the risk shows. |
| D165 | **DesignSync uploads and a discarded worktree meet the rules** (2026-11-12, reading the schemas of the tools D154 named): `DesignSync` reads files from disk **itself** and uploads them to claude.ai (`finalize_plan` names a `localDir`, `write_files` a `localPath` per file; "the contents never enter your context"), so `localDir="~/.ssh"` plus `localPath="id_rsa"` would have uploaded a private key past the read deny list -- the rules only looked at read, edit and shell tools. `ExitWorktree(action="remove", discard_changes=true)` deletes uncommitted work, the same loss as `git reset --hard`, which asks. `Rules._evaluate_other` now treats DesignSync's `localDir` (with every file below it) and each `localPath` as reads, and makes the discarding exit ask; both tools stay with the checker otherwise, since an upload leaves the machine. The other new tools were read the same way: `PushNotification` sends a short text to the user's own terminal or phone, `TaskStop` stops a background task, `CronCreate`/`ScheduleWakeup` queue prompts for later turns of the same session (whose steps pass the gate), `ListAgents`/`ReportFindings` report -- none reads or writes a file past the rules, so they stay with the command checker and in the D154 warning. |
| D166 | **Any tool that names a protected path asks -- MCP servers included** (2026-11-12, the general form of D156/D165): the rules looked only at the read, edit and shell tools, so an MCP filesystem server's `read_file(path="~/.ssh/id_rsa")` reached nothing but the checker, and a `write_file(path="CLAUDE.md")` likewise. For every tool without a specific rule, `Rules._evaluate_named_paths` now checks each string argument under a path-like key (`PATH_KEYS`: path, paths, file, directory, source, destination, uri, ...) or that starts like a path (`/`, `~/`, `./`, `../`, `file://`) against the read deny list, and against the write deny list too when the tool's name says it writes (`WRITE_WORDS`). Content-like arguments (`CONTENT_KEYS`: content, text, prompt, query, sql, command, ...) are never read as paths, which a test pins (a write whose *content* is `~/.ssh/id_rsa` does not ask). A match asks; nothing else changes, so the step still goes to the checker and no tool becomes free. A heuristic by nature: an MCP tool that takes a path under an unusual name and without a leading `/` or `./` is still the checker's. |
| D167 | **A path in an interpreter's inline code meets the read deny list** (2026-11-12, narrowing the gap D162 names): `python3 -c "..."`, `node -e`, `perl -e`, `ruby -e` and the like passed their code to the rules as one long argument. Deny patterns are searched within it, so `/.config/calu/` and `/.ssh/` were found by accident, but every rule anchored at the end of a path -- `.env`, `.aws/credentials`, `*.pem` -- could not match in the middle of code: `node -e "...readFileSync('.env')"` went to the checker. `rules.inline_code_paths` takes the quoted literals out of the code that follows an interpreter's code option, from the raw command (the splitter's form has lost the quotes), and a literal that looks like a path is checked against the read deny list. This closes the one-liner that names its file; code that assembles the path at run time is still the checker's, and the start-up warning of D162 stays. |
| D168 | **What an independent review of D145-D167 found, and the fixes** (2026-11-12; a separate reviewer read the diff and confirmed each finding with a snippet). **Fail-open, fixed:** (1) a Grep of a git-ignored directory was *allowed* -- `git ls-files` lists nothing inside it, but rg reads a directory it is given (`rg x secrets` printed `secrets/prod.key`); an ignored directory is now walked. (2) A Grep of the project was allowed although rg descends into a nested repository or submodule, which git lists only as one entry (calu itself is such a nested repo); such entries are now listed in their own right. **Deny rules missed, fixed:** `python -m calu.cli`, `uv run --project /x calu`, `uv run -- calu`, `uv run python -m calu`, `uvx --from calu calu` (now `rules.runs_calu`, which reads the words instead of one regex); `env -S "sudo ls"`, `script -c "sudo ls"`, `watch`, `strace`, `flock`, `sg`, `chroot`, `systemd-run` (`shell.COMMAND_OPTIONS`, more `WRAPPERS`); `curl -sSLo FILE`, `wget -qO FILE`, `git checkout REV PATH` without `--`, `git config --unset`; `python3 -Sc` and `node --eval=` (inline code, D167); MCP argument names `relative_path`/`pathname`/... and write words copy/append/update/patch/replace/insert (D166); with `honesty.scope = branch`, a command that switches branch itself (`git checkout A && python marked.py`) or a detached HEAD now gets every mark (`session.SWITCHES_BRANCH`). `cat ~root/notes` was free because `~user` was not expanded. **Robustness, fixed:** `~unknownuser`, a NUL in a path and a non-ASCII `?token=` raised (escalation or HTTP 500) -- `rules._absolute`, `web.app._same`; a listing now stops after 3 s as incomplete (`search.TIME_LIMIT_S`), since `/mnt/c` under WSL did not finish in 100 s. **Not changed, recorded:** a one-shot run or an editor prompt waits as long as a background task runs (D170 adds an opt-in bound for runs); the Grep tool's `glob` is matched with fnmatch, not ripgrep's exact rules (the Grep tool is gone in CLI 2.1.319); `docker exec -u postgres` can undo D163's hardening (Docker is root-equivalent). |
| D169 | **Applying a diff is checked against the files the diff changes** (2026-11-12, two of D168's open items): `patch -p1 < fix.diff`, `patch -i fix.diff` and `git apply fix.diff` write the files named *inside* the diff, so `CLAUDE.md` or `.git/hooks/pre-commit` changed that way never met the write lists. `rules.diff_targets` reads the diff (it exists before the command runs, at most 1 MB) and every `+++`/`---`/`diff --git`/`rename to` target is checked as a write; a diff that cannot be read leaves the step to the checker. A `git apply --check` of such a diff asks too, although it writes nothing -- the safe direction. The other open item, `grep -r x src/*`, turned out to be covered already: since D147 a glob resolves to its current matches, and the recursive check lists those (`src/conf/server.key` asked). |
| D170 | **An unattended run can bound its wait for background tasks** (2026-11-12, the open end of D158): `calu run --batch` waits as long as Claude Code's background tasks run, which is right for a build or a workflow and wrong for a dev server that never ends. `calu run --background-timeout SECONDS` (opt-in; no default changed) ends the run once the session has been idle that long with tasks still running -- the session stops, and Claude Code with its tasks; the console says so. `IdleSignal` measures the idle time from the moment a turn ended with tasks running and resets it when Claude works again. The README now describes `--batch` at all, which it did not. |
| D171 | **A container command on calu's own database asks** (2026-11-12, narrowing what D163 leaves open): Docker access is root-equivalent, so `docker exec -u postgres` can undo D163's hardening and `docker cp` can copy the data directory. A built-in rule (`rules.CALU_CONTAINER`, like D160's) now makes every `docker`/`podman`/`nerdctl` command that names calu's container, compose project or volume (`calu-db...`) ask -- `docker exec calu-db-postgres-1 ...`, `docker compose -p calu-db ...`, `docker cp`, `docker volume rm calu-db_data`. It narrows the gap and does not close it: a script that looks the container up by id first is still the checker's, and the sandbox, which denies the Docker socket (Q1a), is what closes it. |
| D172 | **What a second review, of the fixes D168-D171, found** (2026-11-12; again a separate reviewer with confirming snippets). **Worst:** (1) an uninitialised submodule made the search listing recurse until RecursionError -- git lists `./` inside it -- so every Grep or `rg` of such a project asked as an internal error; directories already being listed are now skipped, and each git call is bounded by the listing's time limit. (2) D168's calu detection counted every word named calu after a launcher, so `uv run pytest src/calu`, `uv run --project calu pytest`, `uv add calu` asked -- every test run while working on calu itself; `rules._launched` now finds the program the launcher actually starts (skipping option values) and only that counts, version suffixes (`calu@latest`) and combined `-Im` included. (3) `diff_targets` copied the rest of the command for every word: 14 s for one 150 kB heredoc; it now splits the command once (0.07 s). **Bypasses closed:** diffs with other prefixes and strip levels (`orig/CLAUDE.md` under `-p1` -- every suffix is now checked), `-d DIR`, `-iFILE`, `cat x.diff | patch`, `git -C DIR apply`, `git am`; `watch -d` (it takes no separate value); command options after a wrapper's positional (`flock F -c`, `sg G -c`, `script log -c`) and attached (`env -S'...'`); `git checkout FILE` with one argument that exists as a file; inline code joined to its flag (`python -c'...'`); `docker-compose`/`podman-compose` and `-f .../local-compose.yml`; git global options before `checkout`/`switch` for `honesty.scope = branch`. **False positives removed:** `git config get|list` (git 2.46 syntax), `git config -f FILE` (writes FILE, not `.git/config`), `gcc -flto` (combined output letters only for curl and wget); a `---` line counts as a diff header only when `+++` follows. `calu run --background-timeout` now refuses to run without `--batch` or with a value of 0 or less. |
| D173 | **What a third review, of D172, found -- and the lesson** (2026-11-12). D172's fixes broke things D168 had fixed and opened one fail-open: (1) skipping git's `./` entry of an uninitialised submodule (the recursion fix) let a Grep read `sub/.env` with no check at all -- the directory is now walked; (2) the precise launcher parsing (`_launched`) missed `uv run -m calu.cli`, `python -X dev -m calu`, `uv --quiet tool run calu`, value options it did not know (`--with-editable`, `-i`, `-w`), uv's flag `-n`, and wrappers (`uv run env calu`) -- `runs_calu` now fails closed: after the run verb any word `calu` counts unless it is a known option's value, contains "/", or follows a known ordinary program (`ORDINARY_PROGRAMS`); (3) checking every suffix of a diff path was quadratic (6000 files: about 2 minutes) and asked for a nested `docs/CLAUDE.md` -- `diff_targets` now strips exactly what the tool strips (`patch -pN`, the base name without `-p`, `git apply -pN` default 1 with `--directory`), handles quoted names, `git apply` from a pipe or `<`, and heredoc diffs (6000 files: 0.35 s); (4) `script -qc` (combined flags), `git checkout -q FILE` and of a directory, `python3 -Ic"..."`, `git --git-dir DIR checkout`; (5) any project's `local-compose.yml` asked -- only calu's own `calu/db/local-compose.yml` does now; `--background-timeout nan` is refused. **Lesson for the rules engine:** three rounds of quick fixes each produced regressions of the round before; precise parsing of a command line is where they came from, and failing closed (ask when unsure) is what stops them. Every earlier test stays, so a regression of a fixed case fails the suite. |
| D174 | **A fourth review, of D173 -- and failing closed all the way** (2026-11-12). The review found D173's precise option parsing missing spellings again: a heredoc body taken for `git apply`'s file arguments (so heredoc diffs were never read), a `#include <vector>` in a patch heredoc read as a redirect, combined `patch -Np1`/`-sp1`, `git apply -p 2`, attached `patch -dDIR`, `-i FILE` read from the wrong directory, a tool behind a wrapper (`X=1 patch`, `env git apply`), a diff written by the same command (`cat > fix.diff <<EOF ... patch < fix.diff`), context diffs and renames. **`diff_targets` no longer parses options:** whenever `patch` or `git apply`/`am` appears anywhere in the command, the diff text is every heredoc line plus every existing file the command names; every header path (unified, context, `diff --git`, rename, copy; quoted or not) is tried at strip levels 0-3 and as its base name, against the starting directory and every `-d`/`-C`/`--directory` value. The price, accepted: a diff of a nested `docs/CLAUDE.md` asks as if it were the root one. `runs_calu` also stopped trusting its parse: a program given as a path (`.venv/bin/calu`), attached `-Wonce`/`-Xtracemalloc`, unknown option values before the run verb (now only known non-running subcommands end the check), per-launcher value options (conda's `-n` takes a value, uv's does not), `-m OTHER` as the program, `coverage run -m calu`, hatch's `env:script`; ordinary programs now include ty, pdoc, bandit, vulture, codespell, interrogate, pyupgrade, deptry, and the generic names test/lint/build/docs were dropped (so `hatch run test calu` asks). Also: calu's compose file is recognised by its content (`name: calu-db`), so `docker compose -f local-compose.yml` from its folder asks and another project's does not; `script -qc"..."`; `git checkout -fq`/`--ours`/`--theirs FILE`; interpreter heredocs (`python3 - <<EOF`) and code `shlex` cannot split are scanned for path literals; `session.switches_branch` replaces a regex that backtracked exponentially. 946 tests; every case of the four reviews is pinned in both directions. |
| D175 | **A fifth review, of D174 -- and where the rules engine stands** (2026-11-12). Found: a wrapper's quoted multi-word command lost its arguments, because the splitter flattened the words and `command_variants` re-split them (`script -c 'git push --force origin main'` and `env -S 'python -m calu'` went to the checker) -- `ParsedCommand.argvs` now keeps each simple command's words; `diff_targets` matched the raw text, so `"patch"`, `pa''tch` or `git 'apply'` skipped it, a file name touching `;`/`|`/`<` was never read, and a word `patch`/`apply`/`am` anywhere (`git commit -am`, `npm version patch`) made every named file a diff -- it now runs only when a parsed simple command, in any wrapper form, is `patch` or `git apply`/`am`, reads the splitter's resolved paths of that command plus the tool's own words against `-C`/`-d`, and asks when a source cannot be resolved (`git apply $f`) or a cap is hit (51 diff files, 5001 changed files); a note per candidate outside the allowlist no longer floods the checker; /proc, /sys and /dev are never read. `git checkout FILE` counts as a write unless a branch option is given (the allow list of options missed `--conflict`, `-2`, ...). calu detection: `python -m coverage run -m calu`, `-mcalu`/`--module calu`, coverage/ipython/pypy run directly, `python src/calu`, hatch `env:calu`. Compose: `COMPOSE_PROJECT_NAME=calu-db`, and the content check only for container commands. Inline code: a heredoc is scanned only when an interpreter reads it (commit messages and `cat > run.py <<EOF` asked). Here-strings, pipes into an interpreter and `COMPOSE_FILE=` were closed in D176; a compose file on stdin and a quoted `git checkout` in D177. **Where it stands:** five review rounds each closed real holes and each found new spellings -- a pattern list over shell text is a best-effort layer by nature. The rules make the common and the careless cases ask without a model call; the sandbox (D15, Q1a: it also denies the Docker socket) is what stops a determined command. **Recommendation for the owner:** keep the sandbox required -- `claude.sandbox.fail_if_unavailable` is `true` by default, so a session without a working sandbox does not start unless someone switches the sandbox off -- and treat a session where it is off as one where the checker, not the rules, is the last line. Further review rounds of the rules engine are worth doing when it changes, not as a standing loop. 972 tests. |
| D176 | **Three gaps D175 left to the checker, closed** (2026-11-12): a here-string read by an interpreter (`python3 <<< "open('.env')"`) and text piped into one (`echo "..." | python3`) are scanned for path literals like `-c CODE` (D167); `COMPOSE_FILE=FILE docker compose ...` asks when FILE is calu's compose file by content (D174). Plain pipes of data into a script (`echo hello | python3 count.py`) and harmless here-strings are unaffected. 978 tests. |
| D177 | **The last two gaps of D175, closed** (2026-11-12): `docker compose -f - ...` reads its compose file from the command (a heredoc or a pipe), so a container command whose text holds `name: calu-db` asks; `session.switches_branch` removes quotes and backticks before splitting, so `"git" checkout x` and `` `git checkout x` `` count for `honesty.scope = branch` (an `echo 'git checkout'` counts too -- the safe direction, and only for branch-scoped marks). 983 tests. |
| D178 | **The owner's answers of 2026-11-12, and the order of work** (Q16, Q19, Q20, Q21): (1) D178 -- Claude Code loads its settings exactly as a plain `claude` does (`setting_sources` = user, project, local). (2) D179 -- every allow and deny list ships empty; the former defaults become opt-in sets like `publishing`; calu's own built-in protections (its config folder, Claude running calu, calu's database container) stay, because they protect calu's escalations rather than the project. (3) D180 -- the honesty register moves into the session's memory: no table, no branch scope, no stored reach; what survives a session is nothing. (4) D181 -- checker instructions split into calu's fixed system instructions and the user's compact instructions, the honesty rules a separate editable text; the instructions say that marking another file is the exception. |
| D179 | **No rules are shipped** (2026-11-12, the owner's Q16): `setup.default_rules()` returns nothing, so a new database's six lists start empty and every step goes to a checker. The former defaults are the set `recommended` (`setup.recommended_rules`, the six `calu/defaults/rules/<list>.txt` files), added with `calu rules add-set recommended` or the Rules page's button; `RULE_SETS` sets can now span every list (`rule_set_rows`). A database set up earlier keeps its rules -- nothing a user has is deleted. calu's own protections are not list rules and stay: Claude may not read `~/.config/calu` (D-built-in), run calu (D160) or reach calu's database container (D171) without asking, because they guard the escalations themselves. The unit tests' fixtures and the evaluation's comparison (D76) use the recommended set. |
| D180 | **The honesty register is transient: in the session's memory, stored nowhere** (2026-11-12, the owner's answer to Q19: dirty and clean are kept in memory and not persisted anywhere, least of all somewhere as easy to modify as a table; a commit, not a branch, is what is dirty or clean). `honesty.Register` holds a session's marks; it starts empty and ends with the session, and another session of the project has its own. Migration 0024 drops `honesty_marks` (with the author, file snapshot and reach columns of 0020/0021/0023). Gone with it: the project-wide sharing and its notification channel (D82, D92), reading the register at start, `calu honesty list|clear` (D84), the marks tables on the web Rules and session pages, the per-checker mark statistics (D87), evaluation cases from overruled marks (D94, D95), the branch record and `honesty.scope` (D142), the branch-switch detection of D168/D174/D177 and the store-failure path of D111. A person clears a mark through the session: the new protocol request `honesty.clear` (17 requests now), `/clear-mark ID` in `calu attach`, or the Clear button of the web live view, which shows the open marks from the snapshot and `honesty.update`. `Session.announce_honesty` tells the clients after a settings reload, so switching `honesty.mode` to off still reaches them (D113). The reach tag of D141 stays, in memory. |
| D181 | **A checker's prompt is calu's fixed part plus the user's compact instructions** (2026-11-12, the owner's Q16: system instructions -- the role, the answers, the step summary -- apart from user instructions -- what to escalate, pass, deny -- so the user's part can be much shorter; the honesty rules separate and editable for a project's documentation standard, though not meant to be edited often). `calu.defaults.instructions.compose_instructions(route, user, rules, marks)` joins `checkers/system/<route>.md`, the user's text from `checker_types` under "# The user's instructions", and for the command, edit and changeset routes the honesty rules (`honesty.rules`, empty: `checkers/honesty-rules.md`) and, while `honesty.mode` is not off, `system/marks.md`. The session and `calu evals` build prompts this way. The shipped user parts are rewritten compactly; `checkers/legacy/` keeps the old texts so `calu setup` replaces an unedited old default and leaves an edited one. `calu honesty show|edit|reset [--project]` and a Markdown text area on the settings page edit `honesty.rules`. |
| D182 | **One settings page for everything that steers a session** (2026-11-12, the owner: the Rules and Settings pages redesigned from scratch as one modern page; checker prompts and allow and deny lists are settings too). `calu.web.settings` builds a page model -- panes, groups, fields -- from three stores: the configuration values (generated from `Config`, so a new field or section appears by itself), the `checker_types` rows (instructions; for an extra checker also enabled, model and limits) and the rule lists as text. The page shows one pane at a time with a sidebar, a search across all panes, one save for every pane, a per-value Reset that removes the scope's stored value, and in a project scope the inherited global value; a refused value is reported at its field, keeps what was typed and does not stop the other writes. A switch counts as off only if the form contained it (`_present`), so a partial form cannot switch anything off. `/rules`, `/checkers/{name}` and the old POST routes are gone (the two GETs redirect). Replaces the pages of D137 and D138. |
| D183 | **A review of the whole code base against the requirements, and its fixes** (2026-11-12): eight reviewers, each finding checked by a second agent, then two rounds of fixes, each fix reviewed again. The step gate was found fail-closed in its main path; the defects were at its edges and on the input side of the checkers. D184 to D190 record what changed. Decisions the review superseded are named there: D19 (credential), D25 (checker tools), D26 (commit routing), D30 (printed token), D56 and D68 (heartbeat), D161 (web login). |
| D184 | **A commit is judged by the command checkers, and reviewed on exactly what it records** (replaces the routing of D26): a Bash line with a commit stays on the command route. The changeset review is additional and applies to a line that is one plain `git commit` (with `-a`, `--amend`, message options, the `$(cat <<'EOF' ...)` message form); any other line with a commit is refused without a model call, with the instruction to run the rest first and `git commit` alone. Options are read as git reads them, and a line whose shell syntax calu does not resolve is refused the same way. calu reads the diff without the configuration of the repository, the user or the system, so reading it starts no program; a changeset or write set that calu could not read completely is escalated (or refused with the way to make it readable) and never left to a model's approval. `auto_escalate.commands` covers commits. |
| D185 | **What a checker reads is fenced, labelled and complete, and its tools stay in the project** (supersedes D25): calu confines the checker tools itself -- no absolute path, no `..`, nothing that resolves outside the project or into calu's own directories, with the read deny rules applied to the resolved path, a size bound per file and a time bound per search. Text that comes from Claude, a tool or a file reaches a checker as fenced data on its own lines and cannot take the form of a user turn or of calu's notes. Only text a human sent through calu is recorded as user input; other user-role messages of Claude Code are recorded with their own source. Project files such as AGENTS.md appear as untrusted repository text: binding rules belong in the checker instructions in the database. A step, diff or path that was cut turns a checker's approval into an escalation; the limit rose from 20,000 to 60,000 characters. |
| D186 | **While an escalation is open, the session's other tool calls wait** (2026-11-12, the README's promise made true): a new tool call that needs a check waits while an escalation of the session is open or being opened, up to 300 s (the PreToolUse hook ends at 900 s); an approval still held then becomes an escalation itself. The database decides a client's `escalation.decide` before the step is released. Stop, timeout and an unreachable database are not a human denial: a write set under review is then left as it is and Claude is told that nobody decided. A cancelled wait closes its row, and a session that ends closes its open escalations. One step has 420 s to be decided, a checker 300 s. |
| D187 | **A session survives the loss of its database, and requirement 16 says what it means** (replaces D19's wiping): the password, the key derived from it and the decrypted buffer are wiped before Claude Code starts; the connection string stays in the memory of the calu process (`calu.db.ConnectionSource`), never in the environment, a command line or a file, and `calu run` clears its dumpable flag. Both connections reconnect with backoff; while the database is away, steps wait and nothing is approved by default. An error that cannot heal (authentication, a removed role or database) stops the session with a clear message. The configuration is re-read after every reconnect and every 60 s; if it cannot be read, every auto-escalate category is switched on until it can. The heartbeat is a fixed 30 s (replaces D56, D68). Migration 0025 gives every session a key that proves its identity to the database (corrects D28, D49), 0026 separates the row-level rules per command. |
| D188 | **A project is found by its directory, and an unknown one is an error**: `calu project add DIR` records directory and host; `calu run` without `--project` uses the project registered for the working directory or its nearest registered parent; an unknown `--project` ends the start. `calu run` refuses a `session` credential stored without a password (requirement 17), and a closed stdin no longer stops a session that has work. |
| D189 | **Client tokens can be revoked while connected, and clients that fall behind are told** (corrects D30): the database is asked for every token; a revoked token is refused for changing requests at once and its connection closed within 15 s (close code 4001); a client more than 1000 events behind is closed with 4002 and reconnects with replay. A request that changes the session is finished even if its client disconnects. An ACP editor gets a dialog for every escalation that is already open when it attaches, and `calu acp` ends when the session's connection ends. |
| D190 | **The web interface has a host name of its own and separates reading from changing** (replaces the cookie of D161): `calu web` answers only as `calu.localhost` (still bound to 127.0.0.1). The token is exchanged for a session cookie and a second secret kept by the page; every change needs both, so a cookie alone changes nothing. Every response carries a Content-Security-Policy; error pages are templates. `calu web` reconnects to the database and says so on its pages while it is away. |
| D191 | **The sandbox settings are pinned, and a settings file that would change them stops the start**: calu writes every sandbox key it knows into `--settings`, always denies reads of its own directories (derived from their real location), and refuses to start when a loaded Claude Code settings file holds sandbox entries beyond calu's configuration, a sandbox key calu does not know, or cannot be read. `hidden_env` works and is documented. New setting `claude.require_auto_mode` (default off): with it, a step the checkers pass outside auto mode waits for a human. Measured with CLI 2.1.319: a headless start is in permission mode "default" unless `claude.permission_mode` or Claude Code's own `defaultMode` says "auto"; the start line says which. Open for the owner: whether calu sets "auto" itself (Q22). |
| D192 | **An edit step shows the contract of the function it changes** (2026-11-12): a diff shows the changed lines, and the signature and docstring of the function they belong to are often outside it, so a checker judged whether code still does what it says without the text that says it. For a Python file, `calu.checkers.symbols.changed_definitions` compares the file before and after the edit with the standard library's `ast` (nothing is imported or run) and names each existing function, method or class whose signature, docstring or own code differs; `render_step` lists them below the diff with their header and docstring after the edit and which of the three parts changed ("the body changed; the signature and docstring did not"). New definitions are left out (the diff shows all of them); at most six are listed, each docstring cut at 2,000 characters. Other languages get the diff alone. This is the first piece of the honesty redesign that needs no decision of the owner. |
| D193 | **No file of the workspace is part of a checker's context** (2026-11-12, the owner: files included verbatim in the context can be turned into injected prompts for the checkers, and that is not wanted; replaces the labelling of D185). `context.files` (AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md, GLOSSARY.md, MEMORY.md) and `context.skill_dirs` (skill names and descriptions) are removed; the cached prefix is the chat summary alone. The agent under review can write those files, and a cloned repository brings its own, so they are not a source of rules. A project's coding and documentation standard belongs in the checker instructions, which are in the database; the shipped instructions say so, and no longer speak of "project guidelines". A checker that needs a file reads it with its tools, where the content arrives as a tool result. Claude Code's own loading of these files is untouched (D178). A stored `context.files` or `context.skill_dirs` is ignored. |
| D194 | **A blank model is Claude Haiku: on the Anthropic API, or on Bedrock in an EU region in Bedrock mode** (2026-11-12, the owner; replaces the Mistral defaults and the detection at setup of D129). `CheckerConfig.model`, `context.narrator_model` and `context.summarizer_model` default to blank. `Config` fills every blank model when it is validated, with `calu.config.default_model(claude.use_bedrock)`: `anthropic:claude-haiku-5-5`, or `bedrock:eu.anthropic.claude-haiku-4-5-20251001-v1:0` (the EU inference profile; `calu.models` calls an `eu.` profile in `eu-central-1` when the environment names no region). The stored value stays blank, so switching Bedrock mode moves the default with it. An extra checker type with a blank model gets the same default when the settings are loaded. `calu setup` stores no model unless `--checker-model` is given (`auto` is the detection of D129); `calu config models default` removes every stored model. The settings page offers the default as the first entry of every model list. Not verified against Bedrock from the development machine: the profile id is the one calu used before. |
| D195 | **`calu db reset` drops and recreates the database, behind two confirmations** (2026-11-12, the owner): the schema `public` is dropped with every table, function and type and created again in one transaction, then the migrations run and the default checker definitions are loaded, so the result is what a fresh `calu setup` leaves; rows are not merely deleted. The roles and their passwords are outside the schema and stay, so the stored credentials keep working. It needs the `admin` credential's password typed (a credential stored without one is refused for this command) and the phrase `yes delete the db`, which must take at least two seconds from the prompt to the line end; a piped, pasted or automatic answer arrives faster and deletes nothing. Before asking, the command prints how many sessions, steps, escalations, projects, rules and checker definitions it would delete and how many sessions have not ended. |
| D196 | **Code honesty is redesigned around certified commits and a computed state** (2026-11-12, the owner's decisions after a review found the register of D85/D180 unable to hold: a change made by a shell command opened no mark, the run-blocking matched file names and so refused `cat` while letting `pytest` through, and an empty register read as "clean"). Being built on the branch `feature/honesty-certification`. (1) **Trusted commits** are stored in the database; nothing is assumed honest. A human certifies a commit with `/trust <commit-or-branch>` in the chat or `calu honesty trust` (a branch means the commit it points at now); calu certifies a new commit itself when every change since the baseline was verified. A session's baseline is the nearest trusted ancestor of HEAD; without one, everything counts as unverified. (2) **State** is computed, in memory only: marks on functions a checker found dishonest (a file is dishonest while it has one), contents a checker verified, and unreviewed files (changed since the baseline by anything but a verified edit). Marks are applied after the tool call succeeded. (3) **More than `honesty.max_open_marks` (3) open marks** require an honesty review; single fixes then clear nothing, which keeps documentation updates close to their edits. (4) **The honesty review** is a reviewer agent with a larger budget and read-only tools that judges the difference to the baseline for the unreviewed and marked files, in concurrent batches; above `honesty.review_max_files` (300) it does not run and the step escalates. Recorded as the later solution for that case, not built: the call graph of the unclean functions, reviewed in waves from its leaves, so that each reviewer can rely on the documentation of what the reviewed function calls. (5) **Enforcement** (`honesty.mode = enforce`): running project code and committing need a clean state. Whether a command executes project code is the command checker's judgement; calu decides from it and the state (marks: deny with reasons; only unreviewed files: review first; no baseline: escalate). Reading and editing are never blocked. `track` shows the state and blocks nothing. (6) Every checker is told which code is unverified and must not judge it by its names or documentation.
