(7) **Files git does not hold** (untracked, ignored or temporary files the session wrote) are verified by a SHA-256 of their content, in memory, so they are inspected again after a restart. As the exception, a person can store trust in one file's content (`calu honesty trust --file`, `/trust-file`), which then holds across restarts until the content changes. (8) **Who established trust is recorded, and a setting says which trust is sufficient** (the owner): calu stores what it verifies itself. Every trusted commit and every trusted file content carries its source: `user` (a person certified it), `review` (calu's honesty review) or `check` (calu's step checkers), in that order of strength. `honesty.sufficient_trust` (default `check`) is the weakest source that counts; trust from a weaker source stays stored but is treated as not trusted. A restart therefore does not make verified files unreviewed, which replaces the in-memory-only rule of (7). |
| D197 | **The changeset checker gets limits that fit a review of a whole change** (2026-11-12, the owner: its limits must be far more generous for what it is meant to do). Defaults: 40 model requests (was 8), 120,000 output tokens for a check (was 24,000), 16,000 tokens for one answer (was 8,000), in `calu.config.changeset_limits`, in the seeded checker definition and, by migration 0027, in an existing definition wherever its values are lower. The diff of a commit is shown up to 240,000 characters (was 60,000) before it counts as cut. The time bounds are unchanged: one checker call has 300 s and the decision about a step 420 s, because the gate runs inside Claude Code's hook, which ends at 900 s; a review that needs longer escalates. Supersedes the 8 requests of D75. |
| D198 | **Rules are overrides for the exception; no command allow list is shipped, and old seeded rules are removed** (2026-11-12, the owner: no allow list and no deny list prefilled; they override the checker and, outside auto mode, everything else, and a script of an allowed name on the path can do anything). The lists were already empty on a new database (D179). Now: (1) the optional `recommended` set has no command allow list any more (`setup.RECOMMENDED_LISTS`); its deny lists and path lists stay as a set a person may add. The former list lives on as a test fixture of the rules engine. (2) Migration 0028 deletes the global rules that are exactly the ones `calu setup` seeded before D179; rules with another pattern and every project's rules stay. (3) The settings page and the documents present rules as the exception. Unchanged: an allow match is a PASS, so in auto mode Claude Code's own classifier still sees the command (D21). |
| D199 | **A full-screen terminal client, built with Textual** (2026-11-12, the owner: the line client is too primitive; subagents, model selection, a side chat, an agent switcher, the question tool, the used quota, stable scrolling, user input set apart, thinking shown, checker instructions, the honesty state, a key that starts a git program). `calu attach` on a terminal starts it (`src/calu/tui/`, the optional extra `tui`); `calu attach --plain`, a pipe, `TERM=dumb` or a missing Textual keep the line client, which is unchanged. The client is a model fed by protocol events (`tui/model.py`, no Textual import), a connection interface with a scripted fake for tests, and widgets that render the model. Nothing that asks for a decision is modal: an escalation or a question is a card between transcript and input, decided with two key presses on the step the first press was made for. For it the protocol gained `side.ask` (a question beside the running work, Claude Code's own side question), `usage.get`, `model.set` and the event `usage.update`. Checker instructions and `honesty.mode` are changed with the admin credential of the client's machine, never through the session (requirement 20). `f4` hands the terminal to an existing git program: gitui by default (the owner's choice), another one through `CALU_GIT_TOOL` or `git_tool` in `tui.toml`; without gitui the client offers to install it and never runs a command with `sudo`. User documentation is not written yet. |
| D200 | **calu gets its own plugins for VSCode and for the JetBrains IDEs; they are built after the terminal client, the web interface and standalone use work well** (2026-11-12, the owner's requirement 23; this revises D24 for these two editor families and answers the product half of Q2). The owner wants a proper plugin for VSCode, which may be modelled on sitl's extension (`../extensions/vscode`), and one for the JetBrains IDEs (PyCharm, RustRover). Each offers a full coding-agent interface (chat with streaming output, tool calls with their diffs, subagents, escalations and questions), the settings calu adds (checkers and their instructions, the honesty state and mode, auto-escalation), and connecting or attaching to running sessions. Both must work in the most modern remote development mode the IDE supports: for VSCode Remote-SSH, WSL and Dev Containers; for JetBrains the current remote development of the IDE (the owner notes that PyCharm has just added functionality there; read its present documentation before designing, and do not rely on what is written here). Design consequences, derived: (a) the plugins are clients of calu's own WebSocket protocol (D2), not of ACP, because ACP does not carry the checker verdicts, the honesty state or the settings; `calu acp` (D24) stays for every other editor. (b) The plugin layer stays thin (D11): what both plugins need and the protocol lacks is added to the protocol, as was done for the terminal client (D199), so that no logic exists twice in TypeScript and Kotlin. (c) In a remote mode the session, its WebSocket (loopback) and the token are on the remote host, so the part of the plugin that connects must run there or reach it through the IDE's own forwarding; this is the first thing to prove with a spike for each IDE. (d) Changing settings needs the `admin` credential and never goes through the session (requirement 20), as in the terminal client. Nothing of this is built. Order: phase 9 in §12. |
| D201 | **The web interface should bundle a git browser that also works through an SSH connection or tunnel, if that is possible** (2026-11-12, the owner's soft goal 6; this revises the choice in `src/calu/web/git.py` to only link into a git browser that exists elsewhere). Today a step links to its commit on the remote's site (GitHub, GitLab, or `web.git_url_template`). That shows nothing for commits that are not pushed, which is the normal case for a running session, and nothing for a repository without such a site. Intended: `calu web` shows the session's repository itself (log, a commit's diff, a file at a commit, the working tree's changes), read-only, also when the repository is on another host than the one `calu web` runs on. Open, to decide when it is built: whether calu bundles an existing browser or renders the output of `git` itself; and how the remote case is reached (git run on the session's host over SSH, or `calu web` run there and its port forwarded through an SSH tunnel, which already works for the rest of the interface). Constraints: read-only; no content filter or hook of the repository runs (as in D196's hashing); the existing commit links stay. Not built; it belongs to the web interface work that comes before the plugins of D200. |
| D202 | **A session records in which process table it runs, and only that decides whether a missing pid means a gone process** (2026-11-12, open developer work from the review of the whole code base). `calu sessions` closes the open escalations of a silent session whose process is gone (D56, D57) and looked the pid up for every session with this machine's host name. Two machines or containers with one host name have different process tables, so a listing on one could cancel the open escalation of a silent session of the other. A session now records `machine id|boot id|pid namespace` (`calu.machine`, `sessions.machine`, migration 0031; `calu_start_session` takes it as an optional ninth argument, so a calu of the version before still starts). The listing compares it with its own: the same boot id and pid namespace -> the pid is looked up as before; the same machine id and another boot id -> the process ended with the reboot, so the escalation is closed with that reason; everything else -> not judged from here, and the rule of 24 hours of silence applies. A session that recorded nothing (an older calu) is judged by the host name as before. Limit, accepted: machines cloned from one image share the machine id, so with the same host name a silent session of the one looks like one from before a reboot of the other. |
| D203 | **`calu list-sessions`, `calu run --fork`, and a session id that is known at the start** (2026-11-12, the owner's request). (1) `calu list-sessions [N]` (N = 5) lists the last N Claude conversations of the current directory on this host and of this OS user (Claude Code keeps the transcripts in that user's home directory, so others could not be resumed here), the freshest first, each with the age of its last activity, its Claude session id and the last message of the user (`calu.herd.conversations`; `operator` or `admin` credential, like `calu sessions`). A conversation is one Claude session id, however many `calu run`s it took; its last activity is the latest start of one of those runs or the latest stored turn; one without a stored turn is not listed, because there is nothing to resume. (2) `calu run --fork ID` continues a copy of a whole conversation under a new session id (the SDK's `resume` with `fork_session` and no fork point; `Fork()` without uuids), next to `--resume ID` and `--resume ID --fork-before-turn N` (D36). (3) calu chooses the session id of a new conversation and of a fork itself (a UUID, `--session-id`; measured in `spikes/RESULTS.md`: Claude Code reports exactly that id, also for a fork) and `calu run` prints it as `[session] <id>` when the session has started, before the first turn. Claude Code reports its id only with the first message of a turn, so without this the id could not be printed at the start. A plain resume keeps its id. A session a client starts through the protocol (`session.start`) still lets Claude Code choose. (4) Found while building it and fixed: a fork ran under a new id but its inherited turns stayed with the rows of the original, so a later resume of the fork (which reads a conversation back by its id, D65) found only the turns the fork had run itself. A fork now stores the turns it keeps under its own row (the summary was copied already), and only once Claude Code is running, so that a fork whose start fails leaves no conversation behind that is listed and cannot be resumed. The fork's first turn takes the number of the first discarded turn, also when the summary covers every kept turn, and the fork's copy of the summary is then recorded as covering the turns before the fork point only. `--resume` and `--fork` take a UUID, not a session title, because calu reads a conversation back by that id. Known and left: the copied turns carry no transcript uuids, so a fork cannot be forked again before one of its inherited turns; a line with wide characters can be wider than the terminal. And a fork before turn N kept the discarded turns in the context the checkers read; it now keeps only the turns before N, and says so at the start when the folded summary already covers a discarded turn, which cannot be unfolded. |
| D204 | **calu is one package without optional extras, installed with `uv tool install`** (2026-11-13, the owner: `uv tool install calu` should pull in everything; from the GitHub URL first, from PyPI when it all works). The extras `web` (Starlette, Jinja2, uvicorn) and `tui` (Textual) of D41 and D199 are ordinary dependencies now. `uv tool install git+https://github.com/kadeng/calu` installs every command; verified by installing the checkout into a throwaway tool directory and running the installed `calu` (version, protocol schema, both front ends importable, `calu doctor`). The code that copes with a missing Textual or Starlette stays, as a message about a damaged installation. The single-file binary of D41 stays buildable (`scripts/build-binary.sh`, now with the terminal client's packages in it, not rebuilt or run since); publishing on PyPI needs the one-time setup named in `release.yml`. |
| D205 | **`calu setup` sets the database up in one of three variants, and the local one is started on demand** (2026-11-13, the owner's requirement 25; designed by three independent proposals and a synthesis over a map of the code, then built). (1) **What is stored.** The secret of a credential store entry is now a record (`calu.db.target.Target`, JSON) instead of a bare connection string: the variant (`docker`, `server`, `rds-iam`), the connection parameters, how the login authenticates, and for Docker the port and the data location. It stays inside the encrypted entry, so no unauthenticated file decides which database a session trusts, which directory a container mounts or which port it uses. A connection string as stored before is still read; the one an earlier `calu db up` wrote is read as the Docker variant on the named volume, so existing installations need no conversion. (2) **Docker.** The cluster's files are in a host directory chosen at setup (default `~/.local/state/calu/db`; the directory is 0700 and its subdirectory `pg` is what the container mounts, because the container's PostgreSQL user has to reach the mount -- measured with postgres:18 on Docker Desktop/WSL2: the cluster ends up owned by the container's user, mode 0700). `calu run` starts the container when its port does not answer, after the session password was typed (the location comes out of the decrypted entry) and before it connects; the admin and herd commands and `calu web` do the same (`calu.db.connection.open_stored`). The reconnect path of a running session does not start it: after `calu db down` a session waits. A start without the owner password never initialises a new, empty cluster. The login records of the local database require SCRAM from the server (`require_auth=scram-sha-256`, measured with libpq 18), so another listener on the port, or a cluster in a replaced directory, is refused instead of trusted. (3) **Existing server.** Setup asks host, port, database, user and password; that user is the owner login. Before anything is stored or changed it connects and runs a preflight (`calu.setup.preflight`): PostgreSQL 14 or newer, the right to create the two roles or to manage them if they exist, CREATE on the schema `public`, and a database without foreign tables -- each problem with the SQL that fixes it. One login for everything is not offered: the table owner passes row-level security and counts as operator, so a session that logged in as the owner could change the rules it is judged by. A remote host gets `sslmode=require` unless `--sslmode`/`--sslrootcert` say otherwise. (4) **RDS with IAM.** The owner login authenticates with a token from `boto3` for an explicitly named AWS profile, made anew for every connection attempt (`ConnectionSource.open`) and never stored; TLS is `verify-full` with a CA file copied next to the credential store. The session login uses IAM too by default (`GRANT rds_iam TO calu_session`; the owner's decision of the same day that calu keeps its credentials and reconnects with them), or a generated password with `--session-auth password`. A token that cannot be made is an `OperationalError`, not a refused login, so a session keeps trying instead of giving up for good. Stated plainly: with IAM, who may log in is decided by the AWS policy and by who can use the AWS profile on the machine; while calu and Claude Code run as the same OS user, only the sandbox's read deny list keeps Claude's commands from `~/.aws`. D206 is what closes that. Not run against a real RDS instance. (5) **A repeated setup keeps the session login** (`--renew-session` replaces it): before, every `calu setup` cut off running sessions and other machines. The session login takes over only where the database is and how its server is verified (`Target.for_role`), not every parameter of the owner's connection string. (6) **Roles are per server.** `calu_session` and `calu_operator` belong to the whole PostgreSQL server; setup refuses to replace a session login that exists unless told to. A login role per person or machine, a database per team, and a schema per user (the owner's tentative soft goal 7) are recorded as the later steps, in that order of cost; the stored record already names the role and the schema (`"schema": "public"`, the only accepted value), so none of them needs a new store format. (7) **What an independent review of the build found and what was changed for it:** a refused IAM token is never a final refusal (three of them ended a session although a renewed AWS sign-in would have let it go on; such a login now keeps retrying and says that the sign-in may have expired); making a token is bounded to 15 s and abandoned when the caller is cancelled; the preflight asks for `CREATEROLE` on every version and for the admin option on existing roles only from PostgreSQL 16 on (it had refused every second setup on 14 and 15); a database that was just chosen always gets a new session login, so a `session` entry left from another database is not kept; a first start that failed (docker missing, the port taken, a mistyped password) can be repeated with the same command and the same directory; after the cluster is initialized the container is created once more without the owner password, so `docker inspect` does not show it (confirmed on a real container); only a string without `require_auth` is taken for the container of an earlier `calu db up`, and for a record on the old volume that does not exist nothing is started; the operator login of the local database is handed out as the record that also names the data directory; an owner record that finds its server through a libpq service file is refused before any role is changed; `--session-auth password` on RDS takes `calu_session` out of `rds_iam`. |
| D206 | **calu runs privileged, Claude Code runs as an unprivileged user: decided, not built** (2026-11-13, the owner's requirement 26, which replaces the dropping of credentials in requirement 16). The model: `calu run` is started as root, or by a user with password-protected sudo, in which case calu executes itself again through sudo and passes the original user; as root the user for Claude Code is named explicitly, as a uid (a name where one exists). calu adds no users. calu's installation, credentials and database files are in system directories or below `/root` (`/usr/local/lib/calu` and `/usr/local/bin/calu` through `uv tool install` with `UV_TOOL_DIR` and `UV_TOOL_BIN_DIR`, `/etc/calu`, `/var/lib/calu/db`), never in the unprivileged user's home. What this gives: Claude Code and everything it starts cannot read calu's credentials or the AWS profile calu signs with, cannot signal or trace the calu process, and cannot edit calu's code -- by the operating system's user separation instead of by the sandbox's deny list. Measured already (`spikes/RESULTS.md`, "Claude Code as a bare uid"): Claude Code runs as a uid that has no name and no home directory in a container, but then stores nothing, so a conversation cannot be resumed; with `HOME` set to any directory that uid can write it works fully. The owner's rule: given a uid, calu gives Claude Code a state directory owned by that uid as its home (for example `/var/lib/calu/claude/<uid>`, a volume if it should outlive a container); given a user name, that user is assumed to have a home directory and Claude Code uses it. Consequences: the re-execution through sudo passes the user **name**, so that Claude Code keeps the invoking user's own login and conversations in `~/.claude` (a uid alone would give it an empty state directory); the elevated calu checks that name against `SUDO_UID` and takes home and groups from the system's user database, not from environment variables; and a numeric argument always means a bare uid with a state directory from calu, also when that uid has a name on the machine, so that the rule is predictable. Conditions the build has to meet, each a hole otherwise: (a) calu elevates only from an installation that the unprivileged user cannot write, and refuses otherwise; (b) everything calu itself runs inside the workspace (git for snapshots and the honesty state) runs as the unprivileged user, because git executes programs a repository configures; (c) the user comes from what sudo reports (`SUDO_UID`), and Claude Code's environment (home, PATH, login) is built for that user explicitly, since sudo resets it; (d) `calu run` as root without a named user is refused, so that Claude Code never runs as root by accident. Until this is built, `calu run` behaves as before (one user). First step: a spike that starts Claude Code under another uid through the Agent SDK (its `user` option) with the hooks and the permission callback working across the two users. |
| D207 | **What starts Claude Code under another user is part of calu's installation, not a file written at run time** (2026-11-13, the owner's addition to requirement 26; measured by `spikes/spike_other_uid.sh`). The Agent SDK starts one executable (`cli_path`) and appends its own arguments; its `user` option changes the uid only and leaves root's group and supplementary groups (measured), and it passes the whole environment of the calling process on. calu therefore names its own launcher as `cli_path`: the console script `calu-claude` (`calu.launch`), which the installer creates next to `calu` in the installation's `bin` directory, the directory condition (a) of D206 already requires to be unwritable for the unprivileged user. calu writes no wrapper script. The launcher takes one record from the environment variable `CALU_LAUNCH` (JSON: uid, gid, supplementary groups, home directory, the path of the real `claude`, and the names and name prefixes of the environment variables to keep), sets groups, gid and uid in that order, checks that all three took effect, builds the environment from an empty one (the kept names from what it was given, plus `HOME`, `USER` and `LOGNAME`) and replaces itself with `claude` and the arguments it was given. Started without root rights it accepts only its own uid and gid, so the same path can be tested without root and a user who runs it by hand gains nothing. Without the record it refuses. The record holds no secret. What it does not cover yet: the single-file binary has no second console script (soft goal 2); it would need the one executable to act as the launcher. |
| D208 | **`calu serve`: one process that is authenticated once and runs many sessions; a new session is asked for through the database** (2026-11-13, the owner's requirement 27). (1) **What it is.** `calu serve` asks the session password once, keeps the `session` login as `calu run` does (D205: kept, reconnects), starts the local database when that variant is configured, and then waits. Each session it starts is the same code path as `calu run` (`calu.run`): its own two database connections bound to its own session row, its own Claude Code process, its own protocol server with its own address and token. Every existing client therefore works unchanged: `calu attach` in the project directory, `calu sessions`, the web interface, `calu acp`. The sessions are tasks of the one process, not child processes: a session is mostly waiting, a Python process per session costs its imports again (seconds and about 100 MB each), and the credential would have to be handed to each child. A failure of one session ends that session only. (2) **How a session is asked for.** Not through a socket of the server: who may start a session would then be decided by who can reach that socket, and the commands of a running Claude Code session can reach what its user can. A request is a row in the table `session_requests`, written with the `operator` login (the login `calu attach` already uses, D105; stored without password it asks nothing, D104): host, OS user, directory, what to do (new, resume, fork, fork before a turn), the Claude session id, the first prompt, model, effort and project. The server is notified (`session_requested`), claims the request through a function of the database (only requests for its own host and OS user, only ones younger than a minute, each exactly once), starts the session and writes the session id or the reason of the failure back; the requester reads its row until it is finished and attaches as `calu attach --session` does. Claude Code has no database login, so it cannot ask for a session, which it could then attach to and decide escalations in. The same table lets the web interface and the full-screen client offer "new session" and "fork" later without a new mechanism, also from another machine. (3) **Commands.** `calu serve [--max-sessions N]`; `calu start [DIR] [-p PROMPT] [--resume ID | --fork ID] [--fork-before-turn N] [--model M] [--effort E] [--project P] [--no-attach]` files the request, waits for the answer and attaches (the full-screen client on a terminal, the line client otherwise). When no server answers within the waiting time, the request is withdrawn and the command says that `calu serve` does not seem to run for this user on this host. (4) **A served session ends** when a client sends `shutdown` (it ends that session, not the server), when it fails, or when the server is stopped (Ctrl-C or SIGTERM stops every session in order). It does not end by being idle, because the point of the server is that a session waits for its user. (5) **What the server prints**: one line per event of a session's life (requested, started with its directory and ids, ended), each with the session's short id; the conversation itself is shown by the clients. (6) **Limits and refusals**: at most `--max-sessions` sessions at once (default 10); a directory that does not exist and a resume of a conversation this server already runs are refused with a message in the request row; a request for another host or user is not claimed at all. (7) **With D206** the server is the privileged process and its sessions run Claude Code as the one unprivileged user the server was started for; a request for another OS user is not claimed. (8) **Not in the first build**: starting sessions from the web interface or from inside the full-screen client, and a server on a machine other than the requester's (the table allows it; attaching needs a reachable address). (10) **Also added: starting a session from the clients.** The web interface's session page posts to `/sessions/<id>/request` (new, fork, continue, fork before a turn): it files the request for the host, user and directory of that session, waits like `calu start` and redirects to the new session's live view, or says that no server answered. The full-screen client has `/new` and `/fork`: it files the request with this machine's `admin` credential (the access it already uses for a checker's instructions and `honesty.mode`, asking for the password), ends, and `calu attach` then attaches to the new session with a token of its own and revokes the old one. The request of the client names this machine and this user, so a client attached from another machine gets no answer and is told so. Not built: the same in the line client and through ACP, and a "new session" on the fleet page for a directory no session ran in yet. (9) **Added the same day:** `calu serve --idle-timeout MINUTES` ends a session that was idle that long with no client attached (default: never); `--user` and `--sudo` as for `calu run` (D211, D212). A resume of a conversation that a separate `calu run` still has is not refused: the session login cannot see whether another process's session is alive, and Claude Code itself does not refuse a second writer either. |
| D209 | **A conversation that was started with `claude` itself can be listed, resumed and forked** (2026-11-13, the owner's requirement 27, last sentence). Claude Code keeps every conversation of a directory as a transcript below its configuration directory, and the Agent SDK reads them (`list_sessions`, `get_session_messages`). `calu list-sessions` lists, after the conversations the database knows, those of the directory it has no record of, marked as started outside calu, with age and last user input like the others; `calu run --resume ID`, `--fork ID` and the same options of `calu start` take such an id. What the checkers know about it: one turn that says the conversation ran without calu and quotes what its transcript says the user wrote (the last 30 messages). It is given as a report, not as user input of this session: a transcript is a file in the user's home directory that calu did not write and cannot verify, and text that counts as the user's words can authorize a step. Nothing else of the transcript is given to the checkers, because Claude's own text reaches them only narrated (the narrator is not run over an old transcript). The turn is stored with the session, so a later resume finds it in the database. Built: `calu.claude.transcripts`, `Session._unwitnessed`, `herd.with_transcripts`; the report is rendered under the heading of a narrated answer, which is imprecise and left so. What does not carry over: no step of that conversation was checked, and the honesty state does not depend on it (it is computed from the trusted commits and the files as they are, D196). A conversation that is still open in a running `claude` should be forked, not resumed: two processes would write one transcript. |
| D210 | **The skill `honest-code` states the honesty rule for coding agents, and the rule is about names and doc comments only** (2026-11-13, the owner's text and two rulings). `.claude/skills/honest-code/SKILL.md`: a unit (function, method, class, module, script, executable) is honest when its name says what it does, its doc comment or co-located documentation gives intent, behaviour, inputs, outputs and side effects at the unit's level of abstraction, and it calls only honest units and trusted third-party libraries; honesty must hold before the code is run or tested. The owner's rulings: a README or guide is **not** covered (the intent is honest doc comments and honest names, not documents that may or may not be read when looking at a function); what a trusted third party is depends on the project and is not defined in the skill beyond the typical case (popular, well documented libraries; components of the OS distribution or of a renowned vendor). A third ruling of the same day: a test is honest when it also describes the side effects it has beyond checking something, including what it prints when it passes and when it fails, at an appropriate level of abstraction; plain error information needs little description, but data a test prints that it read from a database, a file, an environment variable, a subprocess or global state must be named, because that decides whether running it is safe and keeps data confidential. A fourth: a configuration that can widen a unit's side effects must be mentioned in the unit's contract and is then code the honesty rule applies to, wherever it is stored (it has the standing of an imported package: it may name classes or functions that are invoked, files that are read or written); where a configuration cannot carry doc comments, a checker understands and follows its references. The owner's answer for calu's shipped checker rules (`defaults/checkers/honesty-rules.md`), the same day: code honesty is about the code and its inline documentation. A README should not contradict the code, but it may leave out details or exaggerate; the honesty rules do not apply to it, the general rules do (a README that calls a script harmless while the script sends the user's keys away is plainly dishonest). The rules file was changed accordingly: a stale document is no longer listed as dishonesty, and a document that misstates a harmful fact stays an escalation. |
| D211 | **calu as root does its own work in the project as the unprivileged user: `--user` on `calu run` and `calu serve`** (2026-11-13, builds conditions (b) and (d) of D206; `calu.asuser`). (1) **The option.** As root, `--user` is required (root itself only as `--user root` or `--user 0`, which runs everything as one user as before); without root it is refused unless it names the user calu runs as. A number, `UID` or `UID:GID`, is always a bare uid: no name, no supplementary groups, and the home `/var/lib/calu/claude/<uid>` that calu creates for Claude Code's login and conversations. A name takes uid, gid, groups and home from the system's user database, and the home must exist. (2) **Programs calu starts.** Every program calu itself starts in the project (git for the snapshots, the honesty state, the changeset checker and the directory search; the search's regular-expression child; the `claude` of a `claude:` checker model) gets the user's uid, gid and groups and an environment built from an empty one (`asuser.child`), because git runs what a repository configures. Measured with a repository whose clean filter records who runs it: uid 1000. (3) **calu's own file access.** The condition of D206 named only programs; reading is the same hole, since a symbolic link in the project can point at a file only root may read, and a checker's read tool, a rendered step or the honesty state would then hand its content on. Linux keeps the file-system uid, gid and supplementary groups per thread, separate from who the process is: after `asuser.enter`, in the main thread before any other thread exists, every file calu opens is opened with the user's rights and every file it creates belongs to the user, while the process stays root for signals, tracing and `/proc` (measured: `spikes/spike_fs_identity.py`). No call site had to change for this, which is why it was chosen over checking each path. Everything that needs root's files is read before the switch: the credential store, the checker keys. (4) **Where calu's state for that user goes.** The snapshots of the project are written by git as the user, so they are in `/var/lib/calu/state/<uid>` (owned by the user, 0700), which `enter` makes the process's `XDG_STATE_HOME`; the sandbox's deny list follows, because it is computed from the same variable. `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` of the calu process points at the user's `.claude`, so `calu.claude.transcripts` reads that user's conversations; the launcher never passes that variable on. (5) **The session's row** names the user calu works for (the name, or the uid as text), which is also the user whose requests a `calu serve --user` answers. (6) **Run for real** as root in the development image with the real Claude Code and uid 1000 (`spikes/smoke_privileged.sh`): a command of Claude ran as `uid=1000 gid=1000 groups=1000`; the write-set snapshot of that command worked; git's filter ran as uid 1000; every file in the project and below `/var/lib/calu` came out owned by 1000; Claude's read of a link to a root-only file was refused by the kernel. (7) **Not covered yet:** a thread that must read root's files after the switch (the AWS profile of the RDS variant when a token is renewed, a CA file that is not world-readable) -- the RDS variant is therefore not usable together with `--user` yet; Claude Code's sandbox under the other uid was not switched on in the run; a named user with a real home was not run (no second user on this machine, and calu adds none). |
| D212 | **`--sudo`, and a root calu keeps its files in the system directories** (2026-11-13, builds conditions (a) and (c) of D206 and the last part of requirement 26). (1) **`calu run --sudo` and `calu serve --sudo`**, started by an ordinary user: calu replaces itself by `sudo -- <the calu of this installation> <the same arguments> --user <the invoking user's name> --from-sudo`. It is an option and not the default, because `calu run` as one user keeps working as before. The name is passed, not the uid, so that Claude Code keeps that user's home, login and conversations (D206). (2) **Condition (a):** before elevating, calu checks the `calu` script, the interpreter and the package directory, and every directory above each: all must belong to root and none be writable by group or others; otherwise it refuses and prints the install command for root (`UV_TOOL_DIR=/usr/local/lib/calu UV_TOOL_BIN_DIR=/usr/local/bin UV_PYTHON_INSTALL_DIR=/usr/local/lib/calu/python uv tool install ...`; run in a container: it puts `calu` and `calu-claude` into `/usr/local/lib/calu/calu/bin` with links in `/usr/local/bin`, the interpreter below `/usr/local/lib/calu/python`, all readable and executable for the unprivileged user, which the launcher needs, and the check passes). (3) **Condition (c):** with `--from-sudo`, the uid of `--user` must be `SUDO_UID`; the environment of Claude Code is built by the launcher (D207), home and groups come from the system's user database (D211). A root who names another user by hand (`sudo calu run --user other`) is not held to `SUDO_UID`: that is root's decision. (4) **System directories:** a calu that runs as root keeps its credentials and checker keys in `/etc/calu` and its state in `/var/lib/calu` (`XDG_CONFIG_HOME=/etc`, `XDG_STATE_HOME=/var/lib`, set at the start of every command), so a local database's files default to `/var/lib/calu/db`. Started through sudo, the two variables are always overwritten, because what sudo lets through may be the invoking user's; a root that set them itself, without sudo, keeps them (a container, a test). (5) **Never run:** the path through a real sudo (this machine's asks a password; the image has no sudo). `docs/TESTING.md` item 30 is what the owner has to try. **What the owner's first use showed (2026-11-13):** (i) with sudo's password still remembered, `--sudo` asks nothing, and the root calu's "no credential 'session'" read as if the user's own setup were missing; the message now names the store and, under sudo, the two commands that copy the user's store to `/etc/calu`. (ii) A setup as root with the Docker variant replaced the container of the user's own local database (the same compose project and port, other files: `/var/lib/calu/db` instead of the volume): the old cluster's files stayed, but its sessions lost their database and the user's credentials no longer fitted what answered on the port. `calu.db.local.up` now refuses to start a local database while a container of the project exists whose files are elsewhere (`container_data`). One machine has one local calu database. (iii) The commands are inconsistent (`calu run --sudo` but `sudo calu setup`) and nothing moves an existing setup to `/etc/calu`; proposed, not decided: `--sudo` on every command that touches credentials or the database, and an import of the invoking user's store. Known gap: `sudo calu setup` creates the `session` credential in `/etc/calu`, while `calu attach` and `calu start` of the user need an `operator` credential in the user's own store, which the user creates separately (`calu credential new operator` as root, `calu credential set operator` as the user). |
| D213 | **Settings are changed as the database role `calu_admin`, with the admin password a person types; nothing is stored for it** (2026-11-13, the owner's correction: with calu privileged, the files are privileged, not the ability to change settings). (1) **The role.** Migration 0033 creates `calu_admin`: it may read and write every table and call every function, is a member of `calu_operator`, and has a policy `admin_all` on every table with row-level security (a test checks that none lacks it). It owns nothing: no schema change, no roles, no switching off of the audit triggers; migrations and logins stay with the owner's login, which is root's where calu is privileged. (2) **Its password is the admin password.** `calu setup` sets it every time it runs, to the password typed at "Admin password" (the one that protects the stored owner login); the server gets the SCRAM verifier, computed in the client, not the password. An `admin` entry without a password leaves the role without a login. (3) **Clients.** Where a machine holds only an `operator` entry, the commands that change settings (`calu config`, `calu checker`, `calu rules`, `calu honesty`, `calu summary --edit`, `calu web`) ask for the admin password and log in as `calu_admin` at the operator's database (`cli._admin_secret`). The full-screen client is given the place of the database it found its session in, and saves a checker's instructions, `honesty.mode` and a session request by logging in there as `calu_admin` with the password typed at that moment (`StoreAdmin(database=...)`); a wrong password asks again. A stale `admin` entry of another database in the user's store is no longer used by the client. (4) **What this does not protect:** a client that runs as the same OS user as Claude Code shares that user's reach with Claude's unsandboxed programs; the password is typed into such a process. That is what running the client through sudo is for (requirement 26, last point). (5) **For an existing installation:** run `calu setup` once more as whoever holds the `admin` entry (as root where calu is privileged); it applies migration 0033 and sets the role's password. |
| D214 | **A client can run as root for its user: `calu attach --sudo`, `calu start --sudo`** (2026-11-13, the owner's suggestion; requirement 26, last point). (1) **Why.** A client that runs as the same OS user as Claude Code is within reach of every unsandboxed program Claude starts: its memory, its terminal, the password typed into it. As root it is not, by the same user separation that protects the session (D206). (2) **How.** The command runs itself again through sudo (the path and the installation check of D212) with `--user <you>`. As root it reads the `admin` entry of `/etc/calu` (else `operator`), asking its password once, and keeps that login in memory (`cli._held`). It finds the session, mints its token, then switches its file access and the programs it starts (the editor, the git program) to the user (`asuser.enter`), with the user's own `tui.toml`. (3) **No second authentication.** The held login serves every later step: saving a checker's instructions and `honesty.mode` (no password dialog), a session request (`/new`, `/fork`), minting and revoking tokens, and the two new commands of the full-screen client, `/running` (the sessions that run, of every directory) and `/switch <beginning of an id>` (attach this client to another one). Both commands also exist in a client that is not root; there they ask for the admin password (D213). (4) **A root-owned Unix socket** between a root session and a root client would add that only root can connect at all, where today any local process can connect to the session's loopback port and is stopped by the token. Not built: it would also shut out the user's own non-root `calu attach`, the web interface when it does not run as root, and an editor's ACP adapter, so it needs the owner's decision on whether a privileged session accepts only privileged clients; and the socket has to be created before the session switches its file identity. (5) **Not run:** the path through a real sudo, and the client as root at all (no test runs as root; the pieces -- the held login, `/running`, `/switch`, the switch of the file identity -- are tested apart). (6) **Corrected by D249:** as built, (3) did not hold for the full-screen client -- it asked the admin password at each database feature, because it was given the role `calu_admin` without a password while it held the owner login. Since D249 it asks once per run and keeps `calu_admin` (or the owner login after a blank admin password), and each action narrows to `calu_operator` or `calu_admin`. |
| D215 | **A root session also listens on a root-owned Unix socket, and a root client on it needs no token** (2026-11-13, the owner's requirement; `calu.localsocket`). (1) **Where.** `/run/calu/sessions/<session row id>.sock`, the socket with mode 0600 and its directory with 0700, both root's; created by every session whose process is root, next to the TCP port, which stays as it is with its tokens. (2) **Who is trusted.** The session accepts a connection of that socket without a token only when the kernel reports the peer as uid 0 (`SO_PEERCRED`); any other peer there has to show a token like a TCP client. A trusted client has no token that could be revoked, and a decision it makes is recorded without one. (3) **What the client verifies** before it sends anything (`localsocket.problems`, without following a link at the socket's name): the path is a socket, it and its directory belong to root, and neither grants anything to a group or to others. With the directory closed, nothing above it can be used to reach or replace the socket. A client that is root uses the socket when that check passes (`cli._local_endpoint`) and mints no token; otherwise it uses the address and a token as before. (4) **Root's file access after the switch.** A session with `--user` and a client with `--sudo` do their own file access as the user (D211), so binding, checking, connecting and removing run through `asuser.as_root`: one thread that takes root's file identity for that call. The event loop is told not to clean the socket file up itself (`cleanup_socket=False`), because it would look at it with the user's access; found when the first run as root failed on exactly that. (5) **Run as root in a container** (`spikes/spike_root_socket.py`): socket `0:0 0600`, directory `0:0 0700`, the process's own access refused, a connection with root's access accepted and reported as uid 0; and a whole session with `--user 1000` runs with the socket served (`spikes/smoke_privileged.sh`). Not run: a root client attaching over it to a real session. |
| D216 | **The web interface: a login with the admin password, and `calu web --sudo`** (2026-11-13, agreed with the owner). (1) **Login.** `TokenGate` (D161) keeps what it does after a login -- an HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict cookie for reading and a per-origin key for everything that changes something -- and gets a second way in: a browser without a login is answered with a page that asks for the admin password and posts it to `/_login`. The password is checked by logging in as `calu_admin` at the database the interface serves (`setup.is_admin_password`) and is kept nowhere. One password is checked at a time, and after a wrong one the answer waits `min(8, 2^n / 2)` seconds, n counting wrong ones in a row. The page loads no other file, since a browser without a login is served none; the place to go on to is kept on this host. (2) **Which login is offered.** `calu web` asks the database whether `calu_admin` may log in: if so it prints the plain address and the password page is the login; if not (no `calu setup` since migration 0033, or an `admin` entry without a password) it prints the address with the token as before. The token keeps working in both cases. (3) **`calu web --sudo`** (and `--user` as root): the pattern of D214. The login is read from `/etc/calu`, stays in the process's memory, and the process then touches files as the user; nothing Claude Code runs as the user can read it out. (4) **Not built:** the web interface's live view attaching over a session's root-owned socket (it mints a token, as before); a logout; and a limit on how long a browser login lasts. Not run: `calu web --sudo` through a real sudo, and the password page in a real browser (the gate is tested through the application in-process). |
| D217 | **The honesty checker answers HONEST or DISHONEST per file, not pass or deny** (2026-11-13, the owner's requirement 28; **built the same day**: `checkers/model.HonestyAnswer`, `Checker.judges_honesty`, `defaults/checkers/system/honesty.md`, `tests/unit/test_honesty_checker.py`). As built: the checker type named `honesty` gets the answer form of its own and a system part of its own instead of the route's; a DISHONEST file opens marks on its dishonest units (or on the file), an HONEST file closes every open mark of that file (a clean mark with the symbol `*`), and the step is never stopped by a verdict; `escalate` alone becomes an escalation. A DISHONEST verdict without a reason is asked for again. The verdicts are written into the check's record (`reason`) and the DISHONEST ones into the remark Claude reads with the approval (`comment`), which is also what the clients show. Not changed: the event and the stored row of a check still name its decision as PASS, so a client shows the badge PASS beside the text DISHONEST; the other change checkers (command, edit, changeset) still record marks in the common answer; `calu evals` still scores the honesty checker against cases that expect PASS, DENY or ESCALATE, where it can now only answer PASS or ESCALATE. Run once against a real model (Nemotron Lightning on Fireworks): an edit that left a docstring promising a sorted list over code that no longer sorts was answered DISHONEST for the file, with the function and the reason; the following edit that corrected the docstring was answered HONEST. The design as written before the build: Today the honesty checker is one more checker on the edit route with the common answer (`CheckDecision`: PASS, DENY, ESCALATE, plus `marks_dirty` and `marks_clean`), and its instructions let it deny dishonesty an edit could have avoided. The requirement makes its answer a judgement about files. Design: (1) an answer type of its own, `HonestyAnswer`: for each file the step touches a verdict HONEST or DISHONEST, for DISHONEST a short reason, and a list of units (function, method, class) with whether each is now honest or dishonest and why; and separately `escalate` with its reason, for what looks like an intentional backdoor or malicious behaviour. (2) It never stops a step by itself: DISHONEST records marks (the named units, or the file when none is named) and the step goes on, because honesty may be lost for the moment; HONEST clears the marks of that file. Running code and committing stay governed by the honesty state and `honesty.mode` (D196), which is where a DISHONEST answer has its effect. Only `escalate` stops, as an escalation. (3) Inside calu the answer is converted to what the gate and the honesty state already take (marks to open, marks to close, PASS or ESCALATE), so D196's state, the review and the enforcement stay as they are; the reviewer of the honesty review (`honesty_review.FileVerdict`) already judges per file and is aligned in wording. (4) To change with it: the checker's instructions (`defaults/checkers/honesty.md`, `system/marks.md`), the fake models of the tests, the eval cases that expect PASS or DENY from it, and what the clients show for its run: HONEST or DISHONEST per file instead of PASS, which needs the verdicts in the `check.result` event and in `checker_runs`. |
| D218 | **The live view of the web interface: Markdown, a gap between turns, thumbnails of images Claude reads** (2026-11-13, the owner's request). (1) Claude's text is rendered from Markdown by `static/markdown.js`, a small renderer of calu's own (headings, paragraphs, fenced and inline code, bold, italic, links to http, https and mailto only, lists, block quotes, rules, simple tables): it builds DOM nodes and never sets HTML from text, so what Claude writes cannot become markup or script of the page, and it needs no library and no build step. A streamed message is rendered again from its whole source with every delta. (2) The line that ends a turn is followed by a gap. (3) A `Read` tool call that names a file ending in png, jpg, jpeg, gif or webp gets the image small under it, a click opens it; the image comes from a new route, `/sessions/<id>/file?path=`, which serves only image files inside the session's project directory (symbolic links resolved), at most 10 MB, and only for a session of the machine the web interface runs on. It shows the file as it is now, not as Claude read it. (4) Not covered: an image a user attaches to a message (calu's chat carries text only), an image outside the project directory, and images in the recorded history page. (5) The owner asked whether the "fork before this turn" buttons work: the buttons are real (D208 part 10), but the link of that name on a turn only jumped to a collapsed section and did not open it; a link to a collapsed section now opens it (`calu.js`). The button needs a running `calu serve` and says so. |
| D219 | **A fork says where it comes from; the escalation card of the full-screen client has buttons** (2026-11-13, from the owner's use). (1) **Fork origin.** A fork runs under its own Claude session id, and nothing recorded that it continues a copy: its page listed turns it never ran without a word about them, and the conversation it left gave no way to find it. Migration 0034 adds `sessions.forked_from` (the Claude session id that was copied) and `forked_before_turn`; the session records them when it starts as a fork. The web interface's history and live pages of a fork say that it is one, of which conversation (with a link), and what it took over; the page of the conversation lists its forks. What the owner saw (a fork whose history calu did not show) was not reproduced: a fork through `calu serve` stores the turns it inherits, and the page shows them. Possible causes that remain: the conversation was run before the database was replaced (then calu has only what Claude Code's transcript says, D209), or its last turn had not ended. (2) **The fork control per turn** was a link that scrolled to a collapsed section holding the button. It is now one button on the turn, labelled with what it does ("new session that keeps turns 1 to N-1 and drops turn N and everything after it"); the terminal command and the note about the files are in the section below it. (3) **Mouse in the full-screen client.** The card of an open escalation gets a row of buttons under it (`widgets/chrome.Buttons`): Allow, Deny, Deny with a note, Show everything; for a question its options, own answer, send and decline. A click does what the key does, including the two steps of allow and deny: the first click arms and relabels the button ("click again to allow"), the second carries it out, so a stray click decides nothing. The row costs one line of the transcript. Already clickable before: agent tabs, the side panel's entries, a step in the transcript, the marker for new output. **The rest, added the same day:** every line of key hints is clickable -- the footer of the main screen and the key lines of the detail, diff, text, checkers, confirmation and password screens (`widgets/chrome.KeyLine`). A click on a hint presses the key the hint names, read from the text shown (`key_at`, `key_of`: `a,a` is `a`, `esc` is `escape`, `^p` is `ctrl+p`), so a screen only has to show its hints there and nothing has to be kept in step with the keys. A click on a line of the command list completes that command, and a click on a checker in the checkers screen selects it. Text input stays text input: the composer, the note, the editor and the password field take the mouse as Textual's own widgets do. Not covered: dragging to select text in the transcript for copying (the terminal's own selection, with shift held, does that). (4) **Asked for, not built: images in the chat.** A user cannot attach an image to a message yet; it needs the protocol's `chat.send` and `user.input` to carry images, the runner to send them to Claude Code, and the clients to offer and show them. |
| D220 | **A chat message can carry images, and the web interface attaches and shows them** (2026-11-13, the owner's request). (1) **Protocol.** `chat.send` takes `images`: at most 4, each a PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP of at most 5 MB as base64 (`ChatImage`; no SVG, which is a document that can carry scripts). `user.input` carries the images of the message it reports. Protocol messages may now be 32 MB (`MAX_FRAME_BYTES`) on the session's sockets, in the clients and in `calu web`. (2) **To Claude Code** the message goes as a text block followed by image blocks. Measured with the real CLI (`spikes/spike_chat_image.py`): Claude sees the image, and the replay of the message holds the text block only, which is what the runner matches to report the user's input; it adds the images it kept for that text. An image goes to the main agent only: a message to a subagent is relayed as text. (3) **The checkers do not see images.** They judge by the user's words, so the user's input in their context gets a line that says how many images were attached and that calu does not show them. An instruction that exists only inside an image is therefore unknown to the checkers; a step that rests on it will look unasked for to them. (4) **Web interface.** The live view has a file input and takes a pasted image; the waiting images are shown small with a way to remove each; a sent image appears under the user's message and a click enlarges it in place (a browser does not open a `data:` address in a new tab). The content policy allows `data:` for images. (5) **Not covered:** images are not stored in the database, so the recorded history page and a re-attached client after the session's replay buffer has moved on do not show them; the full-screen and line clients cannot attach one and show nothing for one; the events with images stay in the session's replay buffer at their full size. |
| D221 | **`calu acp` was driven by a real ACP client, and what that found was fixed** (2026-11-16, the owner's request after the adapter did not work for him in PyCharm). (1) **The test.** `spikes/acp_host.py` starts the adapter as an editor does and speaks to it with the client side of the official `agent-client-protocol` package (1.0.0), which checks every message of the agent against the protocol's schema; the session behind it was a real one (Claude Code on Haiku, fake checkers). Conversation: initialize, session/new, a prompt with a command, a prompt whose command escalates (answered in the client), a prompt cancelled mid-command, session/set_mode, session/list, a prompt with an image, session/load, a prompt after the load. (2) **What worked as it was:** every message passed the schema; text, thinking, tool calls and their results streamed; the escalation arrived as a permission request and the answer continued the turn; `session/cancel` interrupted a running command and the prompt answered `cancelled`; a mode change and the replay of the conversation by session/load. (3) **Found and fixed:** the permission request named its tool call by id only, without a title, so a client that draws the request alone asks a question without a subject; it now carries the title, the kind, the status and the input. `session/list` was not implemented, and PyCharm's log shows it asking once a minute and setting the agent aside ("cannot list in any workspace"); the adapter now offers it and lists the one session it serves, for that session's directory. Images of a prompt were replaced by a note; they are now sent with the chat message (D220), and the adapter says so in its capabilities. A directory that the editor names the way Windows sees a WSL directory (`//wsl.localhost/Ubuntu/...`, PyCharm's workspace id in the log has that form) is read as the path inside WSL. (4) **Why it did not work for the owner**, as far as his log shows: on 2026-11-12 the adapter started in PyCharm, answered initialize and session/new and served until the session ended the next day; since then the start script names the address and the token of that ended session, and the listing failed every minute. Not verified in PyCharm itself. (5) **Not covered:** a second ACP session per adapter, resuming a stored conversation through ACP (`session/resume`, and `session/load` of another id than the running session's), audio, and the client's file system and terminal (refused on purpose, §6.1). |
| D222 | **What a ten-area review found, and what was changed for it** (2026-11-17; ten read-only reviewers, then eight fixing agents on disjoint files, then the full suite). **Changed:** (1) programs calu starts in the project as the user (git, the search child, `claude` of a `claude:` checker, `doctor`'s calls) get no provider key: `asuser.CHILD_KEEP` no longer keeps `ANTHROPIC_*`/`CLAUDE_*`, and every name in `PROVIDER_KEYS` is dropped -- a `claude:` checker under `--user` therefore has to log in through the user's `~/.claude`, not through environment keys; (2) `calu evals` runs the honesty row as the honesty checker and scores it by its marks (expected DENY = a dirty mark); (3) the marks of all checkers of one step are applied clean first, then dirty, so one checker's HONEST cannot close what another opened in the same step; the honesty checker no longer vetoes an ALLOW, and its answer is not read for `executes_project_code`; (4) a clock jump is printed once (the watch logs at debug level); (5) images are paired with their message per text in order and the backlog is bounded; the replay buffer keeps image data only for the newest 20 image events; a connection must send `initialize` within 10 s and a message over 64K characters before it closes the connection; a root client's decision is recorded with a `root:` client name; (6) `calu serve` stops a session whose start it could not report, and a request claimed longer than 180 s can be failed by the requester (`calu_expire_session_request`, migration 0035); (7) migration 0035 also gives `calu_session` UPDATE only on the columns its store writes, takes UPDATE and DELETE on `audit_log` from `calu_admin`, and puts `pg_temp` last in one search path; a later column the session writes must be granted explicitly; (8) setup: a setup of a new database drops a `session` entry of another one; the local database guard reads `docker inspect` as JSON (a data directory may contain spaces); `calu_admin` leaves `rds_iam`; an `rds-iam` record must use `verify-full`; options that do not apply are refused; `calu credential set` checks the login it stores; (9) command line: cheap checks before the re-execution through sudo, `--user/--from-sudo` placed before `--`, `--prompt` with `--prompt-file` and an empty prompt file refused, `calu start` withdraws its request on Ctrl-C and never prints an empty session id, `--no-attach` refuses `--plain`/`--thinking`, `calu web --port` checked; (10) full-screen client: `/switch` only to sessions `calu attach` can reach, the card's buttons wrap and every option of a question has one, a click on a card that changed meanwhile does nothing, a message in a line of hints is not clickable, `/new` and `/fork` carry project, model and effort and name the user calu works for; (11) web: the wait after wrong passwords decays after a quiet minute, a database failure does not count, the wait is not taken under the lock (a guesser in parallel is limited by the database's check only); the password page's style is allowed by a hash in the content policy; the Markdown renderer is no longer quadratic on a line of `[`; an old schema gives a page that says to run `calu setup`; the web buttons carry the project; (12) ACP: `session/load` accepts only the running session's id, `session/close` detaches and refuses an unknown id, `session/list` answers an empty list when the session cannot be asked. **Not changed, by the owner's decision:** any holder of the session login may claim any host's and user's session request, prompt included -- intended. **Left open:** browser tests for the styled password page and pathological Markdown; Ctrl-C mid-turn under `calu serve`; the root-only paths still have no automated test. |
| D223 | **`calu login`: Claude Code's own login, for the user it runs as in sessions** (2026-11-18, the owner's request). Claude Code keeps its login below the home of the user it runs as, so under `--user` that user must be logged in. `calu login [claudeai|console|sso]`, `--status`, `--logout` run `claude auth login|status|logout` as that user: directly as calu's own user, and for another user through calu's launcher with that user's ids and home (the user's home for a name, `/var/lib/calu/claude/<uid>` for a bare uid, created by `asuser.prepare`), exactly as a session starts Claude Code. `--sudo` and `--user` behave as for `calu run`; as root a user must be named. The login flow is Claude Code's: calu asks for nothing and stores nothing. Run as root in a container for uid 1000: the state directory was created for that uid and `--status` reported "not logged in". Not run: an actual sign-in (it needs a browser and an account). |
| D224 | **Three rulings of the owner on 2026-11-18** (requirements 28 and 29). (1) **A `claude:` checker under `--user`** runs as that user with an environment built for the user (`asuser.child`: that user's `HOME`, no variable of root's environment, no provider key), so it authenticates with the user's own Claude Code login -- the one `calu login --sudo` or `calu login --user ...` creates. That is what the build of D222 already does; nothing changed in the code. An API key in root's environment is not passed on. (2) **Parallel web login attempts are not capped**: the trade-off recorded in D222 is accepted. (3) **Honesty is judged by the honesty checker alone** (decided 2026-11-18, **built the same day** with requirement 38; what is built follows this entry's text): the other checkers (command, edit, changeset, user-defined) no longer get the honesty rules or the instructions to record marks, and marks they write are ignored. They keep what they need of the honesty state: not to rely on the names and documentation of code the state lists, and to say whether a step executes project code (`executes_project_code`, read by the gate in enforce mode). With `honesty.mode` on and the honesty checker disabled, nothing judges honesty per step; a start-up warning is to say so, and only the honesty review and trust decide. As built: `defaults/instructions.compose_instructions` gives the honesty rules to the honesty checker only; `system/marks.md` is gone, and the checkers of the command, edit and changeset routes get the three sentences of `system/honesty-state.md` while `honesty.mode` is on (`executes_project_code` is asked in the command checker's own system part, always). The other checkers' models answer with `checkers/model.StepVerdict`, which has no mark fields at all; `CheckDecision` adds them and they are filled only by `HonestyAnswer.decision`. `Session._keep_honesty`, `_mark_at_once` and the review of a write set read marks only from a run of the honesty checker (`Checker.judges_honesty`). One consequence beyond the list: an edit verifies its file (source `check`) only if the honesty checker judged it, so with the checker disabled every edited file stays unreviewed until a review or a person verifies it; `preflight.honesty_warnings` says so at the start. The tests make marks with `testing.honesty_answering` (the `HonestyAnswer` form); `testing.marking_checker` remains for `executes_project_code` and for the test that another checker's marks are ignored. `calu evals` measures the honesty checker on cases of its own kind (`honesty`: HONEST, DISHONEST or ESCALATE per edit, with the units) and the honesty reviewer on batches (`honesty-review`), with precision and recall per class; results in `agent/records/notes/honesty-evals-2026-11-18.md`. (4) **The honesty checker is no pass/deny checker, and its result is not one label** (the owner's correction, 2026-11-18): its job is different from the checkers that answer PASS/ALLOW/DENY/ESCALATE. It may escalate, and it assigns a status to each file (and to units in it) -- a structured result of its own, unlike the checkers' much simpler answer. It might also keep that state through tool calls (for example `mark_dishonest` / `mark_honest` tools) instead of returning it as structured output. Consequences: its run is not to be shown or stored as a single PASS/ESCALATE label; clients show the per-file statuses (and an escalation when it escalates); the conversion of its answer into a `CheckDecision` (`HonestyAnswer.decision`) is an internal adapter for the gate and the honesty state, not its result. Built 2026-11-18: `CheckOutcome.honesty` keeps the checker's own answer, the `check.result` event carries it as `honesty` (a list of `HonestyFileStatus`: path, verdict, reason, units) and `honesty_escalation` (its reason when it asks for a person), and the combined `reason` of a step no longer contains the honesty checker's verdict lines unless it escalates. Not built: the clients still render only the decision and the comment (the DISHONEST lines reach the chat through the comment); the `checker_runs` row of the honesty checker still stores the adapter's decision PASS or ESCALATE with the verdict lines as its reason. Whether it answers in structured output or through marking tools is open. |
| D225 | **`calu run` and `calu serve` print a status, never the conversation; tracebacks go to a log file** (2026-11-18, requirement 30). (1) **The terminal** shows the events in `calu.run.STATUS_EVENTS`: escalations and their outcome, what waits for a human, usage limits, `log` events and the end of the session (`stopped`, `failed`), plus the lines about the session's life (project, ids, address). Not printed: the conversation (the user's input, Claude's text and thinking, tool calls, their results, the checkers' verdicts) and, after the owner's second message the same day, the course of the work (the other states such as `[idle]`, the end of each turn, the honesty state); the clients (`calu attach`, the web live view, ACP) show them. `calu run --thinking` is gone. `calu run` without `--batch` still reads messages from stdin and says that `calu attach` shows the answers. (2) **The log file** is `$XDG_STATE_HOME/calu/log/<command>.log` (`~/.local/state/calu/log/run.log`; as root `/var/lib/calu/log/serve.log`), directory 0700, file 0600, inside calu's state directory that Claude Code's sandbox may not read. It gets every record at INFO and above with its traceback. It is opened before the process switches its file access to the session's user and stays open, so a session under `--user` still writes to a file only root can read. calu never rotates or truncates it (not decided: rotation that works after the switch). (3) **stderr** gets one line per warning or error: the message, and for a record with an exception the exception in one line and the log file's path. An error `calu run` or `calu serve` ends with is logged with its traceback and printed as one line; Ctrl-C prints one line and exits 130. (4) Tests read the conversation through `run_session(..., on_event=...)`. |
| D226 | **Local root clients need neither a token nor a password; `--secure` turns that off** (2026-11-18, requirement 31). (1) **Who is trusted.** A connection is let in without a token only where the kernel tells the client apart from Claude Code: root on the session's root-owned socket (D215). A session that does not run as root runs Claude Code as the same uid as calu, so every process Claude Code starts would pass a check of the peer's uid; the owner chose that such a session keeps asking every client for a token, also on a Unix socket. (2) **Finding the session without the database.** A root client without an address (`calu attach --sudo`, also `/switch` and `calu start --sudo`) first lists `/run/calu/sessions/*.sock` that are root's alone (`localsocket.listed`), asks each one who it is (`protocol.client.probe`: `initialize` only, no subscription) and picks the session by `--session` or by the nearest project directory, as `_pick_session` does; only when none matches does it ask the database. Found that way it reads no password-protected credential: it keeps a login of root's store only if one needs no password (`admin`, else `operator`); without one it attaches anyway and says that `/running`, `/switch`, `/new`, `/fork` and saving settings need one (`sudo calu credential set operator --no-password`). (3) **`calu web --sudo`** relays the live view through the session's root-owned socket without minting a token. The browser's login with the admin password stays: it is a network connection. (4) **`--secure`** on `calu run` and `calu serve`: no root-owned socket is bound, so every client, root's included, shows a token. Not together with `--insecure`. (5) **Not covered:** an automated test as real root; the sockets were tested with the tests' own uid standing for root. |
| D227 | **`--insecure`: clients on this machine need no token** (2026-11-18, requirement 32). (1) On `calu run` and `calu serve`: every connection on a Unix socket of the session (the `--socket` socket, root's socket, and the socket below) and every TCP connection from a loopback address (127.0.0.0/8, ::1, also IPv4-mapped) is let in without a token (`SessionServer(insecure=True)`). The session prints a line saying so at its start. (2) **What it costs**, as the help text says: any process on the machine that can reach the port, and any process of the socket's owner, Claude Code's own commands included, can act as a client, also decide escalations. It is the owner's choice per run; the default stays as before. A decision of such a client is recorded with the client name `local:<name>`. (3) A session that does not run as root also binds `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/calu/sessions/<session id>.sock` (directories 0700, socket 0600; without the variable `<tmp>/calu-<uid>/sessions`), where `calu attach` of the same user finds it without the database (the search of D226 (2), for the user's own directory; a session that wants a token refuses the probe and is skipped). (4) `calu attach ADDRESS` without `--token` now tries without one; a session that is not `--insecure` refuses, and the message says to pass `--token=<token>`. (5) `calu web` is not changed: its browser login is its own gate. |
| D228 | **The place file of root's database, and a setup without passwords** (2026-11-18, requirement 33; after the owner could not save checker instructions from the full-screen client). (1) **The defect.** A client that finds its session through a local socket (D226, D227) asks no database, so it no longer learned which database the session uses; the full-screen client fell back to the `admin` entry of the user's own store, which on the owner's machine names another, older database. (2) **The place file.** `calu setup` as root writes `/var/lib/calu/database.json` (`calu.db.place`): host, port, database name and how the server is verified, no secret; mode 0644 in a directory only root writes. A client reads it when it attached to a session of root's calu through a socket: it runs as root for a user, or the kernel reports uid 0 as the listener of the socket (`cli._server_uid`). It checks that the file and its directory are root's and not writable by others, and logs in there as `calu_admin` with the admin password a person types. Without the file the client says so and offers no database features (`StoreAdmin(unavailable=...)`); it never falls back to the user's own store. The session is not asked for its database: a socket in a user's runtime directory can be made by any program of that user, and the client would send the admin password wherever such a "session" pointed. (3) **`calu setup --no-password`**: asks for no admin and no session password. The entries `admin` and `session` are stored protected by the store file's mode alone; the role `calu_admin` gets a generated password (always, see D230), and its login is stored as the entry `calu_admin`, also without a password. An existing setup is converted: the old passwords are asked once. Commands that change settings (`_admin_secret`), root clients (`_hold_password_free`, `_herd_value`) and `calu web` use that entry first and ask nothing; `calu web` then prints the address with its token, because nobody knows a password a browser could be asked for. (4) **Who can read it.** As root the store is `/etc/calu` (0700 root): only root, so `calu run --sudo`, `calu serve --sudo`, `calu attach --sudo` and `calu web --sudo` ask for nothing, and a client that is not root still has to type a password it does not have, i.e. cannot change settings. In a user's own account every program of that user can read the logins, Claude Code's commands included; setup prints that as a warning. These are the owner's two options ("both") by one mechanism: the first option was described to him as a root-only Unix socket of the database with a password-free `calu_admin`, and was built as a stored login instead, which gives root clients the same and needs no change to the database container, its pg_hba.conf or its mounts, and works for the server and RDS variants too. The second, a password-free login for every local process, is `--no-password` in one's own account. (4a) **The session entry without a password** is accepted by `calu run` and `calu serve` only from a store that root alone can read and write (`cli._only_root_writes`: the process is root, the file is root's with nothing for group or others, its directory is root's and not writable by others). Elsewhere the refusal of D115 stays, because whatever can write the store could point sessions at another database; `calu setup --no-password` there keeps asking for the session password and says why. (Found when the owner's first `calu run --sudo` after the conversion was refused.) (4b) **With `--no-password`, every client changes settings without a password** (the owner's ruling the same day: "When I use --no-password, security is weak. This should be allowed."). As built at first, a client that is not root was asked for a password nobody knew. Now `sudo calu setup --no-password` also writes the generated password of `calu_admin` into the place file (`admin_password`), which every user and every program of the machine can read; a client takes it from there (`place.read`, `cli._with_shared_password` when it knows the database another way) and asks for nothing. PostgreSQL accepts no empty password, so the role keeps a generated one. Setup prints a warning that names the file and says that nothing but the checkers then keeps a program from changing the checkers. A later `sudo calu setup` without the option gives the role the typed admin password and writes the file without a secret. This replaces what (4) says about a client that is not root. (4c) **Every command uses the shared login** after such a setup: the lookup of `calu attach`, `calu acp` and `calu start`, the fleet commands (`calu sessions`, `calu resolve`, ...) and the commands that change settings take the login of the place file before any entry of the user's own store (`cli._shared_admin`), so nothing asks a password and the user's own store, which may name another database, is not consulted. `CALU_PLACE_FILE` names another place file; calu's tests set it for every process they start, after a test's `calu attach` process read the real file of the owner's machine and listed the sessions of his live database (read only, 2026-11-18). (4d) **`calu web` without `--sudo`** after such a setup also takes the shared login and asks no password in the terminal; the browser is not asked for the admin password, which nobody knows, and the printed address carries the token (opened once per browser), as `calu web --sudo` does. (5) **The database's own socket**: the local container listens on TCP 127.0.0.1:<port> only (default 5433), published by Docker; its Unix socket exists only inside the container. (6) **Not covered:** `calu doctor` still reports a password-free entry without looking at who can read the store; no run as real root. |
