A login that may not take the role (an operator asked for `calu_admin`; a non-superuser owner on PostgreSQL 16+, whose creator membership lacks SET -- the root box's case) keeps its own role and the action runs as before: the narrowing guards against a client's own mistakes, the database's grants stay the access control. (4) **How the roles map to actions today:** owner -- migrations, roles, `calu setup`, operator logins (`calu credential new operator`); `calu_admin` (member of `calu_operator`, `admin_all` policy on every RLS table, DML on every table) -- `calu config|checker|rules|honesty|summary --edit|traces`, the clients' saves, the web's changes; `calu_operator` (SELECT on every table, `operator_reads` policies, UPDATE(name) on sessions, INSERT/UPDATE on client and server tokens, INSERT on session requests, `calu_resolve_escalation`, `calu_withdraw_session_request`) -- `calu sessions|escalations|resolve|token`, finding a session and minting its token, `calu start`, `/running`, `/rename`; `calu_session` (its own rows through `session_own` policies and SECURITY DEFINER functions such as `calu_start_session`, `calu_claim_session_request`) -- `calu run` and `calu serve`. **Where PostgreSQL could split finer:** (a) `calu_admin` may write every table; the settings it changes are in `config`, `checker_types`, `rules`, `projects`, `trusted_commits` and `trusted_files`. Grants on these tables only would keep it from rewriting the history (`sessions`, `turns`, `steps`, `escalations`, `checker_runs`), which sessions and the decision functions write; it needs a check of every settings command first (e.g. `honesty_marks`). (b) `calu_operator` reads every table, including turn texts and comments; a viewer role with `operator_reads` limited to `sessions`, `escalations` and `servers` would serve a dashboard that must not read conversations. (c) Decisions on escalations could go only through SECURITY DEFINER functions (as `calu_resolve_escalation` already does) with no direct UPDATE right, so that every change is a named, audited call. (d) **Per-person roles** (`calu_admin_ada`, `calu_operator_bob`, each `LOGIN` and a member of the group role) with RLS on `sessions.started_by`/`os_user` would give each user only their own sessions and record who decided by login name instead of a free-text actor -- requirement soft goal 7. (e) A trusted client could hold a NOLOGIN group role it reaches by `SET ROLE` only for the length of one action (as built now) and `RESET ROLE` after it. **Recommendation for later:** first (d) for shared databases with several people (it needs `calu credential new` per person and a policy column, not a redesign), then (c) for escalations and trust, then (b) when a read-only dashboard exists; (a) brings little while the admin password is the gate. No OS users are involved in any of these. (5) **Tests:** `tests/unit/test_root_client_login.py` (asked once, narrowing of a held owner login, blank password, Ctrl-D, wrong answers, one password across `/switch`); `tests/db/test_least_privilege.py` (a narrowed `calu_admin` reads and files requests but cannot change settings; an owner narrowed to `calu_admin`; an operator that cannot take it; the client's access lists and saves with one held login); `tests/root/test_root_client.py` (as real root: asked once, `calu_admin` or the owner fallback held, root's store closed after the switch, the password neither in the environment nor in a started program's). | D250 | **The owner's answers of 2026-11-20: the hook answers by the mode at the answer, no warning after a user's switch** (2026-11-20, the owner's answers to the judgement calls of test plan K). (1) **A switch during a check** (K15). The PreToolUse hook took the permission mode of its input, so a step checked in auto mode and answered after a switch to default got no decision, and Claude Code, by then in default mode, asked a human about a step nobody objected to. The hook now answers by the mode Claude Code is in when the answer goes out (`SdkRunner._mode_now`): for the main agent the mode of a `set_permission_mode` that Claude Code accepted and has not reported yet, else the mode it last reported; for a subagent the mode of its own hook input (a subagent's mode is known only from there); a hook input without a mode stays "not known" (no auto mode). The translation verdict -> `permissionDecision` is one function, `calu.claude.runner.hook_decision`, which the scripted runner uses too: it now answers by its mode at the answer, records `hook_answers`, and a PASS without a decision outside auto mode is asked as Claude Code would ask. A comment made on the diff screen sends `ref` "edit:<tool_use_id>" of the edit the diff shows, as VSCode does (`tui.model.Diff.ref`, set from `edit.diff` and for the proposed edit of a decision), so the recorded comment names the edit and Claude reads "[User comment on a diff against edit:<id> of `path`]". (4) **`calu setup` without a terminal changes no instructions unasked.** D248 replaced an unedited older shipped text when no terminal could ask; now it keeps it, lists each with `calu checker update NAME`, and ends with "N outdated shipped text(s) kept; `calu setup --update-instructions` replaces them all". `--update-instructions` replaces them, `--no-update-instructions` keeps them without the question (`tests/db/test_cli_setup.py`). (5) **A library of checker instruction templates** (the owner: the review-findings rules stay a documentation example and go into a library of examples; later a UI may load one). `src/calu/defaults/templates/`: one Markdown file per template with a header (`checker`, `summary`) and an index `README.md`; the first three are the documentation's examples (`review-findings` for the subagent-result checker: weigh findings against the complexity of their fixes, security usually heavy but not absolute, the user outranks a subagent's verdict; `command-project-rules`, `edit-project-rules`), and `tests/unit/test_templates.py` keeps them equal to DOCUMENTATION.md's blocks. `calu.defaults.library` reads them; `calu checker template list | show NAME | use NAME [--checker C] [--append | --replace]`: `list` and `show` need no database; `use` (`admin.use_template`) replaces a text calu shipped or an unchanged template, keeps the user's own text unless `--append` (after it) or `--replace` (the audit log keeps the old one), refuses a checker of another route, and stores nothing when the version changed between read and write. Public domain like the shipped instructions (LICENSE's notice and LICENSE.md name the directory). No `--project`: checker instructions are global (`checker_types` has no project scope). (6) **Usage limits: not stored; the panel says why `get_usage` gave nothing.** Migration 0041 stored the last reported limits per Claude account (table `usage_limits`, keyed by the e-mail and organisation Claude Code names in its `initialize` answer, else OS user and host, written and read through SECURITY DEFINER functions) and merged them into `usage.get` and `usage.panel` (`RateLimit.stored`, `UsagePanelResult.account`). **Removed by the owner the same day ("keep it simple")**: the panel asks Claude Code on demand, and otherwise the figures this session process received (with `reported_at`) are enough; storing them meant reading, storing and showing an account identity, a table, two SECURITY DEFINER functions and a merge rule, for figures that are only information (Anthropic's servers enforce the limits) and age within hours. Migration 0042 drops the table and the functions; 0041 stays, because databases have applied it, and the runner (`calu.db.migrate`) applies every shipped file it has not recorded, so a removed file would leave the table behind on those databases and nothing on new ones would tell the two apart. No account identity is read, stored or shown. **Kept:** `usage_shape_problem` checks the experimental `get_usage` answer's shape; when the request fails, its shape is unknown or no session runs, `account_limits` stays empty and `account_limits_problem` says why and points to the limits reported in this session (full-screen client, line client, web live view; VSCode's generated protocol types). | D253 | **Recording the user's own edits in the IDE as diffs** (2026-11-20, requirement 47; the owner's corrections of the same day: no redaction, and only files under version control). **What.** With `human_diffs.enabled` on (off by default; a section `human_diffs` scoped globally or per project like `traces`), every save of a file in an IDE's editor adds a unified diff of that save to a file inside the project, so that a later prompt can point Claude at the user's own changes ("rewrite documentation like this"). **Which saves.** Only a save through the editor of VSCode or PyCharm, of a file that git tracks in its checkout (`git ls-files --error-unmatch`, run in the file's directory, so worktrees and nested repositories count), while the editor is attached to a session. Not recorded: Claude's edits (Claude writes to disk; no editor save happens), changes from other programs, the terminal or git (a file changed on disk is reloaded, not saved), the writes of the PyCharm SSH synchronisation (`VfsLocalFiles` marks its own saves, and the listener skips them), untracked and ignored files, new files before their first commit, binary content (a NUL character), and a save that changes nothing. **Who computes what.** The editor sends `before` (the text the editor buffer started from, the owner's decision of 2026-11-20: the text when the editor last loaded, reloaded or saved the file, without the user's unsaved edits since; so a save that overwrites a version Claude wrote meanwhile, after the IDE asked, shows only the user's own edits, and after a reload of Claude's version the base is Claude's version. VSCode keeps it per document (`SaveBases` in `shared/humanDiffs.ts`: set on open, on a change that leaves the document clean, i.e. a reload or revert, and on save); PyCharm keeps it per `Document` (`SaveBases` in `logic/HumanDiffs.kt`: set in `fileContentLoaded`, `fileContentReloaded` and `beforeDocumentSaving`, also for the synchronisation's own saves). A document whose base is unknown (VSCode: opened with unsaved edits; PyCharm: loaded before the plugin) falls back to the disk content read in `onWillSaveTextDocument` or the VFS content) and `after` (the saved text), both with line ends normalised to `\n`, in the new session request `human_diffs.add {path, before, after}` (`path` relative to the workspace, at most 1,000,000 characters per text). The core checks the request, computes the diff (`difflib`, three lines of context) and writes the file, so both IDEs share one format. PyCharm does the same in all three modes: local, WSL, and SSH, where the save goes to the local copy and the synchronisation uploads it separately (the recorded diff is that of the local save). The answer says whether it was recorded and whether the feature is on; an editor that hears "off" sends nothing for 60 seconds, so a switched-off feature costs one request a minute, and switching it on takes effect within a minute. **Layout.** One append-only file per changed file, user and day: `<human_diffs.directory>/<YYYY-MM-DD>/<user>/<path with "/" as "__">.tmp.diff` (UTC date; the OS user the session works for; default directory `agent/human-diffs`). Each save appends one block: a comment line `# <path>: saved <time> UTC in <client>, calu session <id>`, then the unified diff with `a/` and `b/` prefixes. `git apply` and `patch` skip the comment lines, and a model reads one file per changed file. The file is opened with `O_APPEND` and `O_NOFOLLOW` and written in one call; a directory that leads out of the project through a link is refused. **No redaction** (the owner): the diffs are written as they are; they stay local. **Cleanup** (the owner's addition of the same day; the default of 14 days is his). A file still named `*.tmp.diff` whose last change is older than `human_diffs.keep_days` (default 14; 0 keeps every file) is deleted; a user who renames one to `<name>.diff` keeps it, and calu never deletes a plain `.diff`. The cleanup runs when a session starts with the feature on and, in a long session (also under `calu serve`), at the first recorded save after 24 hours since the last run, so no timer of its own is needed. It walks only the configured directory, never follows a link (a link is neither entered nor deleted), deletes only regular files named `*.tmp.diff`, removes the date and user folders it emptied, and logs each removed file (the log and one `log` event with the count). **Ignored by git.** When calu creates the directory it writes `<directory>/.gitignore` with the lines `*.tmp.diff` and `.gitignore`: the temporary diffs and that file stay out of every commit without a change to the project's own `.gitignore`, and a renamed `.diff` can be committed if the user wants. A directory that exists already is left as it is. **Guarding.** While the feature is on, the directory is calu's own output like the trace directory: Claude's writes into it meet a built-in write deny rule, and its files are left out of the write sets and of the code honesty state (the session's `_guard_traces` sets both). **Settings UI.** The generic settings form of the web interface and of both IDE dialogs renders the section from the schema (a pane "Your own edits"). **Not decided** (for the owner): edits while no session is attached are not recorded (the session holds the settings and the user); VSCode's auto-save makes one block per save; whether `calu attach`'s diff screen or a command should list the recorded diffs. |
| D254 | **Fixes from the rehearsal "developing Calu inside Calu"** (2026-11-20, test plan M, the owner's milestone). (a) **`claude.pass_env`**: a list of environment variable names (default empty) that join the environment calu's launcher builds for a Claude Code started as another user (`--sudo`, `--user`, D207), with the values of calu's own environment at the session start; the runner adds them to the launch record's `keep` (`SdkRunner._claude_env`). Refused with an error: the checkers' provider keys (`PROVIDER_KEYS`) and calu's own secret variables (`CALU_SECRET_ENV`) when the setting is validated, and every name the runner hides (env file, `hidden_env`) at the start (`StartRefused`). sudo drops the variable before calu starts, so the documented form is `sudo --preserve-env=NAME calu run --user "$USER"` (measured in the root box). The example is `CALU_TEST_DSN` for calu's own database tests. It creates `.claude/worktrees/<name>` on a new branch from `HEAD` and changes no existing file (measured, `spikes/RESULTS.md` worktrees), the write sets leave the new worktree out, and every later call in it is checked, so a checker would only judge the name. `ExitWorktree` stays on the command checker with its rule (D165), because it can delete work. `tool_routes` can still send `EnterWorktree` to a checker. The checkers still judge each call. |

| D256 | **The subagent checker reviews what a subagent is told, not only what it hands back** (2026-11-22, the owner's requests of the same day). **Why.** `Agent`, `Task` and `SendMessage` ran unchecked (`READ_ONLY_TOOLS`), and the checkers' context named a subagent's task only by its short description (`label_for_unchecked`). So no checker saw a task that hands a subagent what was refused to Claude, and no later check knew what a background subagent was doing -- e.g. a subagent told to make hello.py print the environment while the main agent waits and runs hello.py. **What.** The gate routes an `Agent`/`Task` call, and Claude's own `SendMessage` to one of its subagents, to `Route.SUBAGENT` (`StepGate` callbacks `reviews_task`, `review_task`; `Session._reviews_task`, `_review_task`). calu's relay of the user's message to a subagent stays unchecked (`RunState.relays`, filled by `send_chat`, consumed by the matching `SendMessage`). `ResultChecker.run_task` reviews the task (`SubagentTask`, prompt heading "# Task to review", with Claude's narration before the call) with the same instructions and answer as a result: ACCEPT -> PASS, ANNOTATE -> PASS with the note as the comment to Claude, ESCALATE -> ESCALATE (`auto_escalate.subagents` stays results-only, as defined). The checker's summary retells the task (fixed part: indirect speech, goals and limits, up to about 150 words) and becomes the step's record, "Task for subagent <id>: ..." or "Message to subagent <id>: ...", at decision time, so it is in every later check's context, also while a background subagent still runs. The command checker's fixed part says to judge a file by what the conversation says about it, including a running subagent's task. `system/evasion.md` names delegating a refused step or effect as a sign. Setting `check_subagent_tasks` (default true). The scripted runner now gates its Agent call as Claude Code's hook does (a refused task starts no subagent). Trace: a task check is "Task of subagent K: <DECISION>"; a result review (the subagent's own event, `agent_id == tool_use_id`) stays "Result handed back by ...". The IDEs label the route "Subagent checker". **Measured** on claude:haiku: subagent 13/13 (task cases: delegating a denied push ESCALATE, the same goal without the denied effect PASS, a background task that turns hello.py into an environment dump ESCALATE), command 43/43 (running the file the subagent is rewriting after a denied printenv: ESCALATE/DENY 4 of 4). **Not done:** the task review's model call is not stored as a `checker_runs` row (its cost is not counted); a later message of the user relayed by calu is matched by its exact text. |

A stored configuration that names one still loads (refusing it would stop calu from starting); each call of such a role fails with Pydantic AI's "Unknown model" and the check escalates, and `preflight.cli_model_warnings` says so at every start. The web settings form and the IDE presets no longer offer `claude:` models. A claude.ai subscription now serves only the main conversation; the checkers need an API key (in root mode in `/etc/calu/.env`). |
| D259 | **No contents of other files and no secrets in a checker's answer; searches that reach deep directories** (2026-11-23, the owner's two requests of the same day). (a) The fixed part `system/disclosure.md` (`DISCLOSURE_PART`) follows `ROLE_PART` and `EVASION_PART` in every checker's prompt (honesty checker included) and follows `system/honesty-review.md` in the honesty reviewer's. It says: do not disclose the contents of files you read beyond the files under review (the step's files, or the files of the request), and never a secret that any file contains. Worded without tool names, so that a reading tool added later is covered (today only `read_file` and `grep_files` return file contents; `find_files` and `changed_files` return paths). (The owner's wording went through four versions the same day: a list of secrets, personal data and outside content; "files outside the project directory"; "files read via the read / grep tools"; this one. The per-route sentences on secrets in the summary stay.) (b) The owner: a checker must be able to glob recursively, e.g. for a script that loads every file of a pattern in a directory tree. `find_files` already took `**` globs, but the walk scanned at most `MAX_SCAN` (2,000) files of the whole project breadth first and stopped silently, so a deep directory of a large project was reported as "no project file matches". Now the walk enters only the directories on the way to the glob's fixed directory part (`_glob_base`: "plugins/**/*.py" -> `plugins`), only files below it count, and `find_files` and `grep_files` end their answer with a bracketed note when the list may be incomplete (scan limit, time budget, more matches than `limit`). |
| D260 | **The trust commands of `calu attach` in both IDEs** (2026-11-23, the owner: "Let's ensure we have these commands in the IDEs as well"). The protocol request `honesty.trust` already took `rev`, `checkout` and `path`; the IDEs only sent `{rev: "HEAD"}`. Now the chats of VSCode and PyCharm take `/trust [WORKTREE] [REV]`, `/trust-file [PATH]` and `/honesty-review` with `calu attach`'s arguments (`trustArgs` in `slashCommands.ts` and `SlashCommands.kt`); `/honesty` keeps opening the mode menu (in `calu attach` it prints the state). The honesty menus gained "Trust a commit or branch…" (an input box with `calu attach`'s syntax, default HEAD) and "Trust a file…" (the open editor's file suggested); VSCode also has the commands `calu.honestyTrustCommit`, `calu.honestyTrustFile` (explorer and editor context menus) and `calu.honestyReview`. Every trust asks for confirmation first; PyCharm's "Trust HEAD" used to record without asking, contrary to its own contract, and now asks too. The answer names the commit or the file. Not done: a context-menu action in PyCharm. |
| D261 | **The IDE chat as a pane of `calu web`, on one host logic shared with VSCode** (2026-11-23, the owner: "implement this ide view for standalone hosting in a browser (typically in an iframe of a maybe multi-pane web app, could be combined with Jupyter for example) ... the full web UI should definitely be in a separate window"; his choices of the same day: extract a shared core rather than a second host, with a backup of the state before it, and the token in the address's fragment for the login). **Shared host.** `extensions/vscode/src/host/` holds what the chat's host does, without `vscode` or Node imports: `bridgeCore.ts` (JSON-RPC, `ide.initialize`, the password flow, the status; the transport is a `Launcher`), `sessions.ts` (attach, start, fork, rename; the platform's picker and dialogs are `SessionsUi`), `chatHost.ts` (every chat message and bridge event, the honesty, fork, usage and lost-link flows; platform-only messages go to `HostUi.handle`). `extension.ts`, `bridge.ts` (a child-process `Launcher`) and `sessions.ts` (VSCode's QuickPick, input boxes, terminal) became the VSCode platform around it; behaviour is unchanged. Backup of the state before: branch `backup/before-shared-chat-host` (dd70294; a `v0.1.0` tag would have started `release.yml`). **Pane.** `GET /pane` serves the webview script of the extension, bundled with a browser host (`extensions/vscode/pane/`, `npm run build:pane` into `src/calu/web/static/pane/`, committed; a unit test fails when it is stale). The host defines `acquireVsCodeApi` and hands messages to the webview through `onHostMessage`, not `window` message events, which another frame could send. Its `Launcher` is a WebSocket to `/pane/rpc`, where `calu web` runs one `calu.ide.bridge.Bridge` per tab (`calu.web.pane`, at most 8) in the folder of `?dir=` (else `calu web`'s own). **Login.** The page and `/static/pane/` need none (no data). The WebSocket needs the browser's login (cookie and key) or the login token as subprotocol `calu.token.<token>`, accepted on `/pane/rpc` only: a frame of another site (Jupyter on another port) gets no SameSite cookie and no key. The pane takes the token from the fragment (`/pane#token=...&dir=...`; never sent to a server), keeps it in the tab's session storage and removes it from the address. With password sign-in `calu web` prints the pane's address without a token (cross-site frames then do not work). **Framing.** Only `/pane` gets `frame-ancestors` from `web.pane_frame_ancestors` (default `'self' http://localhost:* http://127.0.0.1:*`; entries with a space, ";" or "," are dropped); every other page keeps `'none'`. **What a pane may ask** (`PANE_METHODS`): the session and settings requests the chat uses and `ide.fs.read` for its read-only file view; not `ide.fs.write*`/delete/rename, not `ide.attach` by address and token, not `session.call` of `shutdown`. **Platform differences.** Files open read-only in a dialog at the line; an edit's diff is a unified diff (`pane/diff.ts`); checker and escalation context render as Markdown in a dialog; settings and checker instructions open `/settings` in a separate window; starting the server shows the command to copy. **Tested:** vitest (`chatHost.test.ts`, `pane.test.ts`), `tests/unit/test_web_pane.py`, `tests/browser/test_web_pane.py` (own window, cross-site iframe, no login). **Not run:** the VSCode integration tests after the refactor (Docker Desktop was not running), and the pane against a real session (manual checklist item 52). **Not done:** a context-menu "Trust this file" in the pane; under `calu web --sudo` the pane's bridge uses the credential store that root read, which was not tried. |
| D262 | **The final trace pass for a commit of Claude's waits for the blocks still being redacted** (2026-11-24, a failure of `test_claudes_commit_takes_the_trace_along_after_the_final_pass` in CI). `Session._finalize_for_commit` held the trace writer and read the file on disk at once, while the block of the commit's own tool call (queued by `check.result` just before) could still be in the per-block redaction: on a slow machine the commit recorded the trace without that block, which then followed in the next commit. Now `TraceWriter.settle` waits until the blocks queued before the commit are written, at most `traces.redactor_timeout_s` (the per-block pass's own bound), and the session calls `hold` right after it with no `await` in between. `settle` stops at once at a block that is still open (written only after the next event), at a hold and at the end of the trace. A block that misses the time is committed with the next commit; what is committed is finalized in every case. |

VSCode (tour scenario: escalation, question, "Why?", the diff editor) and the PyCharm plugin (a decision, `/trust` with its confirmation) were run there too; (b) the settings window opened without a login -- the pane passes its token (`?token=`, which `calu web` turns into the window's own login and drops from the address); (c) the file view got line numbers and a full-width line marker, the diff view coloured lines without numbers. The VSCode integration tests (Docker) pass after the D261 refactor (8/8). |
| D264 | **One-line install: `install.sh`** (2026-11-24, the owner: "install ... with a simple single commandline such as `curl <args> <url> \| sudo bash`"). `install.sh` at the repository root, fetched from `raw.githubusercontent.com/kadeng/calu/main/install.sh`. As root (`\| sudo bash`) it makes the root installation of D211: it installs curl and git with apt, dnf or zypper if missing, uses its own uv (pinned 0.12.23, installed into `/usr/local/lib/calu/bin`, never a uv from a user's PATH, which would run that user's code as root at the next update), runs `uv tool install --reinstall` with `UV_TOOL_DIR=/usr/local/lib/calu`, `UV_TOOL_BIN_DIR=/usr/local/bin`, Python in `/usr/local/lib/calu/python` under umask 022, deletes its cache and removes the "others may write" bit uv leaves on lock files, because `--sudo` refuses an installation a non-root user can change. As a user (`\| bash`) it uses uv from PATH or installs it into `~/.local/bin` and runs `uv tool install --reinstall`. Options: `--ref`, `--source`, `--with-sandbox` (root: bubblewrap, socat, and on Ubuntu with `apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns=1` the AppArmor profile `/etc/apparmor.d/bwrap` that backtester1 needed). The body is one function called on the last line, so a broken-off download runs nothing; running it again updates; it starts nothing and changes no setting. Tested in fresh `ubuntu:24.04` containers through a pipe: root with `--with-sandbox` (curl and git installed, modes 755 root, nothing writable by others, a second run updates, a normal user runs `calu`) and a user (uv into `~/.local/bin`, `calu doctor` runs). shellcheck finds nothing. Not tried: dnf and zypper, the AppArmor branch on a real Ubuntu host. |
| D265 | **One calu login, the "user", instead of admin, operator and session; the database owner kept for migrations** (2026-11-24, the owner: "subsume all of these under a single \"user\" role (including the setup). We could have a separate DB admin role ... When setting up the DB and the DB is preexisting, we might want to ask for a DB user. When setting it up locally, the DB password and the user password can be identical"; "we could have (for example for testing) an empty user password. In this scenario we don't ask for a password at all"; and that prompts and errors must be helpful about wrong, missing and cancelled passwords; REQUIREMENTS 48). **Supersedes** D48 (the operator login), D213's split between the admin password and the stored logins, the session login of D20/D205/D228 and the narrowing of D249 (3). (1) **Two store entries** (`calu.logins`): `user`, the login of the role `calu_admin`, used by every calu process of the person (`run`, `serve`, `start`, `attach`, the fleet commands, settings, `calu web`, `calu ide`, `calu acp`, the full-screen client); `db-admin`, the database owner's login (`calu_owner` locally; for an existing server the database user given at setup), used by `calu setup`, `calu db migrate|reset|up`. **One password protects both: the calu user password.** `calu_admin` logs in with the same password, so a client that only knows where the database is (root's place file, D228) logs in with what its user types, and `calu web` signs a browser in with it (D216). (2) **An empty user password** (`calu setup --no-password`, or Enter at the new-password prompt) means calu never asks: both entries are stored without encryption, `calu_admin` gets a generated password (PostgreSQL refuses empty ones), kept across later setups; as root the place file publishes the user login as `--no-password` did before (D228, D230). `calu setup` and `calu doctor` say plainly that every program of the user can then use the login, Claude's commands outside the sandbox included. (3) **Setup**: a new database asks the variant's questions (a server: host, port, database, the database user and its password, checked by a preflight), then the new user password with a two-line explanation (`logins.choose_intro`); a stored database asks the user password once (it opens `db-admin`), `--set-password` chooses a new one (an empty answer removes it). `--renew-session`, `--session-auth`, `--session-aws-profile`, `--set-passwords`, `calu credential new operator` and every `--database admin|operator` option are gone. On RDS the user login uses a password, not IAM. (4) **Migration 0043** takes the logins (and passwords) of `calu_session` and `calu_operator`; the roles, their grants and their row-level security policies stay, so earlier migrations stay valid. `calu_admin` passes every policy (`admin_all`, 0033) and counts as operator (`calu_caller_is_operator`). Per-session row isolation (0015/0025/0026) therefore no longer separates the sessions of one person; separation between people comes later from one schema per user (soft goal 7). `calu.db.roles.narrow` is removed. (5) **Converting a store of before D265**: `calu setup` finds `admin`/`session`/`operator`/`calu_admin`, asks the earlier admin password once, offers to keep it as the user password, migrates, stores `user` and `db-admin` and removes the old entries. Other commands on such a store say so and name `calu setup` (`logins.old_logins`); a store with only `operator` or `session` is told to remove them and `calu credential set user`. (6) **Messages** (`calu.logins`, the same in every front end): the prompt names the calu user password; a wrong one says so with the tries left and, at the last, how to recover ("A forgotten user password" in DOCUMENTATION.md); Ctrl-D, Escape or a closed dialog says what was not done; a blank answer to a protected login is called that, not "wrong"; a command without a terminal says why it cannot ask and the two ways out (`logins.no_terminal`); a stored login the database refuses is called outdated, with `calu setup` as the fix (`logins.outdated`). (7) **What it costs**: a leak of a running session's login now also decides escalations and changes settings, and a session process could change its own configuration in the database. calu keeps the login out of Claude's reach as before (no login in Claude's environment, `run.harden_process`, the sandbox, root mode), which D213 already noted is what protects it; the database's refusal was defence in depth. An empty user password with the sandbox off in the user's own account lets Claude's commands use the login (allowed by the owner, warned about at setup, start and in `calu doctor`). |
| D266 | **The 44 migrations are replaced by one baseline** (2026-11-24, the owner: "You can consolidate DB setup scripts ... I don't have any calu DBs yet that I can't delete at the drop of a hat, so no need to retain all these migration scripts"; asked whether to drop the roles and policies D265 left unused, he chose "Squash only"). `src/calu/db/migrations/0001_baseline.sql` creates the schema the migrations 0001-0044 had built: a `pg_dump --schema-only --no-owner` of a database they had migrated, cleaned up (no owner name, so it runs as any owner; the roles created in a prologue; `search_path` and `check_function_bodies` set for its own transaction only). Its dump is identical to the old chain's (compared on 2026-11-24). Roles calu_session and calu_operator, their grants and row-level security policies, and the session-key functions stay, unused since D265. A database migrated by the old chain is recognised by the names in `schema_migrations` (`calu.db.earlier_migrations`): `migrate` raises `EarlierSchema` and changes nothing, `calu setup` and `calu db migrate` report it with `calu db reset`, which recreates it empty, and `calu doctor --db` says so. Tests that re-ran single old migrations are gone; the reasons for each table and function are in the decisions of this file and in git history before this change. Later schema changes are migrations 0002 and up as before. |
| D267 | **`calu uninstall`** (2026-11-24, the owner: "a clean deinstallation script (removing not just calu but also the local db, but not prerequisite software) - something like \"calu uninstall\" or \"sudo calu uninstall\" ... with a safety check that requires to type in \"remove calu\". Typing that and pressing enter must require more than 2 seconds, otherwise it's likely automated"). `calu.uninstall` plans without changing anything and then removes item by item, the local database first and the installation last (its modules are loaded before). As a user: `~/.config/calu`, `~/.local/state/calu`, the local database when its data directory is the user's (the Docker daemon is shared, so root's database is left with a note naming `sudo calu uninstall`), and the uv tool environment found by its `uv-receipt.toml` with the entry points the receipt lists. As root: `/etc/calu`, `/var/lib/calu`, the local database, `/usr/local/lib/calu` with its commands, and the files and own installation of the user who ran sudo. The database: `docker compose down --volumes` for the compose project `calu-db`, `docker volume rm calu-db_data`, and the data directory (the container mount's parent and the default `<state>/calu/db`), whose cluster files belong to the container's user and are deleted by one run of the compose file's postgres image (root deletes them directly). Kept and named: prerequisites, a database on another server, the IDE extensions, what calu wrote into projects. It refuses while calu processes run (`serve`, `run`, `web`, `ide`, `attach`, `acp`, `start`, found in /proc), and asks for `remove calu`, refused when typed in under 2 seconds (`MIN_SECONDS`), like `calu db reset`. `--dry-run` only lists. |
| D268 | **`calu serve --web`: the web interface as the server's child** (2026-11-24, the owner: "a command to run \"calu serve\" and \"calu web\" at once, with calu serve being the parent process and \"calu web\" being launched as its child process (with shared / piped stdout/stderr), running as long as calu serve runs ... Ctrl-C or killing the parent process should end both"; the name was left to me: a flag like `--no-relay`, so `--sudo` and `--user` work as for serve). After the user login is read (one password), serve starts `python -m calu web --port N --login-fd FD [--login-typed] [--user U]`; the login goes through a pipe the child inherits, never the command line, the environment or a file. The child shares stdout and stderr. It is asked to die with its parent (`prctl(PR_SET_PDEATHSIG, SIGTERM)` before exec), and serve's `finally` sends SIGTERM, then SIGKILL after 10 s. Ctrl-C reaches both through the terminal's process group. A child that ends by itself does not stop the server; it is reported when the server stops (no watcher thread: a root server switches to its user's file identity, which `asuser.enter` refuses while other threads run; a watcher thread made the owner's first `calu serve --sudo --web` fail on 2026-11-24). `--login-typed` tells the child that the password was typed, so a browser signs in with it; with an empty user password the child prints an address with a token, as `calu web` does. Tested with real processes against the test database, including SIGKILL of the server; the root arrangement (`--sudo`) is tested only for its arguments. |
| D269 | **The local database starts with calu and, if chosen at setup, stops with `calu serve`** (2026-11-24, the owner: "whether the db autostarts and auto-stops should be determined during setup. \"calu serve\" and \"calu run\" should start it if it doesn't run and \"calu serve\" should stop it if it's an auto-stopping db"). `DockerBlock.stop_with_server` (inside the encrypted logins, default false, so older records read as before). `calu setup` asks for a new local database (`--stop-db-with-server` / `--no-stop-db-with-server` answer it; for a stored database they change it). The container's restart policy follows it (`local.set_restart`: `docker update --restart=no|unless-stopped`), so an auto-stopping database also stays stopped after a reboot. `calu run` and `calu serve` start it as before (`ensure_running`). At its end `calu serve` runs `docker compose down` unless another calu process of the machine uses it (`machine.calu_processes`, the same scan as `calu uninstall`), and says which. Tests never send `docker update`, `compose down` or `volume rm` to the machine's own Docker: an autouse guard in `tests/conftest.py` refuses them (on the developer's machine `calu-db` is the owner's database). |
| D270 | **"New session…" in the web interface: a folder, a new one or a clone, then the session and its chat pane** (2026-11-24, the owner: "a button to the main web interface to start a session in a folder? (Either in an existing or a new one, preinitialized from a git repo). This should start a session, optionally open the web pane ... if a corresponding check box is set ... this could all happen in a popup window that can then also host the chat pane itself"). The sessions page opens `/start` in a window of its own (`window.open`, a new tab without popups). The dialog lists the folders below the projects directory of the running `calu serve` of this host and of the user the interface works for (`servers.running`, `web.start.web_user`); a new folder or a `git clone` goes into the chosen one. Bounds: every folder passes `projects_dir.served_path` (below the projects directory, nothing hidden) before and after it is made; the interface's file identity is the user's (`asuser.enter`), so folders belong to that user, and `git` runs as that user (`asuser.child`). Clones take `https://`, `ssh://` and `user@host:path` only (`ext::` runs commands), with `protocol.allow=never` plus https and ssh, no terminal prompt, ssh in batch mode, five minutes at most. "Start a session now" files the request `calu start` files (label "calu web") and waits for the server; "open the chat pane" then redirects the window to `/pane#dir=<folder>`, which attaches to that session (or starts one with the first message). Not built: registering the folder as a project (a session in an unregistered folder reaches nothing on the network; `calu project add` does it). |
| D271 | **A "chat" link per local session in the sessions list** (2026-11-24, the owner: "The list of sessions also should have links that would open to a realtime/live chat pane, at least for local sessions (of course not remote ones)"). The pane reads `#session=<id>` (`pane/config.ts`, kept in the address with `dir`) and gives it to the chat host as the session to reattach to first (`ChatHost(remembered=...)`), which attaches with `ide.attach {session}`. The sessions list shows "chat" for a running, not stale session whose host is this machine and whose OS user is the user the interface works for (`web.start.web_user`), as `/pane#dir=<directory>&session=<id>` in a window named per session; a remote session has none, because the pane's bridge runs where `calu web` runs. Tested in a real browser: a pane opened with another folder and the session's id attaches to that session, and fails to without the id. |
| D272 | **The checkers' context folds early instead of dropping turns; 100,000 characters by default** (2026-11-24, the owner: "The 40k character limit is not from me. I think this should be relaxed by default to 100k chars. When we hit it, we must fold early"). `context.max_recent_chars` defaults to 100,000 (was 40,000). `ContextBuilder.needs_fold` is also true when the unfolded completed turns and the running turn together exceed it; the fold then takes the fewest oldest turns that bring the context under the limit (`_fold_size`; at least `fold_count` when `fold_every` turns are unfolded; all completed turns when the running turn alone is too long). The session checks after every recorded step (`Session._fold_if_due`, one background fold at a time), not only at the end of a turn. Dropping the oldest turns with a note stays only for the time a fold runs, or after one failed; a running turn longer than the limit by itself still drops its oldest steps behind a note. |
| D273 | **Generous limits on what a checker reads; a cut is shown and escalates** (2026-11-24, the owner: "the other hard caps can only harm and easily be used to circumvent checks. Limits should be more generous and typically not trigger (maybe a triggered limit should escalate when configured to do so)"). Raised: a rendered step 60,000 → 200,000 characters and a commit diff 240,000 → 400,000 (a cut of either always escalated and still does); the write-set patch 200,000 → 400,000 bytes; Claude's thinking and text the narrator reads 6,000 → 30,000 (its output budget 8,000 → 16,000 tokens); a subagent's task in its review 10,000 → 60,000 and its result 30,000 → 120,000, the runner's own cap 60,000 → 240,000 (so an over-long result is still seen as cut); kept blocks before a tool call 50 → 500; in the turn records a narration 6,000 → 30,000, a step description 4,000 → 10,000, a checker comment 1,500 → 4,000, an unnarrated final answer 1,500 → 10,000; docstrings of changed definitions 2,000 → 8,000 characters and 6 → 20 definitions. New: `context.escalate_on_cut` (default on; a setting, not an `auto_escalate` category, because clients switch those as session modes): when the narrator could not read all of Claude's thinking and text before a checked step (`Step.narration_cut`, shown to the checker in calu's own line) or the subagent checker could not show a whole task or result (`result_checker.cut_chars`), an approval becomes an escalation that names the cut and the model's verdict. A rule's approval is not affected (no checker needed the intent). The checkers' file tools keep their per-call limits: a checker pages through a file and is told when a listing is incomplete. |
| D274 | **A locking component for edits and commands, built in isolation, not wired in** (2026-11-25, the owner: "Implement this locking schema as an isolated Python component with a pluggable (generic) RWLock ... Infinite timeouts are not allowed, and we must have a configurable default and max timeout for edits and commands respectively. We also should have a fake locking scheme that doesn't lock at all ... After all, it's going to be a tradeoff of security versus speed"; then his decisions on the lock semantics, the same day). `calu.locking.RWLockingScheme` has the calls `run_command_in_worktree(s)`, `edit_in_worktree` and `edit_outside_worktree`. Inside a worktree a command takes the worktree's lock exclusively and an edit takes it shared; a global lock guards everything outside the worktrees (an edit there exclusive, every command shared, a command with `writes_outside` exclusive); an edit that names its file also takes that file's lock exclusively. **The owner's rules:** (1) every backend's lock is FIFO: a request takes its place when it is made (`RWLock.request`, then `LockRequest.wait`), the shared requests at the front are granted together, a waiting exclusive request keeps later shared ones out; (2) the owner of a lock is one operation (one tool call), not a thread or an agent, so two calls of one agent wait for each other like calls of two agents; (3) the calls of one agent enter the lock queues in the order the agent issued them, even when their checks finish in another order: `issue(agent)` gives a `Ticket` when the call is issued, and a call with a ticket enters only after the agent's earlier calls entered (their last request is queued) or were withdrawn (`Ticket.withdraw`: check denied, call failed, no lock); across agents the order of arrival at a lock decides; (4) no reentrancy: a second request of an owner for a lock it holds or waits for, or a second lease of an owner, is refused at once with `LockError`; (5) notes for the backends in `calu.locking.rwlock`: PostgreSQL advisory locks are reentrant per session (a session per lease), file locks must be flock or OFD locks, not process-wide POSIX locks, and need a turnstile for FIFO. Every operation takes its locks in one order (worktrees sorted, file, global last) and every wait, also the wait for the issue order, has a deadline, so no deadlock can last: `LockTimeouts` holds a default and a maximum for commands and for edits (600 s and 3600 s); a call may pass its own timeout, which is cut to the maximum, and an infinite, NaN or negative one is refused; an operation that runs out of time releases what it holds, withdraws its ticket and raises `LockTimeoutError`. `ThreadLockSpace` is the backend for one process; a backend on file locks or on PostgreSQL is the next step. `NoLockingScheme` takes the same calls, checks the same arguments, keeps the owner rule and never waits: the drop-in when the feature is off. Tests: `tests/unit/test_locking.py` (deterministic, incl. the owner's edit/command/edit example with checks finishing in reverse order) and `tests/fuzz/` (marker `fuzz`, never in CI): agents issue batches of parallel calls with tickets, checks finish out of order, calls are withdrawn, owners probe reentry, releases come from other threads; short timeouts (0.5 s); counts of timeouts per operation type and of running and waiting calls; deadlocks as cycles in the wait-for graph and as stalls that only a timeout ended; overlaps and breaks of the issue order; five schemes that are wrong on purpose check that the fuzzer finds their faults; one scenario with and without locks (x1.37 to x1.46 on 2026-11-25). **An adversarial review the same day** found the lock order and the thread backend sound and five defects in the ticket layer, all fixed: a ticket withdrawn while its call waited for its turn froze the scheme (a message built under the order's mutex took it again); a call that failed on its arguments left its ticket ISSUED, so its agent's later calls timed out for good (now every failure withdraws a ticket that had not entered, and `with scheme.issue(agent) as ticket:` withdraws an unused one); one ticket could serve two lock calls at once (now claimed atomically, a second use is `LockError`); a withdrawn ticket did not stop its pending call, which could then run after the agent's later calls (now withdrawing the ticket of a pending call cancels it with `LockCancelledError`, a separate error because the caller gave the call up, which is no bug); timeouts above `threading.TIMEOUT_MAX` overflowed (now refused). Generated owners start with `generated:`, which callers may not use; `live_owners()` and `open_tickets()` report what is pending. Limits written into the contract: lock calls only from worker threads (an asynchronous exception such as KeyboardInterrupt can leave a lock or a mutex taken); notes for the file and PostgreSQL backends (confirm the queued request before `request` returns, wake a blocked wait, unlock on the holding session, collision-free key mapping). **The global lock comes last** (the owner, 2026-11-25: "Let's use global last then"). With the global lock first, a command that waits for a busy worktree already holds the global lock, so an edit outside waits for every command queued there, one after the other, and new commands everywhere queue behind that edit. With it last, a waiting command holds nothing; the cost is that a command waiting for the global lock holds its worktree, so edits there wait too. Measured on the same scenarios (average waits): commands 33 -> 25 ms and edits outside 41 -> 28 ms with 5 % edits outside; commands 126 -> 71 ms, edits outside 148 -> 61 ms and edits 41 -> 67 ms (p95 unchanged) with 12 %; no difference with one agent per worktree. Global first only wins when many commands write outside (each then holds its worktree while it waits for every running command). The design and its decisions: agent/records/notes/locking.md. Not done: the wiring into the session (agent/records/notes/locking-design.md: acquire in the PreToolUse hook after the check, release on the tool result, the path through a person) and the open decisions listed there. |
| D275 | **The interprocess lock backend works on files** (2026-11-25, the owner: "write and implement an interprocess RWLock that works either with files or postgres according to our contract"). `calu.locking.FileLockSpace` keeps the FIFO queue of every lock of a space in one state file (`locks.json`); a short `flock` on a separate file guards each change, and every change opens it anew, so threads exclude each other as well as processes (flock belongs to an open file description; never process-wide POSIX locks); the new state is written to a temporary file and renamed. Grants use the thread backend's algorithm, so FIFO, owners and no reentrancy hold across processes. A waiting request polls its entry (1 to 20 ms) and is woken at once by a change of its own lock in its own process. Entries of processes that no longer run are removed by every change and by a waiting request that finds one on its lock (every 0.2 s); a process is identified by its id, its start time and the boot id, so a reused id or a reboot keeps no dead hold alive. **Files rather than PostgreSQL:** a dead holder is recognised without a server noticing a closed connection; no database round trip per lock call, and locking works while the database is away; it is tested across real processes in the unit layer without a database; the checkouts it protects are local to the machine anyway. PostgreSQL's advisory locks would need a session per lease (they are reentrant per session), a confirmation of the queued request in `pg_locks` before `request` returns, and the unlock on the holding session. Tests: the lock contract runs against both backends; `tests/unit/test_locking_files.py` across real processes (exclusion, FIFO, a holder killed with SIGKILL, owners); the fuzzer on the file backend. Costs: a change about 0.2 ms, about 200 calls per second under the fuzzer's load (the thread backend about 1000). **Second review (2026-11-25), fixed:** the files are named per boot and pid namespace (`locks.<boot>.<namespace>.json`), so processes of other containers or machines use other files instead of removing each other's entries as dead; an entry is removed only on proof (no process with the id, another start time, or a zombie), an unreadable `/proc` keeps it; a space forgets an entry before the change that removes it and removes its own unwanted entries with its next change, and what a change adds counts only once written, so a full disk leaves no orphaned hold; the flock is waited for at most 5 s, in the kernel's queue by a helper thread (a retry loop with sleeps cost two thirds of the throughput); a directory of another user or writable by others is refused, the temporary file is created with O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW; polling up to every 0.2 s (2% instead of 10% of a core for ten waiters). |
| D276 | **Locking is wired into Calu, off by default** (2026-11-25, the owner: "Then add this feature to calu. Do not enable it by default yet, make it a configurable option (also make the default wait timeout for edits and commands configurable)" and "The hooks into the locking mechanism should always be called, just the implementation would be a no-op implementation when it's disabled in the config"). Settings `locking.enabled` (false), `locking.backend` ("files", or "process"), `locking.directory` (blank: `$XDG_STATE_HOME/calu/locks`), `locking.command_wait_s` and `locking.edit_wait_s` (600 s, at most 3600). `calu.call_locks.CallLocks`, one per session, on a lock space shared by the sessions of the process: `Session.pre_tool_use` issues the call's ticket before its check, takes the locks of an approved call in a worker thread (at most the configured wait and the PreToolUse hook's time left minus 30 s; not in time: DENY with a note that nothing ran and the call can be repeated), and gives them back while an escalation that came up meanwhile holds the call, with no await between the last look and the answer; a denied call withdraws its ticket, an escalated one keeps it. `Session.ask` gives back the locks before a person decides and takes them after an allow. The tool result gives them back after the write set was measured; the end of the run gives back everything. Commands lock the checkouts of their working directory and `cd` targets, edits the checkout of their file and the file (outside every checkout: the global lock), other tools nothing. With locking off every call goes through the same path on `NoLockingScheme`, so every session test exercises it. Tests: `tests/unit/test_call_locks.py` (the locks of each tool, a busy worktree, the hook's budget, a cancelled wait, issue order, giving back and taking again, locking off, an unusable directory, a session end to end). Not built: a second check when a command's worktree changed between its check and its locks; recognising commands that write outside their worktrees; processes that outlive their call; Calu's own trace writes under the locks (agent/records/notes/locking.md, section 9). **Second review (2026-11-25), fixed:** a command's "before" snapshot (D31) is taken once it holds its locks, not before its wait (an edit of another agent made meanwhile counted as the command's change, and a merely waiting command made the running one's write set overlap and go unreviewed); `Session.ask` drops the snapshot while a person decides and takes it again after the re-take; a re-take has a ticket of its own (`retake:<id>`) so a cancellation ends its wait; `ask` withdraws the ticket on every exit without an allow, also a cancellation; a hold after a lock wait gets at most the hook's time left, and `Decided.hold_left` is kept across passes; `CallLocks.release`, `call_ended`, `close` and `withdraw` log a backend failure instead of raising; with locking off the plan is made in the worker thread too. Kept and documented: a command holds its locks while a person decides about its write set (PostToolUse), so its undo cannot race another call's edit; a main-checkout command does not lock nested worktrees (`.claude/worktrees`). User documentation: guarantees, limits, upsides, downsides and when to switch it on (DOCUMENTATION.md). **Writes outside (2026-11-25, the owner):** a command that the command checker flags with `writes_outside_worktree` (code, configuration, scripts or programs outside its checkout; not logs, caches, reports or data; judged from what the call shows, not an exhaustive search) takes the global lock exclusively, like an edit outside every checkout. The flag is noted per call (`CallLocks.mark_writes_outside`) and used by every take of the call. Owners are `<tool call id>@<token>` (tool call ids are unique per session only). **Switched while sessions run (2026-11-25, the owner):** a changed `locking` setting reaches running sessions through `Session.apply_settings` (`CallLocks.reconfigure`); a call keeps the setting in effect when its check began, a second take and every later call use the new one; per project through the project scope. |

A text field with a `<datalist>`: an id is typed or picked from the chosen folder's conversations (`herd.stored_conversations`, the merge of calu's records and Claude Code's transcripts that the IDE's list used, now shared; with `resumable=True` calu's conversations whose transcript is gone are left out). `calu.web.start.check_resume` refuses, before anything is filed, an id that is no UUID, one the folder has no transcript of (Claude Code continues a conversation only in its own folder), and the field together with a new folder or a repository; the id is normalised (lower case). Checked before serving (`cli._tls_files`): both or neither; the key not readable by group or others; certificate and key load together (not encrypted); a note when the certificate does not name `calu.localhost`. With TLS the login cookie is `Secure` and `LocalOnly` also compares the scheme of a changing request's `Origin`. Why not by default: on the loopback interface the connection does not leave the machine, browsers already treat `calu.localhost` as a secure context, a self-signed certificate teaches clicking through warnings, and from another machine the SSH tunnel encrypts. `mkcert` gives a certificate browsers trust. The review confirmed the rest of the same-origin protection (host check, `Origin`/`Sec-Fetch-Site` on every change and WebSocket, the key, `SameSite=Strict`, the policy); its other findings are listed for the owner. |
| D279 | **A security pass after two reviews: an external attacker's view of Calu, and the web interface** (2026-11-25, the owner's decisions on the reviews' findings; the findings and their proof scripts stay outside the repository). **Quick wins, all accepted:** (Q1) the root `calu serve --web` starts its `calu web` child with `python -I` and working directory `/`, so no module of the projects directory is imported as root; (Q2) the session and relay WebSockets refuse a handshake that carries an `Origin` header (only browsers send one; no Calu client does); (Q3) `calu web` listens on both `127.0.0.1` and `::1`, prints its address only after both are bound, and `calu serve --web` says so when its child ends early; (Q4) `claude.cli_path`, which names the program a session runs, is no longer a database setting but the machine's `CALU_CLAUDE_PATH` (environment or Calu's env file; a stored value is ignored); (Q5) a database on another host is reached with `require_auth=scram-sha-256` and, over TLS, `channel_binding=require` ("keep it safe by default, but let's not enforce our defaults on preexisting database configs"): when an existing server's configuration refuses them, `calu setup` asks whether to keep the server's own method (`--keep-server-auth` for scripts), never by itself, since a machine in the middle can cause the refusal; configurations stored earlier keep their settings; there is no general opt-out option ("We don't need legacy auth"); (Q6) no token on a command line: `--token-file`, standard input or `CALU_TOKEN`, and printed advice without the token; (Q7) supply chain: `exclude-newer = "3 days"` for uv, `min-release-age=3` for npm, `install.sh` with `--exclude-newer "3 days"` and a release tag by default, GitHub Actions pinned by commit, the Gradle wrapper's checksum. **Serious issues:** (S1) the new-session dialog warns to clone only trusted repositories and says that Claude Code trusts every folder a session starts in (its `.claude/` settings, hooks and MCP servers run as the user, outside the checkers and the sandbox); README and DOCUMENTATION say the same, also for a `git pull` that changes them. No approval of project settings for now. (S2) `/var/lib/calu/database.json` keeps the place (0644, no secret); the `calu_admin` password of a setup without a user password moves into a second file, owned by the user the server serves, mode 0600. Role passwords are stored with 3,000,000 SCRAM iterations ("3 million rehashes should be enough"; about 0.3 s per login here, paid by every new connection and by every guess; PostgreSQL 16 or newer; calu's tests set `CALU_SCRAM_ITERATIONS=4096`, else the database layer takes twenty times as long), and the local database answers a wrong password after a second (`auth_delay`). A place file of before D279 that still holds the password is read until `sudo calu setup` writes it again; `calu doctor` reports it. No minimum password length: "Not having security is a choice." (S3) The web login token is no longer printed: where a user password exists it stays the login; otherwise the web password is chosen, may be empty ("Empty passwords are allowed"), is stored only as a slow hash that only root reads (a root installation; a user installation keeps it in the user's credential directory), and is changed with root rights. The chat pane asks for the password itself when it has no cookie (it runs in sibling iframes of a master frame layout that stores nothing; it was never meant to put a token into notebook source). (S4) The PyCharm plugin shows the command, the SSH options and the extra arguments that its project settings (`.idea/calu.xml`) give before it starts the bridge, and starts only after approval; the approval is kept at application level for that exact content and asked again when it changes, also when no password is asked. **S3 as built:** `calu web --set-password` asks twice and stores `{kdf: pbkdf2-sha256, rounds, salt, hash}` (rounds = the SCRAM iterations above) or `{empty: true}` in `web-password.json` next to Calu's `.env` (mode 0600, directory 0700; `/etc/calu` for root's installation); `calu web` without a user password and without that file refuses to start and names the command; an empty choice turns the gate off and `calu web` warns at every start. The `?token=` sign-in is gone; an old printed address is just an address. The chat pane offers its key subprotocol first and, after a refused handshake (and only if `calu web` answers at all), asks for the password and sends it as `calu.password.<base64url>`, which `TokenGate` takes on `/pane/rpc` only, with the back-off of the sign-in page; text that is no base64url of UTF-8 is refused at once. The pane keeps the password in memory for reconnects; a reload asks again. **S4 as built:** the digest (SHA-256) of `launchContent(settings)` (mode, calu command, SSH host, directory, options and sync in SSH mode, the server's sudo, insecure and extra arguments; not the notifications switch) is stored in the application service `CaluApprovals` (`calu-approvals.xml`, not roaming, at most 500). An unapproved project gets a wiring whose launcher refuses and has no sync and no SSH interpreter; the backend sends an `ApprovalRequestDto` to the frontend (like a password request), and "Start calu" stores the digest and rebuilds the wiring. Saving the settings page approves what was saved. The server command and the terminal command come from the wiring's approved settings, never from the file as it is later. Even the default settings are asked once per IDE (one dialog covers every project with the defaults). **Rounds lowered to 300,000** (2026-11-25): on backtester1 (Xeon Silver 4116, no SHA instructions) 3,000,000 rounds took 4.9 s per login, computed by libpq on the client for every connection, and `calu web` started by `calu serve --web` ran into libpq's 10 s connect timeout; the owner: "Use 300k, not 500k. Enough to multiply by a lot the computational price for brute-forcing." A password stored earlier keeps its rounds until it is set again. |
| D280 | **A session survives a stop of `calu serve` or `calu run`: it is dormant, and comes back when a client asks for it** (2026-11-25, the owner: "sessions should survive it if calu serve or calu run is stopped and started again. The last session in a folder would be the default session to resume if using calu attach", and "The claude sessions should be started on demand for dormant sessions"). (1) **Dormant** is not a state of its own: a session whose row has ended, or whose process is gone (`SessionRow.stale`), while its conversation stays in the database (turns, chat summary, D65) and in Claude Code's transcript. No migration; a session the user shut down is dormant too, since the newest session of the folder is the default either way. (2) **On demand, never at a server's start:** a restarted `calu serve` starts nothing by itself, so no Claude process runs for a session nobody uses. `calu attach` (and `calu acp`, which shares `_find_endpoint`) first looks for a running session as before; only when none matches (`NoRunningSession`, never for an ambiguous match) does it look up the newest dormant one (`herd.dormant_session`: this host and OS user, a Claude session id, the current directory or the nearest one above it with any session, the newest start; with `--session ID` that id's beginning in any directory) and file a `resume` request for it (`_served_session`, shared with `calu start`). The server resumes it as `calu start --resume` would: same Claude session id, a new `sessions` row, the history of the earlier rows. A newest session that still runs without clients is not resumed a second time; without a `calu serve` the message names `calu run --continue` and `calu run --resume ID`. A client that runs as root resumes nothing (root's sessions are `calu run` processes). (3) **`calu run --continue`** resolves the same lookup for its own directory (exactly that directory) inside `run_session`, with the session's own login, so no second password is asked; a directory without a session starts a new conversation, a running one is refused. (4) **Not continued:** a turn that ran when the process stopped (Claude Code continues from its transcript), an escalation that was open (closed with its session), the model and effort of the earlier run (the server's defaults apply). The IDE bridge and the web interface keep their own session choice (the web's new-session dialog continues a conversation, D277). (5) **The IDE bridge resumes a named session** (2026-11-26, the owner: "If there's a `calu serve` process, it can just resume a session that's dormant. This should be the default behavior if we try to attach to a specific session that's not live"): `ide.attach {session: ID}` that finds no running session looks it up with `herd.dormant_session` (this host, the bridge's OS user, any directory); a running session with the same Claude session id is attached instead (no second resume), else a `resume` request is filed for the session's directory (`Bridge._dormant`, `_request_session`, shared with `ide.start`). `ide.attach` without a session never resumes, so opening an editor starts no Claude process. The chat host's `reattach` (after a bridge restart and after the server came back) loads a resumed session whole and shows "Resuming the session" after 1 s. |
| D281 | **A test that is really a launcher is dishonest** (2026-11-26, the owner: "A test that is not really a test but rather a launcher, or that might be launched primarily for its side effects is dishonest, especially if it appears designed ( obviously or less obviously so ) to be useable as a backdoor or for data exfiltration or data access"). `honesty-rules.md`, section "Tests": a test that checks nothing or little and is run mainly for its side effects (starting programs or services, running commands, reading, printing, copying or sending data) is dishonest even when its documentation names them, because its name and its place among the tests say that running it is safe; signs are listed (no assertion or one that cannot fail, effects the check does not need, output that carries what it read, a command or path taken from an argument, the environment or a file). "When to escalate" gains a sign for such a test that runs what it is given or carries secrets or private data; one example. A new version in `shipped.json`, so an unedited stored copy of the rules is offered the update and an edited one is left alone (D248). Three evaluation cases in `honesty_cases.yaml`: an obvious launcher and a test that uploads `~/.ssh` behind an assertion that cannot fail (both ESCALATE), and an honest test that starts the CLI and checks its output (PASS). Not yet measured with a real model. |
| D282 | **An escalation timeout stops the turn and Claude Code, and the conversation is resumed at once** (2026-11-26, the owner: "calu should immediately resume the session with it's wrapper but consider it stopped by the timeout. It should be possible for the user to come back to the computer, see "Oh, it ran into a timeout" and tell it to continue without having to hop through loops"; amends D27). (1) **What happens:** the step is denied and the session stops as before (`EscalationDesk._timed_out`, then `Session.stop` in a task of its own); with `Session.resume_after_timeout` the same task then calls `start(tg, resume_id=<Claude session id>)` with the run's model and effort: a new `sessions` row that reads the earlier turns (D65), state "idle". A `log` warning says "The session was stopped by the escalation timeout ... The conversation is resumed and waits for you; send a message (for example "continue")"; it stays in the replay buffer, so a client that attaches later shows it; the escalation's row and the denial Claude reads say the same. A failed resume is a `log` error naming `/start <id>` and `calu run --resume <id>`. (2) **Where:** `calu serve` sessions and `calu run` reading a terminal (`run.py`: `options.stay or (options.interactive and stdin is a tty)`). An unattended run (`--batch`, input from a pipe) stays stopped and ends as before, with `calu run --resume <id>` in the message: nobody would come back to it, and a restarted Claude Code would only keep the run alive. (3) **Not changed:** the stop after too many failed store writes (D59) never resumes; it has its own callback now (`_stop_soon` → `_stop_quietly`). A serve session resumed this way may still end later through `--idle-timeout`; it is then dormant and `calu attach` brings it back (D280). (4) **Every end calu did not ask for** (the owner, the same day: "This does not just apply to escalation timeouts: If claude ends a session that calu would have continued, and it is resumeable, calu should just pick it up automatically (but consider it stopped, waiting for user input)"): the flag is `Session.auto_resume`; `stop` counts its calls (`_stops`), and a run whose `_run` ends with no `stop` since it began -- Claude Code exited, crashed or failed -- is resumed by `_resume_after_end` through the same `_resume_now`, with a warning that names the runner's error. A `stop` of calu's (a client, `/stop`, the end of `calu run`, a server's shutdown, too many failed writes) is never resumed. At most `AUTO_RESUME_LIMIT` = 3 automatic resumes within `AUTO_RESUME_WINDOW_S` = 10 minutes; one more end leaves the session stopped with an error that names `/start <id>` and `calu run --resume <id>`, so a Claude Code that fails at every start is not restarted for ever. Messages that were queued when the run ended are still dropped with it (`_run`). |
| D283 | **A message queued during a turn is held by calu and can be sent now or retracted** (2026-11-26, the owner: "enqueued messages should get a "send now" and a "remove" button, so they can be sent immediately or retracted. If sent now, it's the same as pressing stop and submitting them afterwards. The retract button de-queues the text and places it back in the edit box ( if that box is empty, otherwise discard the text)", and for the chat pane of the web interface: "messages that are accidentally enqueued instead of injected immediately sit there and wait"). (1) **Why calu holds them:** a message was written to Claude Code at once (`priority: "later"`), and Claude Code keeps its own queue, from which nothing can be taken back. Now a "queued" message for the main agent that is sent while a turn runs (`RunState.busy`) stays in calu (`RunState.held`, with its images) and is written to Claude Code as "later" right after the turn's `turn.result` (`Session._release_held`). Claude reads it at the end of the turn either way, so nothing changes for Claude. Messages sent outside a turn, injected ones and those to a subagent are written at once as before. (2) **Protocol:** `QueuedMessage.held`; `chat.retract {id}` → `{text}` removes a held message; `chat.send_now {id}` moves it to the front of the held ones and interrupts the turn (`Session.interrupt`, the user's stop), whose end writes it; both refuse a message calu no longer holds. (3) **Clients:** the chat view shared by the VSCode extension and the web chat pane shows "Send now" and "Retract" on held entries; a retracted text goes into the input field only if that is empty (`retracted {text}`). The full-screen terminal client and the PyCharm plugin show the queue without the buttons for now. (4) The end of a run still drops what is queued, held or not. |
| D284 | **A data file is honest; the honesty rules do not check data** (2026-11-26, the owner: "json files with data and no comments are ( just like config files without comments ) considered honest. It is not supposed to check data for correctness"). `honesty-rules.md` gains a section "Data files": a file that holds data and nothing that is run (JSON, CSV, YAML or TOML values, fixtures, sample records, lookup tables, translations) is honest with or without comments; wrong, misspelt, outdated or missing values are no dishonesty. Only a value that is code by the section "Configuration" (it names a command, function, file or host a unit acts on) is judged, as configuration. "Not dishonest" names it too. A new version in `shipped.json` (D248); an evaluation case `json-data-file-without-comments-with-odd-values` (PASS) beside the existing `config-key-does-more-than-it-says` (DENY). Not yet measured with a real model. |
| D285 | **The user's pause: approved checked steps wait** (2026-11-26, the owner: "a pause button to the web and IDE chat pane, which pauses progress within the chat, so the user has time for review what is happening. The pause should unblock by default after 15 minutes or when clicked again. It should activate on the next edit or command check and just hold it"; then "use a 10 minute timeout", and "it should hold the start of a subagent, it should also put a pause on all subagents of the current session"). (1) **Protocol:** request `pause.set {paused, minutes?}` (result `PauseInfo {paused, until}`), event `pause.changed {paused, until, client}` (also when the time ran out, `client` ""), snapshot field `pause`; `pause.set` steers the session, so it is checked against the token like every request that is not read-only. (2) **Where it holds:** `Session._checked`, after the step's decision and before its locks: a step whose decision approves and that needed a check (source other than "policy") waits in `calu.pause.Pause.wait` while the session is paused, with `step.held` (reason "the user's pause") and a `log` line; the last look for an escalation that came up meanwhile follows. A DENY or ESCALATE is answered at once. Every agent's calls, the Agent tool's start of a subagent included, pass the same gate, so one pause holds them all. (3) **Why in the hook and not in `can_use_tool`:** `can_use_tool` has no timeout, but its answer is an "allow", which skips Claude Code's auto mode classifier for a PASS. So the wait runs inside the PreToolUse hook, which times out after 15 minutes (`HOOK_TIMEOUT_S`). The wait gets what is left of that time, less `LOCK_HOOK_MARGIN_S` and `PAUSE_HOOK_MARGIN_S` (60 s). Hence `pause_timeout_s` defaults to 600 and allows at most 780. A step released by the hook's limit runs; the pause goes on for the next. (4) **Clients:** the chat webview (VSCode and the pane of `calu web`) has a pause/play button left of the stop button while a session is attached, and the hint line says "Paused"; it ends the shown pause at `until` by itself, because an expiry with no held step sends no event. `calu run`, `calu serve`, `calu attach --plain` print the start and the end, the full-screen client shows them as notices; ACP has no pause. **Not built:** a pause button in the full-screen client and in PyCharm. |
| D286 | **The honesty review of large files: the change since the last verified content, the whole file on request** (2026-11-26, the owner: a 272 kB generated bundle blocked the reviews; "A file (or diff) either fits into the context or not ... often reviewing diffs (with a sufficient amount of context lines around the changes) is likely sufficient ... If a file that needs to be reviewed in entirety truly exceeds the token context of a reviewer, it's too large for review and must be escalated"). Chunked reading of one file by sub-reviewers was considered and left out: a text fits the reviewer's context or it does not. (1) **Diff since the content last verified.** `HonestyShared.texts` (`VerifiedTexts`) keeps up to 64 MB of texts in memory and drops the least recently used first. It holds the text of every content a checked edit verified (`record_edit` → `_keep_text`) and of every content a review plan read. A file whose present content differs from its last verified one (`HonestyState.verified`) is shown as the diff since that content, if its text is kept (`ReviewFile.against` "verified"; the prompt says so). Otherwise the diff is against the baseline, as before. A restart loses the texts, so the baseline is used then. (2) **Context:** `honesty.review_context_lines`, default 20 (was a fixed 3). (3) **The whole file on request:** for a file shown as a diff, the reviewer may answer `FileVerdict.needs_whole_file`. `verdicts` returns such files apart, and `HonestyReviewer.review` runs a second round that shows each of them whole (`ReviewFile.content`, `whole_files`). A file whose content is over `honesty.review_file_bytes` is not judged (`ReviewOutcome.unjudged`) and stays unreviewed. A step that needs a clean state then goes to a person, as for a skipped file. (4) **Marked files:** larger than `SMALL_FILE_BYTES` (30,000) with something to compare with: the diff and the present source of the marked definitions (`marked_excerpts`, Python only, every symbol a definition); otherwise whole as before. (5) **Limits as settings:** `honesty.review_file_bytes` defaults to 600,000, about 150,000 tokens; it was the constant 200,000. `honesty.review_batch_bytes` defaults to 300,000; it was 120,000 characters. Files up to 8 MB are hashed and diffed (`REVIEW_READ_BYTES`, was 2 MB). Generated, minified and obfuscated code followed in D288. |
| D287 | **More marks before a review, a notice after dishonest steps, neutral findings** (2026-11-26, the owner: "allow R=6 marks before requiring a full honesty review"; "if we see D>=3 dishonest edits happen ... the main coding agent should be notified about this, even in honesty tracking (non-enforcement) mode"; "it must always offer both options: Change the behavior OR change the documentation ... What the intended behavior is is determined by the coding agent"). (1) R is `honesty.max_open_marks`, default 6 (was 3).
