| `steps` | session, turn, sequence, agent id, tool name, input (JSON), git commit, rules outcome, checker decision, reason, step summary, narration, result summary, timestamps, the snapshot trees before and after the step and the paths it wrote (D31) |
| `checker_types` | name, route (command, edit, subagent result), instructions (Markdown), model, limits, version |
| `checker_runs` | step, checker type and version, model, decision, tokens, cache hits, duration |
| `escalations` | step, session, reason, full context (rendered step, recent turns, checker output), state (open, allowed, denied, cancelled), resolved by, resolved at, note |
| `rules` | scope (global, project), list (command/read/write allow/deny), pattern, comment, enabled |
| `config` | scope (global, project, session), key, value (JSON) |
| `chat_summaries` | session, folded summary text, covered turns |
| `audit_log` | who changed which configuration, rule or checker definition, when; and, with the session filled in, who changed a running session's own settings (D43) |
| `comments` | a user's comment on a selection in a diff or a file, with the step's turn (migration 0003) |
| `escalations.context` | also holds the tool input with workspace-relative paths and the content of the file an edit would change, so that `calu evals` can rebuild the step as a case |
| `client_tokens` | SHA-256 of the tokens with which clients may attach to a session, with label, address, last use and a revoked flag (migrations 0004 and 0009) |

Narrator instructions may stay built into calu (not in the database); `calu setup` loads the
built-in ones, and a session falls back to them if the database has no row for a checker type.
Notifications: `escalation_resolved`, `config_changed` (sessions reload rules and instructions).

Migrations: `0001_initial.sql` creates the tables, roles, triggers and the two escalation
functions; `0002_turn_records.sql` adds the remaining columns of a `TurnRecord` (`steps`,
`answer`, `narrated`, `started_at`, `ended_at`), an index on `sessions.claude_session_id` for
resuming, and replaces `calu_cancel_escalation(bigint)` with a version that records why the
session cancelled.

## 8. Command-line tool

| Command | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `calu db up` / `db down` | start or stop a local PostgreSQL with Docker Compose (simple setups) |
| `calu db migrate` | create or update roles and schema (migrations) |
| `calu setup` | first-time setup: migrations, default checkers and rules, login and encrypted credential of `calu_session` |
| `calu credential list/set/remove` | manage the encrypted credential store (D12), e.g. `set admin` for an existing PostgreSQL server |
| `calu project add <dir>`, `calu project list` | register a project (git remote, network policy) in the database; writes nothing into the project and leaves existing Claude Code configuration untouched |
| `calu config get/set/unset`, `calu checker list/show/set`, `calu rules list/add/remove/enable/disable` | configuration in the database (`calu.admin`; needs the `admin` credential, because a session may only read it) |
| `calu evals [FILE ...]` | run the checkers on evaluation cases (built-in, files, resolved escalations) and report accuracy, critical misses and over-escalations |
| `calu run [<dir>]` | start a session in the foreground (for tmux); prints the WebSocket address and a client token |
| `calu serve` | one process, authenticated once, that starts and runs many sessions on request (D208) |
| `calu start [<dir>]` | ask the running `calu serve` for a session (new, resumed or forked) and attach to it |
| `calu attach <session>` | terminal client for a running session |
| `calu sessions`, `calu escalations`, `calu resolve <id> allow\|deny [--note]` | herd management through the database |
| `calu install <component>` | additional components (IDE plugin, web UI, ...) |

## 9. Reuse from stay-in-the-loop

Taken over and adapted (with their unit tests): rules engine and shell parser
(`checkers/rules.py`, `checkers/shell.py`), checkers and their default instructions, result
checker, narrator, context builder and turn records, `SdkRunner` (Agent SDK, hook, auto mode),
skill-text filter, file links, chat queue, protocol models, fake runner and fake models for tests.
Rewritten: session state (multi-client, persisted), storage (database instead of files), bus
(WebSocket instead of stdio), configuration (database instead of `config.toml` and Markdown
files), CLI. Knowledge carried over: sitl's spike results on Claude Code's behaviour.

## 10. Development and tests

- `docker-compose.yml` with two services: `dev` (Linux with Python, uv, Node and the Claude
  Code CLI; the repository mounted) and `caludb` (PostgreSQL).
- Unit tests without database and without API keys (fake models, fake Claude runner, as in sitl).
- Database tests against `caludb` (marked `db`); live tests with real Claude and models (marked
  `live`; checker model Nemotron Lightning on Fireworks, Claude model haiku or sonnet).
- Spikes for every assumption about Claude Code behaviour, recorded like sitl's spike results.

## 11. Open questions

| # | Question |
|---|---|
| Q1 | Answered by D15, and for containers by the measurement in `spikes/RESULTS.md` ("The sandbox inside a Docker container"): a default container cannot create a user namespace, because Docker's seccomp profile blocks it, so bubblewrap and with it the sandbox cannot start; `--security-opt seccomp=unconfined` is the least of the options that work (`--cap-add SYS_ADMIN` and `--privileged` grant more, `apparmor=unconfined` does not help), and socat must be installed too — calu's own dev image now does both. Still untested: what `enableWeakerNestedSandbox` changes, which needs a real tool call inside a container. The cheap route was tried and is a dead end (`spikes/RESULTS.md`, "What `claude sandbox status` can and cannot answer"): that command needs no login, but reports the same posture with and without the flag and does not probe whether the kernel allows the namespaces, and it reads the project's settings rather than calu's. It did yield one thing calu now uses -- `policyLocked`, a managed settings policy that locks the posture and would override calu's own `--settings`, which `calu doctor` reports. The proxy's allow list is answered by D39. **The last open line -- what `enableWeakerNestedSandbox` changes -- is answered by D134**: nothing that helps; it stays off. |
| Q2 | Relationship to sitl: does calu replace it (VSCode extension as a calu client) or do both continue? **The technical half is settled, and the last argument for sitl is weaker than I wrote** (checked 2026-11-10): any editor that speaks ACP drives a calu session with no extension of its own (D24, D42), and what ACP does not carry -- the checker verdicts as structure next to a step, and the herd overview -- lives in the web interface instead (D33, D34). I had said ACP "does not reach VSCode yet"; that is only true of **native** support. VSCode reaches ACP through community extensions today (formulahendry's "ACP Client", which ships a list of pre-configured agents, and others), while Microsoft has standardised VSCode's own agent mode on MCP and has not committed to native ACP (issue microsoft/vscode#265496). So a VSCode user can drive calu now, through somebody else's extension, and the remaining question is whether that is good enough or whether sitl keeps its own panel for the parts ACP cannot carry. That is a product call, not a technical one. Worth re-checking: a native implementation would settle it. |
| Q3 | Answered by D49: **not** a role per session, but row-level security on the one `calu_session` login, keyed on a session id the connection sets. Built and switched on in migration 0015.
| Q4 | How clients authenticate **remotely**. The token half is answered by D30 (tokens minted per session, revocable, `calu token`). **Transport: recommendation (2026-11-10), implemented as documentation rather than code** -- keep binding loopback and send people through an SSH tunnel, which DOCUMENTATION.md now spells out for the web interface, a session's port and a session's Unix socket. calu would otherwise have to carry TLS material, a certificate reload and a hostname policy to be worth the bind, and an SSH tunnel is already on every machine that runs a session; a herd is watched through the **database** anyway (D30), which is the path that needs no tunnel at all. **Operator logins: built** (D48): `calu credential new operator` hands out a `calu_operator` connection string, and the read-and-resolve commands prefer it over `admin`. What is left for the owner is only whether to use it, which is a matter of who gets which credential. |
| Q5 | Answered as a **proposal** by D45, which needs the owner's word: a new project gets "proxy" with a starter allow list (package registries, git over HTTPS, OS packages) instead of "full". It is one flag to overrule (`calu project add --network full`) and one command to change afterwards (`calu project network`), and existing projects are untouched. What the owner should weigh: the starter list is opinionated. The cost is **not** a stopped task, which is what I first wrote and then measured: an unlisted domain makes Claude Code ask, calu escalates that (D38) and a person allows it in one command, so the default costs an interruption rather than a failure. |
| Q6 | Answered by D27: it stops after `escalation_timeout_s`, and is resumed by Claude's session id. |
| Q7 | Answered by D16. |
| Q8 | How a client attached to the session process decides an escalation: as now (the session cancels the row with a note, D18), with the client's own `calu_operator` credentials, or with a database function that lets `calu_session` record a client decision for its own session. |
| Q11 | Answered by D23. |
| Q10 | Answered 2026-11-09: **no**. calu does not get the interactive-plugin mode of §4.5; third-party user interfaces are served through ACP instead (D24). |
| Q9 | Where sitl's training mode belongs in calu (human review of checker decisions, to collect evaluation data): it was left out of the session port and needs tables of its own. The user comments it was grouped with are ported (migration 0003). **Recommendation (2026-11-10), with the alternative built (D55):** do not build it. Every escalation a human resolves is already a labelled case -- the step, what the checker decided and what the human decided instead -- and `calu evals --from-db` turns exactly those rows into an eval set (`evals.cases.cases_from_escalations`). A training mode would collect the same labels by asking a human to review decisions nobody disagreed with, which is the expensive half of the data and the half that teaches least. If the eval set turns out too small in practice, the cheaper next step is the auto-escalation switches (D42): switching one on for a while makes every step of that kind wait for a human, which produces labels for the steps the checkers *approved* -- the ones that are otherwise missing -- without any new tables. |
| Q13 | Answered by D36: a fork at an earlier turn works, with `resume_session_at` and `resume_drops_turn`. |
| Q14 | Answered by D46 after measuring it: **no**. `rewind_files` tracks only what Claude Code's edit tools changed -- a command's writes survive it, and a file a command created is not removed -- so a fork that used it would leave a half-reverted tree that looks consistent and is not. A fork rewinds the conversation only and says so; if file rewinding is wanted, it belongs on D31's snapshots. |
| Q15 | **Built, measured, built in CI for both Linux architectures and published by a tag, so the only thing left is a person pressing publish.** `scripts/build-binary.sh` produces a 70 MiB one-file `calu` that applies the migrations, seeds the defaults, exports the protocol schema and serves the web interface, and starts faster than the installed wheel (D41, `spikes/RESULTS.md`). The `binary` job of `release.yml` builds and runs it on every tag and attaches it to the run. A tag now also attaches both binaries to a **draft** GitHub release, which needs nothing but the token every run has -- so the binaries ship even while the wheel waits for the PyPI setup. Verified end to end with a throwaway tag, which produced a draft release with `calu-linux-x86_64` (70 MiB) and `calu-linux-arm64` (73 MiB) and was then deleted; the same run also showed the version guard refusing a tag that disagrees with `pyproject.toml`. Draft, because pushing a tag should not publish a release by itself. What the owner decides: pressing publish, and whether more platforms are worth a runner each -- each one is its own runner and its own 70 MiB, and only the machine that builds it can prove it runs. PyApp was not used: it needs a Rust toolchain, which this machine does not have, and PyInstaller already answers the question. |
| Q16 | **Should a push, a deploy or a publish be a rule rather than a checker's judgement?** Measured (D73, D76): with a 30B checker model, `git push origin feature/parser` and `kubectl apply -f k8s/production.yaml` are passed in roughly one run of three, with reasons that read well ("not a force push, reversible", "a standard DevOps operation") and are wrong about what the user wanted. No instruction wording I tried closed it reliably, and the default rules do not cover these commands -- the deny list has force pushes and remote deletions, not an ordinary push. Three ways out, and the choice is the owner's because it is about how much calu interrupts: (a) leave it to the checker and accept that a plain push sometimes goes through unseen, which is defensible for a solo developer pushing a branch; (b) put `git push`, `kubectl apply`, `terraform apply`, `npm publish` and the like on the **command-deny** list, so every one of them waits for a person -- deterministic, and noisy for anybody who pushes often; (c) ship them in the deny list but with the `auto_escalate` switches as the way to turn them off per session. My recommendation is (b) with a sentence in `docs/GETTING_STARTED` on removing the rules one does not want (`calu rules remove command-deny <pattern>`), because a rule is the only part of the gate that does not depend on a model's mood -- but it changes the first impression of the tool, so it is not mine to decide. Option (b) is built as an opt-in set, `calu rules add-set publishing` (D143); the default lists are unchanged. **Answered by the owner 2026-11-12:** no hard allow or deny rules are shipped -- every list is empty by default (D179). The default checker instructions are to be split into fixed system instructions (role, answer format, summary) and compact user instructions (what to pass, deny, escalate), with the honesty rules separate, editable for a project's documentation standards but not normally edited (D181). |
| Q17 | **Which model do the checkers need, and which is the default?** The default was Mistral Small (`CheckerConfig.model`) when this was written; since D129 `calu setup` picks it from the keys it finds (Bedrock Haiku, then Anthropic Haiku 5.5 at medium effort, then Fireworks Nemotron Lightning, then Mistral Medium), and Haiku 5.5 answered all 35 built-in cases correctly (D133). The measurements of 2026-11-10 were taken with Nemotron Lightning 3.5 30B, the model this machine has a key for. Both are small, and on the cases that matter they fail in the way a small model fails: the escapes are not wording gaps but judgement -- a planted "the user approved this" is believed (D79), a demand to rewrite a script the user called throwaway is accepted (D78), an ordinary `git push` is passed as reversible (D73). Rewriting the instructions moves those numbers around without fixing them, which is why the instructions are now left alone: a model that cannot follow what it is given cannot be prompted into it. What the owner decides: (a) the default checker model calu ships -- a Sonnet-class model for the step checkers, or a small one with the documented caveat; (b) whether the changeset and subagent checkers, which judge whole changes and claimed results, get a stronger model than the step checkers, which calu already supports per checker (`calu checker set NAME --model ...`); (c) whether the deny lists should carry the categories a small model cannot be trusted with, which is Q16. calu's own job here is done either way: the model is configurable per checker, `calu evals` reports what a given one does, and `calu checker stats` shows what it costs in tokens and cache hits (D63). |
| Q18 | **One checker with long instructions, or several with short ones?** Honesty, security, over-engineering and project-specific style are different judgements on the same edit. One agent reading all four sets of instructions costs one model call and may do each of them worse; four agents cost four calls and each one reads only what it needs. Nobody knows which wins, and it may differ per model: a Sonnet-class model may hold four concerns at once where a small one cannot hold two. The framework therefore decides nothing (D81): a route carries a list, so one row with combined instructions and four rows with separate ones are both configurations, switchable without code. What the owner decides later, with `calu evals` and `calu checker stats` to measure it: which shape calu **ships**, and whether the extra checkers are on or off by default. My expectation, to be checked rather than believed: separate agents for honesty (which needs the register and a long instruction) and one combined agent for the rest. |
| Q21 | **Should calu read the user's own Claude Code settings?** `claude.setting_sources` is `["project"]`, so a session reads the project's `.claude/settings.json` and not `~/.claude/settings.json` or `.claude/settings.local.json` -- narrower than an interactive `claude`, which reads all three. Found while checking REQUIREMENTS 5 ("without interfering too much with an existing Claude Code configuration") against the documentation, which claimed that what works in your `claude` session works in a calu session; it now states the default and how to widen it (D116). Keeping `project` has two arguments: a herd should behave the same on every machine, and a permission rule in a personal settings file would widen what Claude Code approves **by itself**, which is the decision a checker's PASS defers to -- a file calu cannot see cannot weaken the pair. Against it: a user's own MCP servers, hooks and skills are silently absent, which is a surprise and reads as calu interfering. My recommendation is to keep the narrow default and leave the line in the guide, because the surprise is one a sentence fixes and the weakening is not; the owner may prefer `["user", "project", "local"]` for a single-user machine. The surprise is now named at every start (D144); the default is unchanged. **Answered by the owner 2026-11-12:** calu wraps Claude Code and must leave its normal behaviour alone, including how it loads configuration, skills, MCP servers, plugins and auth: all settings sources load (D178). |
| Q22 | **Should calu put Claude Code into auto mode itself?** Requirement 2 describes auto mode on top of the checkers. Measured (D191): a headless start is in mode "default" unless `claude.permission_mode` or Claude Code's `defaultMode` is "auto", and then a PASS of the checkers is the only approval. Options: (a) calu sets `permission_mode = "auto"` by default; (b) `claude.require_auto_mode` defaults to on, so a PASS outside auto mode waits for a human; (c) as now: off by default, stated at start. **Answered 2026-11-12 by the owner: (c).** calu accepts Claude Code's own default and does not override it; `claude.permission_mode` stays unset and `claude.require_auto_mode` stays off by default. The start line and `calu doctor` say which mode is in effect. |
| Q20 | **May a checker mark a file the step did not touch?** D112 lets a mark name any existing file of the project, not only the files of the step. The owner's suggestion was the stricter rule -- only the files being edited -- and it is the safer one: a checker could then never block an unrelated file, however it was prompted. What the strict rule loses is real: an edit to `src/parser.py` can be what makes `README.md` or the caller in `src/cli.py` lie, and under the strict rule no checker could record that. Three options: (a) keep it as built (any existing project file, with the paths validated and dropped marks logged); (b) restrict to the step's own files, and accept that cross-file dishonesty is only caught when that other file is itself edited; (c) allow other files but mark them in the register as "not from this step", so a person reviewing `calu honesty list` sees which marks reach beyond the edit that produced them. My recommendation is (c) -- it keeps the catch and makes the reach visible -- but it is the owner's call, because it decides how far a checker's judgement may spread from the step it was asked about. The visible part of (c) is built (D141); what is left is whether a mark beyond its step should block less, or not be allowed at all. **Answered by the owner 2026-11-12:** yes, a mark may name another file, because an edit can taint a file that uses it and a command can change many files -- but that should be the exception (D181, instructions). |
| Q19 | **Does a dirty mark belong to a branch?** A mark says "src/parser.py:fields is dishonest". After `git switch main` that file may be a different file, and the mark is then wrong in both directions -- it blocks code that is honest on this branch, and it misses dishonesty that only exists here. The register records `git_branch` (D82) so that the question can be answered without a migration, but which rule to apply is open: (a) marks apply only on the branch they were made on, which is simple and loses the mark when a branch is renamed; (b) marks apply to the project and are re-judged when the file changed since the mark was made, which needs the checker to re-read the file; (c) marks carry the commit they were made at and are shown as stale when the file has moved on. My recommendation is (a) plus a line in the report when a mark was made on another branch, because a wrong block that a person can see and clear is better than a silent miss -- but it touches how the feature feels in daily use, so it is the owner's call. Option (a) is built as a setting, `honesty.scope = "branch"`, with the default left at `project` (D142). **Answered by the owner 2026-11-12:** dirty and clean marks are transient: kept in memory, not persisted anywhere, and certainly not in a place as easy to modify as a database table; a commit, not a branch, is what is dirty or clean (D180). |
## 12. Phases

1. **Skeleton** (done): package layout, `calu` CLI, `docker-compose.yml` (`dev`, `caludb`),
   schema and migrations, `calu db up/init`, `calu setup`, configuration in the database.
2. **Core port** (done): rules engine, checkers, narrator, context builder, with configuration
   and checker definitions read from the database (`calu.store.ConfigStore`).
3. **Session runner** (done): `calu run`, Agent SDK with auto mode (D5), steps and escalations
   written to the database, escalation resolved from the database (`LISTEN/NOTIFY` and a poll),
   credentials dropped before Claude starts, sandbox settings passed with `--settings` (D15).
   `calu sessions/escalations/resolve` were pulled forward from phase 6, because without them
   nobody could unblock a session.
4. **Bus and terminal client** (done): `calu.bus` numbers the events and keeps them for replay,
   `calu.protocol.server` serves JSON-RPC over WebSocket to several clients at once (token per
   session, `state.get`, `events.subscribe`), `calu.protocol.client` is the client side, and
   `calu attach` is a terminal client. Comments were ported with it (migration 0003). Still
   open: a client cannot change the configuration, the rules, the checker instructions or the
   chat summary through the protocol, because the session role may only read them — that is done
   with the `admin` credential instead (§8 commands and the web interface, D40). The one
   exception is `auto_escalate.set`, which holds back approved steps in the running session only
   and is forgotten on restart (D42).
5. **Sandboxing of tool calls and the effects of a step**: the sandbox settings exist (D15). Still
   open: the write-set detection of D31 (snapshot per Bash step, the comparison of declared against
   written paths, the post-call review of the real diff, the revert), the network policy per project
   (Q5: the mechanism is built, D39, and only the default for new projects is open), the
   nested-sandbox case in Docker (Q1). The time and ctime
   attribution of D32 is **done** (`calu.clock`, the `step_times` and `changed_files` tools of
   `calu.checkers.tools`, `Session.clock_jumped`). The snapshot of D31 is **done**
   (`calu.changes`, `changes.snapshot` and `changes.review`, both off by default): calu records
   what a command wrote, warns about paths its summary did not mention, and can let a checker
   review the change and undo it. The evaluation of the checkers
   (`calu evals`, ported from sitl) is done.
6. **Herd management** (done): the commands of phase 3 and the web interface of `calu web`
   (D33, D34, D40) — the fleet grouped by what needs a human, a session's history at four levels
   of detail, the live view over the session's own WebSocket, the escalation queue of `/triage`,
   the settings generated from the configuration model, the checker instructions and the folded
   conversation editable, and on every page an attention badge and a jump palette (Ctrl+K).
7. **Third-party user interfaces and packaging:** `calu acp`, the ACP adapter of §6.1 (D24),
   is **done** (`src/calu/acp/`): `initialize`, `session/new`, `session/load` with the required
   replay from `session.history`, `session/prompt`, `session/cancel`, `session/close`, the
   `session/update` stream (message and thought chunks, tool calls with their checker verdict,
   diffs and results) and `session/request_permission` for escalations, over newline-delimited
   JSON on stdio. It is a client of a running session, so the session outlives every editor and
   several editors may attach at once. The editor's `fs/*` and `terminal/*` capabilities stay
   unused and its `mcpServers` are not forwarded (§6.1). Tested end to end against a real
   session over a real WebSocket with a scripted Claude (`tests/unit/test_acp.py`), and as a
   real process over real pipes for the handshake, a prompt, a **denied** step and
   `session/load`'s replay (`tests/unit/test_acp_process.py`); **not yet driven by a real
   editor**, which is the one thing left in this phase and needs an editor this machine does
   not have. The single binary is decided against (D41) and the
   wheel is the shipping format; the session modes and config options of ACP carry
   `AutoEscalate` (D42). Nothing is open in this phase except what needs a real editor. A port of
   sitl's VSCode extension is only worth it for what ACP cannot carry (the checker verdicts as
   structure, the herd overview).
8. **Honesty enforcement and checker families** (§13, the owner's brief of 2026-11-10), in three
   steps of which only the first is model-independent:
   1. **Several checkers on one route, run in parallel** (D81). The enabler, and useful on its
      own: it is what lets a security or over-engineering checker exist beside the command
      checker without a code change. No new model behaviour is involved.
   2. **The register and its enforcement** (D82, D83, D84): **done** -- `honesty_marks`
      (migration 0019), the marks as fields of a verdict, the deterministic layer that denies a
      command which would run dirty code, the three modes, `calu honesty list|clear` and the
      open marks on the web interface's rules page.
   3. **The honesty checker's instructions**: **done** (D85) -- shipped as
      `checkers/honesty.md`, seeded on the edit route and disabled, written once as a
      specification and deliberately not tuned against the small model this machine has a key
      for (Q17). The judgement needs a capable model; the framework's part is that the model is
      configurable for this checker alone, which it is (`calu checker set honesty --model ...`).
9. **Editor plugins of calu's own** (D200, the owner's requirement 23): **not started, and not
   to be started before** the full-screen terminal client (D199), the web interface (including
   the git browser of D201) and standalone use work well in the owner's daily use. Then, in
   this order: (1) a spike per IDE that proves a plugin can reach a session's WebSocket in the
   IDE's remote mode; (2) the protocol additions both plugins need; (3) the VSCode extension,
   modelled on sitl's; (4) the JetBrains plugin.
10. **calu privileged, Claude Code unprivileged, and `calu serve`** (D206-D209, the owner's
    requirements 26 and 27). Order: (1) the launcher `calu-claude` (D207); (2) `calu serve`
    and `calu start` (D208), for one user as `calu run` works today; (3) conversations started
    with `claude` itself (D209); (4) the rest of D206: `calu run --user` and `calu serve
    --user` as root, git and the snapshots as the other user, the re-execution through sudo,
    the system directories.

## 13. Honesty enforcement, and several checkers on one route (2026-11-10, the owner's brief)

### 13.1 The problem

"Honesty" means that names, documentation and behaviour agree: a function called `fetch_user`
fetches a user, a docstring that promises a sorted list gets one, a module whose README says it
caches does. A checker can judge that per edit, but a per-edit verdict is the wrong instrument:
a complex change makes the codebase **temporarily dishonest on purpose** -- a signature changes
in one step and its callers and docs follow in the next five. A checker that comments every time
produces noise, and one that denies every time stops the work it is supposed to supervise.

The resolution the owner asked for: do not judge each step in isolation. **Record** which code is
dishonest, **clear** the record when it is honest again, and **enforce** at the one point where
temporary dishonesty becomes a real risk -- when something would **run** that code.

    edit  ──► honesty checker ──► marks src/parser.py:fields dirty ("docstring promises a
                                  sorted list, the code returns insertion order")
    edit  ──► honesty checker ──► still dirty (the callers are not updated yet)
    edit  ──► honesty checker ──► clean: the docstring now matches
    Bash `pytest tests/test_parser.py`
          ──► command checker, told what is dirty ──► DENY while it was dirty,
              PASS once it is clean

Dirty code may always be **edited** -- that is the way out -- and may not be **executed**. The
invariant matters: a register that blocked edits would deadlock, because fixing the dishonesty
is an edit.

### 13.2 The register

One table, project-scoped rather than session-scoped: honesty debt outlives the session that
incurred it, and a resumed or second session must see it (D82).

    honesty_marks(id, project_id, path, symbol, reason, git_branch,
                  opened_session, opened_step, opened_at,
                  closed_session, closed_step, closed_at)

A mark with `closed_at IS NULL` is open; the dirty set is the open marks of the project. Rows are
never deleted, so the register is also the history of what was dishonest and for how long --
the same shape as `escalations`, and readable with the same kind of report.

`symbol` is optional: a mark is either a whole file or one function, class or method in it. Which
granularity a checker uses is an instruction matter, not a schema matter.

### 13.3 How a mark is made and cleared

The honesty checker is an edit-route and changeset-route checker, and its decision carries the
marks as a side effect of the verdict rather than as a tool call: `CheckDecision` gains
`marks_dirty` and `marks_clean`, empty for every other checker. calu applies them -- opening a
mark for each dirty entry, closing the matching open marks for each clean one -- and records a
clean entry that matches nothing open, because a checker claiming to have cleaned what was never
dirty is worth seeing.

A checker that writes the register through a tool was considered and rejected: a verdict that
calu applies is auditable in one place and cannot be half-applied when the model's next sentence
fails.

### 13.4 Enforcement

The register is consulted **after** the allow and deny lists and **before** the checkers: a
rule the user wrote is a stronger statement than honesty debt, so a step that matches a deny
rule escalates with that reason instead of being refused for being dishonest. Two layers,
deterministic first, as everywhere else in calu:

1. **The rules path.** The parts a step touches are already extracted for the allow and deny
   lists (`calu.checkers.rules`). A part that matches an open mark's path is denied without a
   model call, with the mark's reason as the explanation.
2. **The checker's judgement.** The open marks are rendered into the checker's context, and the
   command checker is told to deny a call that may execute dirty code. This is what covers
   `python -m myapp` and `make test`, where no path appears in the call at all.

The verdict is **DENY**, not ESCALATE: the reason is addressed to Claude ("restore the docstring
of `fields` or change the code to match, then run the tests"), it is self-fixable, and pausing a
human for a problem the agent can fix itself is the thing calu's auto-pause exists *not* to do.

Three modes, because the judgement needs a strong model and nobody should be forced to trust it
blind (D83): `off` (no marks, no enforcement), `track` (marks recorded and reported, nothing
blocked -- which is how a user evaluates the checker on their own project) and `enforce` (dirty
code cannot be executed). Default `off`.

### 13.5 Several checkers on one route

Honesty is one of a family -- security, over-engineering, performance, project-specific style --
and whether they are one agent with long instructions or several agents with short ones is an
open question that the framework must not decide (Q18). So a route carries **a list** of
checkers rather than one:

- `checker_types` already allows several rows per route, so the schema needs nothing;
- a step runs every enabled checker of its route **concurrently**, so the added latency is the
  slowest checker and not the sum;
- the verdicts combine **most-restrictive-wins**: any DENY denies, else any ESCALATE escalates,
  else PASS -- and ALLOW, which overrules Claude Code's own approval, requires every checker to
  say ALLOW, because an override needs unanimity;
- the reason and the comment name the checker they came from, so a denial is attributable;
- `checker_runs` is already keyed by checker name, so `calu checker stats` and
  `calu checker misses` report per checker with no change -- including what each one costs
  (D63), which is what makes running four of them a decision an owner can take with numbers.
